Source check: every source link was checked on 2026-11-11. v0.3: automatic HTTP check of every source URL on 11 Nov 2026 (follows redirects); v0.4: the papers, official pages and textbooks added on 12 Nov 2026 were checked the same way (each DOI resolved in Crossref); DOI links also checked in the DOI registry; years from Crossref, arXiv ids, the URL, the title, or read from the page during this build (year_note says how). Links that failed were rechecked the same day: read from a second network or confirmed in the Web Archive (archived_copy). For living pages (Wikipedia, MacTutor, Stanford Encyclopedia) year is the last-revised year stated on the page or its history, and year_note says so. Results: 13 ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive); 642 ok; 50 ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks); 53 ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks); 1 ok (rechecked: page read from a second network); 30 site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive; 1 site down on check day (HTTP 503); page confirmed in Web Archive; 1 site unreachable on check day (HTTP 503); recheck; 1 site unreachable on check day; page confirmed in Web Archive; 1 ok (rechecked with a plain client: HTTP 200).

Automatic checks vs science review

Automatic checks: Structural checks run by a script on every build (src/audit.py, report in AUDIT-v0.9.md): every card has a colour and at least one source, links point at cards that exist, share links are exact, the data hash is the same in every file, counts match, and every source URL was fetched to see that it answers. They do not read the science. Passing them does not mean a claim is right, only that it is labelled, sourced and linked.

Science review: No formal peer review yet. A science review means a person with the right expertise reads each claim against its sources and decides whether it is right, up to date and fairly coloured. The automatic audit is not a peer review.

Spotted a mistake? Every card on the site has a Suggest a fix button, or e-mail zyphullen@gmail.com with the subject “Echo Atlas fix: card name (card id)”.

Before the Big Bang?

unknownWhy is there anything? · size No size: maybe no space yet

Nobody knows yet.

Maybe there was no "before".

What we can say

Our physics works back to a tiny fraction of a second after the start.

Before that, we have only ideas.

Ideas people test

Inflation that runs forever, a bounce from an older universe, or time itself starting.

Open questions

Why these laws? Why these numbers?

Chain Theory says only pairs of qudits exist. It does not say where they came from.

How we would know

Gravity waves from the first instant (B-modes): best limit r < 0.034. The big US CMB-S4 was cancelled (2025); Simons Observatory and LiteBIRD (~2036) carry on.

redOther idea: Eternal inflation (multiverse): Inflation never stops everywhere; our Big Bang is one bubble of many.

Sources: Guth 2007: Eternal inflation and its implications (preprint · 2007 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

redOther idea: Bouncing universe: A shrinking universe before ours bounced back; no start from nothing.

Sources: Ijjas & Steinhardt 2018: Bouncing cosmology made simple (preprint · 2018 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

redOther idea: Conformal cyclic cosmology: Penrose: endless "aeons". Claimed CMB circles (2010) were not confirmed by others.

Sources: Gurzadyan & Penrose 2010: concentric circles in WMAP data (CCC) (preprint · 2010 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

redOther idea: Hartle–Hawking no-boundary: Time becomes space near the start, so "before" has no meaning.

Sources: Hartle & Hawking 1983: Wave function of the Universe (no-boundary) (paper · 1983 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Chain Theory

we thinkChain Theory · Idea: only entangled pairs exist · size Below all lengths (space is built later)

A new idea by Alex Wissner-Gross (Oct 2026).

Reality = networks of linked tiny quantum units.

Brand new. Not yet tested by others.

Alex says

"Nothing exists but finitely many maximally entangled pairs of qudits, some qudits shared, known only by distilling them."

That one sentence is the only input. Alex aims for "no free parameters".

Particles would be "chainmail": finite meshes of these links. "A chain, not a string."

Our reading

It is like a building made only of linked rings. Everything else is how the rings join.

Status (Alex's own grading)

A compendium of 100 theorems, 109 conditional theorems and 198 open results (release 0.1).

"Theorem" here often means a check over a finite list of networks, not a proof for all.

That its pieces ARE real physics is called a conjecture by Alex himself.

What is missing

Peer review. Independent checks. A second number that matches a measurement.

Posted 23–24 Oct 2026. No independent expert review found yet (checked 6 Nov 2026).

Disclosure (from the paper)

Made with a "human-superintelligence collaboration". The author has a financial interest in Physical Superintelligence PBC.

Comes from: Before the Big Bang?

Test: Next caesium (Berkeley) and rubidium (Paris) α results: one of them rules Alex's number in or out.

Sources: Berkeley caesium α group: new systematics study (MITP talk, Oct 2025) (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: Keep "3 generations, no 4th" (LEP: 2.9963 ± 0.0074 neutrino types, consistent).

Sources: LEP: number of light neutrino types (arXiv 1912.02067) (preprint · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test (Chain Theory): Independent experts review release 0.1 (none found, checked 6 Nov 2026).

Sources: Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test (Chain Theory): Get a number not put in by hand, e.g. proton/electron mass 1836.15 (not reached yet).

Sources: Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sources: Alex Wissner-Gross: "Chain Theory" (Substack, 24 Oct 2026) (blog · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · @alexwg on X: Chain Theory article (social post · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Preprint: A Parameter-Free Candidate for the Fine-Structure Constant (23 Oct 2026) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Slides: Chain Theory, An Introduction (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Quantum fields

knownStandard Model: tested · size Fills all of space

Every particle is a ripple in a field.

The Standard Model: 17 particles, 3 forces.

Tested extremely precisely.

Recipe

12 matter fields (6 quarks, 6 leptons) + force fields (gluons, photon, W, Z) + the Higgs field.

How it works

A field is spread over all space. A ripple of fixed size is a particle.

The rules come from symmetry: SU(3)×SU(2)×U(1).

How we know

Its predictions match experiments, e.g. the electron's magnetism to about 1 part in a trillion.

The numbers

About 19–26 constants must be measured (masses, strengths). The theory does not explain them.

Open questions

No gravity. No dark matter. Why 3 generations? Why these masses?

Comes from: Before the Big Bang?

Sources: Particle Data Group: Review of Particle Physics (review · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Quantum gravity

unknownGravity at tiny scales? · size Planck length ~1.6×10⁻³⁵ m

Joining gravity with quantum rules.

Ideas: strings, loops, chains…

None proven. No leading winner.

Why it matters

At the Planck scale (~10⁻³⁵ m) both gravity and quantum effects are strong.

Our two best theories then disagree.

Candidates

String theory, loop quantum gravity, causal sets, "spacetime from entanglement", Chain Theory.

Why red

No idea is tested, and none leads by evidence.

How we would know

Lab tests of "gravity-made entanglement" (tiny diamonds, atoms) are being designed; none done yet. LISA (2035) will listen to black holes merging.

Chain Theory

Its gravity results are all graded "open" (Open result 1.9).

Comes from: Before the Big Bang?

redOther idea: String / M-theory: Particles are tiny strings; needs extra dimensions. No test yet.

Sources: Polchinski 1994: What is string theory? (lectures) (preprint · 1994 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

redOther idea: Loop quantum gravity: Space comes in chunks woven from loops. No test yet.

Sources: Rovelli 2008: Loop quantum gravity (Living Reviews) (paper · 2008 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

redOther idea: Causal sets: Spacetime is a scatter of ordered points ("before/after" only).

Sources: Surya 2019: The causal set approach to quantum gravity (Living Reviews) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

redOther idea: Asymptotic safety: Plain gravity stays finite at tiny sizes with no new parts.

Sources: Niedermaier & Reuter 2006: asymptotic safety (Living Reviews) (paper · 2006 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

orangeOther idea: Holography / ER=EPR: Space and gravity come from entanglement; wormholes = entangled pairs. Well supported in theory, unproven for our universe. Close cousin of Chain Theory's "links build space".

Sources: Maldacena & Susskind 2013: Cool horizons for entangled black holes (ER=EPR) (preprint · 2013 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: Lab test: can gravity entangle two tiny masses? (nanodiamond set-ups being designed)

Sources: Nanodiamond interferometers for gravity-made entanglement (arXiv 2405.21029) (preprint · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: LISA (2035): hear black holes merge and test gravity at its strongest.

Qudits & pairs

we thinkChain Theory · The links of the chain · size No size: comes before space

A qudit is a tiny quantum unit with d states.

Two qudits share one perfect entangled state.

That pair is one "link".

Alex says

A qudit has dimension 2 or more (2 = qubit, 3 = qutrit).

A pair is "maximally entangled": as linked as quantum rules allow.

A shared qudit is the end of 2+ pairs. It carries one copy per pair (because entanglement is "monogamous").

Recipe

2 qudits → 1 link. Links join at shared qudits → a network.

Why pairs must be perfect

A less-than-perfect pair would need smooth, continuous numbers. A fully discrete theory cannot have them.

Known physics behind it

Monogamy of entanglement and tensor networks (PEPS) are standard quantum information.

Open

That reality is made ONLY of these pairs is the untested part.

Comes from: Chain Theory

Test: Entanglement limits ("monogamy", bound entanglement) already hold in theory and lab: consistent, not proof.

Sources: Horodecki ×3: bound entanglement (1998) (preprint · 1998 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test (Chain Theory): Show one measured particle is a web of pair-links.

Sources: Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: α test (Cs vs Rb) decides the whole base.

Sources: Berkeley caesium α group: new systematics study (MITP talk, Oct 2025) (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sources: Slides: Chain Theory, An Introduction (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Preprint: A Parameter-Free Candidate for the Fine-Structure Constant (23 Oct 2026) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Distillation

we thinkChain Theory · "To know is to link" · size No size

To look at a link, you distill it.

Distilling 2 qudits makes them a new pair.

Time = a count of these acts.

Alex says

To distill two qudits is to project them onto a perfect pair. One copy, no failure.

A "read" is a distillation at one end of a pair.

What is "known" is only what reads can fix with certainty.

Like

Entanglement swapping: link 1–2 and 3–4, distill 2 with 3, and now 1–4 is a pair.

Time

"Time is counted, not assumed." Graded a conditional theorem (box 50.1).

Born rule

The usual quantum probability rule comes out as an output (Alex grades it conditional).

Known physics behind it

Entanglement swapping is a real lab effect (since 1998).

Comes from: Chain Theory

Test (Chain Theory): Turn "time = count" from conditional (box 50.1) into a theorem.

Sources: Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test (Chain Theory): Predict a measurable effect of time-from-links (e.g. a smallest tick).

Sources: Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sources: Slides: Chain Theory, An Introduction (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Inflation

we thinkSuper-fast first stretch · size Model-dependent: far smaller than a proton → roughly cm–m

Space grew hugely in a split second.

Fits what we see in the sky.

Leading model, not proven.

How it works (model)

A field made space grow by a factor e (~2.7) again and again: at least ~60 times ("e-folds"), ~10²⁶ in all.

In common models it ended by ~10⁻³² s, then dumped its energy as hot particles.

Why we think so

Space is flat, the sky is the same everywhere, and the seed ripples have the right pattern.

Open

What field? Was it really inflation? No "B-mode" gravity-wave sign yet: r < 0.034.

Newest data (with DESI) lean away from the simplest (Starobinsky) model. Not settled.

Rivals: bouncing universes, other early phases.

Next

Simons Observatory (running now), LiteBIRD (launch ~2036).

Comes from: Quantum fields, Quantum gravity

Test: Simons Observatory B-modes (running, to 2035).

Sources: Simons Foundation: Simons Observatory now fully online (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: LiteBIRD (~2036): reach r ~ 0.001.

Sources: JAXA: LiteBIRD mission (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: Sharper n_s from new CMB + DESI data (2025: pressure on simplest models).

Sources: 2025 CMB + DESI inflation constraints (arXiv 2512.10613) (preprint · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sources: Planck 2018 results X: constraints on inflation (preprint · 2018 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · BICEP/Keck + others: r < 0.034 (preprint · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · 2025 CMB + DESI inflation constraints (arXiv 2512.10613) (preprint · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Simons Foundation: Simons Observatory now fully online (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · JAXA: LiteBIRD mission (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Six kinds of link

we thinkChain Theory · Only 2 can be read · size No size

Links are typed by end sizes: (2,2), (3,2)…

Only (2,2) and (3,2) can be "read".

The other four are "dark".

Alex says

Ends have dimension 2, 3 or 4 ("Conjecture 0", a conditional theorem).

That gives 6 genres: (2,2) (3,2) (4,2) (3,3) (4,3) (4,4).

A link is readable when d₁×d₂ ≤ 6 (math of Størmer, Woronowicz, Choi).

Alex's conjectured jobs

(2,2) spacetime · (3,2) photon / electromagnetism · (4,2) weak force · (3,3) strong force · (4,3) Yukawa (mass) · (4,4) Higgs.

Status

The 6 genres and the read cap are derived. Which physics each one IS: conjectured, some matched only "by name" (Alex’s words).

Known maths behind it

Bound entanglement (Horodecki 1998) first appears when d₁×d₂ > 6. That part is solid.

Comes from: Qudits & pairs, Distillation

Test (Chain Theory): Give each "dark" genre a job and a number to measure.

Sources: Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test (Chain Theory): Search for Alex's two conserved "dark" charges (Open 120.2).

Sources: Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sources: Slides: Chain Theory, An Introduction (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Horodecki ×3: bound entanglement (1998) (preprint · 1998 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Forces from one link

we thinkChain Theory · Qutrit + qubit symmetry · size No size

A qutrit linked to a qubit has a symmetry.

It is the same shape as the Standard Model's.

A striking match. Meaning not yet proven.

Alex says

Turning the qutrit end gives SU(3). Turning the qubit end gives SU(2). One shared phase gives U(1).

Together: (SU(3)×SU(2)×U(1))/ℤ₆, the exact group of the Standard Model.

Its charge is the hypercharge with Y = 1/6, like the left-handed quark doublet.

Known maths

The group S(U(3)×U(2)) ≅ (SU(3)×SU(2)×U(1))/ℤ₆ is known (Baez & Huerta 2010).

Alex's new step is reading it as the symmetry of ONE entangled pair.

Open

Why this pair and not others? How hypercharge becomes electric charge is "beyond the Letter".

Comes from: Six kinds of link

Test: Already matches the known Standard Model group (known maths, not new evidence).

Sources: Baez & Huerta: The Algebra of Grand Unified Theories (2010) (preprint · 2009 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: LHC Run 3: any extra force would break it (none seen).

Sources: Particle Data Group: Review of Particle Physics (review · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test (Chain Theory): Predict a new particle or coupling from the link and look for it.

Sources: Chain Theory compendium, release 0.1 (working draft) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sources: Preprint: A Parameter-Free Candidate for the Fine-Structure Constant (23 Oct 2026) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Baez & Huerta: The Algebra of Grand Unified Theories (2010) (preprint · 2009 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Chain Theory’s α formula

we thinkChain Theory · α is measured; this formula is new · size A pure number (no units)

α sets how strongly light and charge interact.

Alex gets 1/137.035999190 with no tuning.

Measured: 1/137.035999177 (±21 in last digits).

Recipe (Alex says)

1 shared qutrit with 3 links to qubits, 2 of them read.

Count 172 "records": 79 + 48 + 42 + 3.

Weigh them 1, √2, √3, √6. Divide by 31104 = 24×36×36.

α = (79 + 48√2 + 42√3 + 3√6) / 31104.

Check

We re-did the sum: 1/α = 137.0359991896.

Graded

The number is proven from his rules, checked in Lean 4.

That it IS α is the "Photon Conjecture". It is not in the Lean proof.

Versus experiments

0.6σ from CODATA 2022. But +1.6σ (electron g−2, 2023), −1.5σ (rubidium, 2020), +5.3σ (caesium, 2018). The experiments disagree with each other.

Caution

One blogger estimated ~140 formulas of this style land as close (rough, not peer-reviewed). No second matching number yet.

Test

New, ~10× sharper measurements (Northwestern, Berkeley, Paris) will confirm or kill it.

Who wins?

His number sides with rubidium, against caesium. If caesium is right, it fails.

Comes from: Forces from one link

Test: Berkeley caesium α remeasurement (systematics study 2025; result pending).

Sources: Berkeley caesium α group: new systematics study (MITP talk, Oct 2025) (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: Next rubidium α (LKB Paris).

Sources: Morel et al., Nature 2020: α from rubidium (paper · 2020 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: New electron g−2 (2023 value: 1.6σ from Alex). Goal: ~10× sharper.

Sources: APS Physics 2023: most precise electron magnetic moment (g−2) (news / announcement · 2023 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-04-10) · archived copy)

Test: If all agree at ~10× precision and miss 137.035999189566: dead.

Sources: Preprint: A Parameter-Free Candidate for the Fine-Structure Constant (23 Oct 2026) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Seed ripples

we thinkTiny bumps, 1 in 100,000 · size Quantum-small, stretched to cosmic size

Tiny quantum jitters were stretched huge.

They became 1-in-100,000 bumps in density.

Every galaxy grew from one.

Recipe

Quantum jitter + inflation's stretch → almost-even ripples on every scale.

Randomness

The exact bump pattern is random. The overall statistics are predicted.

How we know

The bumps are seen directly in the afterglow (CMB). Their tilt (n_s ≈ 0.965 Planck; ~0.97 with newer data) fits inflation well.

Open

That they came from inflation's quantum jitter is the leading idea, not proof.

Comes from: Inflation

Test: Simons Observatory: sharper ripple tilt (n_s).

Sources: Simons Foundation: Simons Observatory now fully online (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: LiteBIRD (~2036): gravity-wave seeds (B-modes).

Sources: JAXA: LiteBIRD mission (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: Euclid maps (DR1 expected 2027): tiny "non-Gaussian" fingerprints.

Sources: ESA Euclid: data release timeline (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 503); page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-10-26 · archived copy)

Hot Big Bang

knownHot, dense, expanding · size Today's visible universe was once tiny

13.8 billion years ago.

Hot, dense, and expanding ever since.

Seen in the afterglow light.

What it is

Not an explosion in space. Space itself stretching, everywhere.

Timeline

10⁻¹² s: forces split. 10⁻⁵ s: protons form. 3 min: first nuclei. 380,000 yr: atoms.

How we know

Galaxies move apart (Hubble). The afterglow (CMB). The right amounts of H, He, Li.

The numbers

Age 13.787 ± 0.020 billion years (Planck 2018).

Open

The "Hubble tension": two ways of measuring the expansion speed disagree (~67 vs ~73 km/s/Mpc).

Comes from: Inflation

Sources: Planck 2018 results VI: cosmological parameters (preprint · 2018 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Higgs field

knownGives tiny particles mass · size Boson ~125 GeV, lives 10⁻²² s

Its particle was found in 2012.

It gives quarks, electrons, W and Z their mass.

NOT most of YOUR mass (see protons).

How it gives mass

Particles get mass by coupling to the field's steady value (246 GeV) everywhere in space.

The numbers

Higgs boson: 125.2 GeV (PDG 2024). Found by ATLAS and CMS at CERN, 4 July 2012.

Important

Only ~1% of a proton's mass comes from Higgs-given quark masses. ~99% is strong-force energy.

Open

Why these couplings? Is the vacuum stable? Do neutrinos get mass this way? Unknown.

Chain Theory

Alex conjectures the Higgs is a "closed hub read" in (4,4) links (conditional).

Comes from: Quantum fields

Sources: CERN: the Higgs boson (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · PDG 2024 Higgs listing: mass 125.20 ± 0.11 GeV (review · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Chainmail particles

we thinkChain Theory · Particles as webs of links · size Looks point-like: <10⁻¹⁸ m

Each particle = a small web of links.

Its insides can't be pulled apart by collisions.

So it looks like a point. Alex's idea.

Alex says

Particles have no visible parts because their parts are "undistillable", not too small.

Example, the electron web: 9 sites and 10 links. A core triangle of three (3,3) links plus one "hub assembly" (links of types (4,2), (3,2), (4,3), (2,2)).

Muon and tau add more hubs to the same core (the tau is "the third hub on the triangle core"). Alex reads this as 3 generations (conditional 152.1).

Recipe

Links + shared qudits → closed webs.

Graded

17 particles drawn on 16 webs: 1 on a theorem, 11 conditional, 2 open, 3 not yet in the release.

"That a web is the named particle is a conjecture."

Our reading

A neat idea: like knots in chainmail. No mass or charge of a real particle has been derived from a web yet (Thm 176.1).

Prediction: no 4th generation. Fits LEP's count of 3 light neutrinos.

Comes from: Qudits & pairs, Six kinds of link

Test: No 4th generation: LEP 2.9963 ± 0.0074 neutrino types (consistent).

Sources: LEP: number of light neutrino types (arXiv 1912.02067) (preprint · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: LHC keeps searching for 4th-generation quarks (none).

Sources: Particle Data Group: Review of Particle Physics (review · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Space & time from links?

we thinkChain Theory · (2,2) links → spacetime · size Builds the lengths themselves

Alex: space and time are not given.

They come from counting links and acts.

Conditional results. Not proven.

Alex says

The Lorentz group (special relativity) appears as the symmetries of a "Hermitian split" (conditional, box 54.1).

Spacetime has 1 time + 3 space directions: "one against three" (a theorem of the networks).

Gravity is "a statement about counting": a metric from the depth of a web, curvature as a graph Laplacian (conditional).

A cosmological term (dark energy?) at the "depth exponent" (conditional).

Known physics behind it

"Spacetime from entanglement" (Van Raamsdonk 2010) and gravity from thermodynamics (Jacobson 1995) are real research lines.

Open

No measured gravity number (like G) is derived yet. Much of Part VI (metric, worldline, horizon) is conditional or open.

Alex grades "spacetime from (2,2) links" as an open conjecture, with all four gravity results open (Open result 1.9).

orangeOther idea: Holography / ER=EPR: Well supported in theory, unproven. The mainstream version of "entanglement builds space" (Van Raamsdonk 2010; Maldacena & Susskind 2013).

What would fill it

Hot particle soup

knownAll particles, ~10⁻¹² s · size The whole universe was hotter than 10¹⁵ K

Right after the Big Bang: pure energy.

Energy turned into particle pairs (E = mc²).

~10⁻¹² s: the Higgs switches on; forces split.

Recipe

Heat energy → pairs of particles + antiparticles, made and destroyed all the time.

At ~10⁻¹² s (~10¹⁵ K) the Higgs field takes its value: the weak and electric forces split, W, Z, quarks and electrons get mass.

Randomness

Which particles pop out where is quantum chance.

How we know

The LHC recreates these energies (13.6 TeV ≈ 10¹³ eV per collision).

Comes from: Hot Big Bang, Quantum fields

Sources: Particle Data Group: Review of Particle Physics (review · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · CERN: the Higgs boson (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Quarks

known6 flavours, 3 colours · size Point-like: <10⁻¹⁸ m

Up, down, strange, charm, bottom, top.

Never seen alone: always stuck together.

Protons and neutrons are made of them.

Family

3 generations × 2 = 6 flavours. Each has 3 "colour" charges.

Up: +⅔ charge, ~2.2 MeV. Down: −⅓, ~4.7 MeV. Top: 173 GeV (heaviest).

Recipe

Mass from the Higgs field. Held by gluons.

How we know

Electron-scattering showed hard points inside protons (SLAC, 1968). Top quark found 1995.

Chain Theory

Alex: quarks as "quark bodies" on star-shaped webs (conditional).

Open

Why 3 generations? Why such different masses?

Comes from: Hot particle soup, Higgs field

Sources: Particle Data Group: Review of Particle Physics (review · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Gluons

knownCarry the strong force · size Massless, but trapped within ~10⁻¹⁵ m

8 kinds of gluon.

They glue quarks together.

They stick to each other too.

Recipe

Strong force field (SU(3)) → 8 gluons. No mass.

Why it matters

Gluon energy makes up most of a proton's mass.

How we know

"Three-jet" collisions at DESY (1979).

Chain Theory

Alex: the strong coupling as a "gluon closed read" in (3,3) links (conditional).

Electrons & leptons

knowne, μ, τ + 3 neutrinos · size Point-like: <10⁻¹⁸ m

Electron found in 1897.

Heavier copies: muon and tau.

Electrons make atoms and electricity.

Family

6 leptons: electron, muon, tau, and a neutrino for each.

Electron: charge −1, mass 0.511 MeV (1/1836 of a proton).

Recipe

Mass from the Higgs field. No colour, so no strong force.

Chain Theory

Open

Why is the muon 207× heavier than the electron? Unknown.

Neutrinos

knownGhosts that pass through · size Mass < 0.5 eV (tiny)

~100 trillion pass through you each second.

They almost never hit anything.

They have tiny masses: why, unknown.

How many

~65 billion per cm² per second from the Sun alone, so ~10¹⁴ through a body each second.

Family

3 types: electron, muon, tau. They change type as they fly ("oscillation", Nobel 2015).

Open

Their masses are not given by the Standard Model. Are they their own antiparticle? Unknown.

The numbers

Sky (DESI 2025): all three masses add up to < 0.064 eV. Lab (KATRIN 2025): each < 0.45 eV.

Comes from: Hot particle soup

Photons

knownParticles of light · size Massless. Wavelength anything

Light, radio, X-rays: all photons.

They carry the electric force.

Massless: always at light speed.

Recipe

Electroweak field + Higgs breaking → 1 massless photon + W and Z.

W & Z bosons

knownHeavy weak-force carriers · size Range ~10⁻¹⁸ m

Found at CERN in 1983.

About 86–97× a proton's mass.

They make some atoms decay.

Recipe

Electroweak field + Higgs → W⁺, W⁻ (80.4 GeV), Z (91.2 GeV).

Heavy, so the weak force reaches only ~10⁻¹⁸ m.

Dark matter

we thinkUnseen mass. A particle? · size Unknown

~5× more than normal matter.

We see its pull, not the stuff.

What it is: still a guess.

The numbers

Dark matter ~26.5% of everything, normal matter ~4.9%, dark energy ~68.5% (Planck 2018).

Evidence (top-down)

Galaxy spin speeds, gravitational lensing, the CMB pattern, the Bullet Cluster.

Colour

The extra pull is very well measured. That it is a new particle is the leading idea, so orange.

Ideas

WIMPs, axions, sterile neutrinos, primordial black holes, modified gravity. None found.

Latest

LZ (Dec 2025): no WIMP, world-leading limits. ADMX (2025): no axion in its range.

Chain Theory

Alex: "dark matter as the dock-free sinks" (open result 120.3), plus new conserved dark charges (open).

Dark energy

unknownPushes space apart. Why? · size Matters only on cosmic scales

About 68% of the universe.

Makes expansion speed up (found 1998).

What it is: unknown.

The numbers

~68.5% of the energy of the universe (Planck 2018).

Simplest idea

A constant energy of empty space (Λ). But theory guesses a value ~10¹²⁰ too big.

New

DESI (2025) hints it may weaken over time (2.8–4.2σ, depending on the supernova set).

Others argue supernova errors or a data mismatch explain it.

Euclid (first big data release expected 2027) and Roman (launched 2026) will check.

Chain Theory

Alex writes a "cosmological term at the depth exponent" (conditional). Its size is not compared with observation.

Strong force

knownStrongest, range ~1 fm · size ~10⁻¹⁵ m

Holds quarks inside protons.

Leaks out to hold nuclei together.

Stronger the further you pull.

Strength

Roughly 100× the electric force at ~1 fm (α_s ≈ 1 vs α ≈ 1/137).

Confinement

Pull quarks apart and new quarks pop out. So no free quarks.

Open

Confinement is seen and simulated, but not proven mathematically (a Millennium Prize problem).

Chain Theory

Alex: confinement = (3,3) links are "dark" (conditional 171.1).

Why matter won?

unknown1 extra in a billion · size —

Matter and antimatter were made equal…

…except ~1 extra matter per billion.

The rest wiped out. Why? Unknown.

The numbers

~6×10⁻¹⁰ protons per photon today. Everything we see is that leftover.

Ideas

"Baryogenesis", "leptogenesis" from heavy neutrinos.

Why red

Known physics does not make enough of an imbalance.

New

2025: CP violation seen in baryons for the first time (LHCb, 5.2σ). But it is the small, expected kind.

Tests

Hyper-K (data from 2028) and DUNE (first beam by 2031) look for it in neutrinos.

Chain Theory

Gives the sign of the imbalance only, "not a magnitude" (Thm 211.2).

Hubble tension

unknownTwo speeds for one universe · size A cosmic-scale puzzle

Nearby: 73.5 km/s/Mpc.

7σ apart. Why? Unknown.

More distance ladders that agree, gravitational-wave "sirens", and Euclid / Roman maps.

Protons & neutrons

known3 quarks + gluon glue · size Proton radius 0.84 fm

Proton = 2 up + 1 down quark.

Neutron = 1 up + 2 down.

Formed ~10 microseconds in.

Recipe

3 quarks (uud) + a sea of gluons and quark pairs → proton.

Size: 0.84 fm radius (8.4×10⁻¹⁶ m).

Mass 938.3 MeV. The 3 quarks' own masses add to only ~9 MeV!

Randomness

None in the recipe. Which ones form where: random.

The numbers

~99% of the mass is gluon and motion energy (lattice QCD, Dürr 2008).

Free neutron lives ~15 min. Proton: stable (>10³⁴ years).

Chain Theory

Alex: a hadron is a "star": a junction qutrit joining three quark bodies (conditional). "Two ups and a down" is a reading, not yet a derivation.

Comes from: Quark-gluon soup

Sources: NIST/CODATA 2022: proton charge radius (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Dürr et al. 2008: proton mass from lattice QCD (Science) (paper · 2008 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Slides: Chain Theory, An Introduction (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

First nuclei

knownH, He, a trace of Li · size ~2–4 fm

Made in about 3–20 minutes.

~75% hydrogen, ~25% helium (by mass).

Plus a pinch of lithium.

Recipe

2 p + 2 n → helium-4 (via deuterium).

1 neutron for every ~7 protons when it started. Almost all neutrons ended in helium.

Energy

~1 billion °C, falling fast.

How we know

Predicted amounts of D, He, Li match old gas clouds.

Open

"Lithium problem": theory gives ~3× more lithium than old stars show.

Comes from: Protons & neutrons, Weak force

Sources: PDG review: Big-Bang nucleosynthesis (review · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Fields 2011: the primordial lithium problem (Annu. Rev.) (preprint · 2011 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia (extra): cosmological lithium problem (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

First atoms

knownNuclei catch electrons · size ~10⁻¹⁰ m (0.1 nm)

380,000 years after the Big Bang.

Cool enough (~3000 K) to hold electrons.

Space became see-through.

Recipe

1 nucleus + as many electrons as protons → neutral atom.

H = 1 p + 1 e. He = 2 p + 2 n + 2 e.

Size

Hydrogen: ~0.1 nm across. The nucleus is ~60,000× smaller across.

The numbers

Bohr radius 0.0529 nm. Binding 13.6 eV.

How we know

The CMB itself is the light set free here.

Comes from: First nuclei, Electrons & leptons, Electromagnetism

Sources: NIST/CODATA: Bohr radius (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Planck 2018 results VI: cosmological parameters (preprint · 2018 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Afterglow (CMB)

knownOldest light we can see · size Fills the visible universe

Light set free when atoms formed.

Now faint microwaves: 2.725 K.

Its bumps are 1 part in 100,000.

Recipe

Photons stop bumping into free electrons → fly freely.

Top-down

We read the universe's age and recipe from its pattern.

The numbers

2.7255 K. Found 1965 (Penzias & Wilson).

Comes from: First atoms, Photons, Seed ripples

Sources: Planck 2018 results VI: cosmological parameters (preprint · 2018 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

First molecules

knownHeH⁺ and H₂ · size ~10⁻¹⁰ m

HeH⁺ was the first molecule.

Then H₂ formed.

H₂ let gas cool enough to make stars.

Recipe

He + H⁺ → HeH⁺. Then H + H (via H⁻) → H₂.

Seen in space

HeH⁺ was finally found in space in 2019.

Why it matters

Without H₂ cooling, the first gas clouds could not shrink into stars.

Comes from: First atoms

Sources: Güsten et al. 2019: HeH⁺, the first molecule, found in space (Nature) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Gas clouds

knownGravity gathers gas · size ~10¹⁶–10¹⁸ m

Gas falls into dark-matter clumps.

It cools and collapses.

Dense cores become stars.

Recipe

H/He gas + a dark-matter halo + cooling by H₂ → collapsing cloud.

Randomness

Turbulence decides where cores form and how big stars get.

The numbers

First star-forming halos ~10⁵–10⁶ Sun masses (simulations).

Comes from: First atoms, Dark-matter clumps, First molecules

Sources: Bromm 2013: formation of the first stars (Rep. Prog. Phys.) (preprint · 2013 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia (extra): Population III stars (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

First stars

we thinkHuge, not yet confirmed · size Maybe ~10¹⁰ m (models)

Only hydrogen and helium.

Probably huge (tens–hundreds of Suns).

JWST: strong candidates (2026). Not confirmed.

When

~100–200 million years after the Big Bang (models).

Recipe

Cloud of ~10⁵ Sun masses → one or a few big stars.

New (2026)

"Hebe" (z = 10.6): a helium glow with no metal lines, called "one of the most convincing pieces of evidence" for first stars.

LAP1-B: the most chemically primitive galaxy known (Nature 2026).

Open

A 2026 follow-up of LAP1 found no strong helium signal. Never seen for sure.

Comes from: Gas clouds

Stars

knownSqueezed gas ignites · size Sun 1.39×10⁹ m

Gravity squeezes the core.

At ~10 million °C hydrogen fuses.

That is why stars shine.

Recipe

~0.08 to ~150 Sun masses of gas → a star. Less than 0.08: a brown dwarf.

The Sun: 2×10³⁰ kg, ~73% H, ~25% He.

Randomness

The mix of star sizes is set by turbulent collapse.

The numbers

Sun core 15.7 million K. It turns ~600 million tonnes of H into He every second.

~10²² –10²⁴ stars in the visible universe (estimate).

Comes from: Gas clouds, First stars

Sources: NASA: Sun facts (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Galaxies

knownBillions of stars swirl · size Milky Way ~10²¹ m

Milky Way: 100–400 billion stars.

Hundreds of billions of galaxies.

JWST sees them ~280–300 Myr after the Big Bang.

Recipe

A dark-matter halo + gas + stars + often a central black hole.

Milky Way: ~100,000 light-years across.

How many

Hundreds of billions of galaxies (estimates up to ~2 trillion).

Open

JWST finds bright galaxies earlier than expected.

Comes from: Gas clouds, Dark-matter clumps, Stars

Sources: Naidu et al. 2025: MoM-z14, a galaxy 280 million years after the Big Bang (preprint · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Lauer et al. 2021: how many galaxies (New Horizons) (preprint · 2020 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Giant black holes

we thinkBorn heavy? Leading idea · size Sgr A* horizon ~2.5×10¹⁰ m wide

Most galaxies have one in the middle.

Ours: 4 million Suns.

JWST hints some were born heavy. Not settled.

Known

They exist (stars orbiting Sgr A*, Nobel 2020; EHT images).

New (2025–26)

JWST "little red dots": in one (QSO1, seen ~13 billion years ago) the black hole outweighs all its stars, over 2×. This points to heavy seeds from collapsing gas.

Still open

Heavy seeds, or light seeds (first stars) growing very fast, or primordial black holes. A 2026 simulation finds light seeds grow slowly.

Colour

Orange: heavy "direct-collapse" seeds now have supporting evidence, but are not proven.

How we would know

More direct black-hole masses (JWST, ALMA). LISA (launch planned 2035) could hear seed black holes merge.

Comes from: Galaxies

Slow neutron catching

knowns-process in old stars · size Giant star ~10¹¹ m

Ageing Sun-like giants catch neutrons slowly.

Makes about half the elements past iron.

E.g. barium, lead, strontium.

Recipe

Nucleus + 1 neutron at a time (years between catches) → heavier, then beta-decay.

In stars of ~1–8 Sun masses (AGB stars).

Reach

Up to lead and bismuth.

How we know

Technetium (no stable form) is seen in such stars (Merrill, 1952).

Comes from: Fusion in stars

Sources: Burbidge, Burbidge, Fowler & Hoyle 1957 (B²FH) (paper · 1957 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Johnson 2019: Populating the periodic table (Science) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Fusion in stars

knownH → He → C, O … → Fe · size Cores ~10⁸ m

4 H → 1 He, in all stars.

3 He → 1 C, then O, Ne, Si…

Up to iron in big stars.

Recipes

4 protons → 1 helium-4 + 26.7 MeV (0.7% of mass becomes energy).

3 helium-4 → carbon-12 at ~100 million K.

C + He → O. Then Ne, Mg, Si, S… Silicon → iron at ~3 billion K.

Why stop at iron

Iron/nickel have the most tightly-bound nuclei. Fusing further costs energy.

Open

Exact rate of C + He → O is still uncertain; it sets how much O vs C we get.

Comes from: Stars, First stars, Strong force, Weak force

Sources: Burbidge, Burbidge, Fowler & Hoyle 1957 (B²FH) (paper · 1957 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · NASA: Sun facts (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Cosmic-ray splitting

knownMakes Li, Be, B · size Nuclear (~10⁻¹⁴ m)

Fast particles smash heavier atoms.

The bits are lithium, beryllium, boron.

Stars mostly destroy these.

Recipe

Fast proton + carbon/oxygen nucleus → smaller pieces.

How we know

Li, Be, B are ~10⁵–10⁷× rarer than C and O; the amounts match cosmic-ray models.

Note

Cosmic rays make only a small share of lithium: most comes from the Big Bang and from stars (novae, giants).

Comes from: Big-star supernovae

Sources: Reeves, Fowler & Hoyle 1970: cosmic rays make Li, Be and B (Nature) (paper · 1970 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia (extra): cosmic-ray spallation (Li, Be, B) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Johnson 2019: Populating the periodic table (Science) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Big-star supernovae

knownStars >8 Suns explode · size Blast reaches ~10¹³ m and beyond

The iron core collapses in under a second.

The blast spreads O, Si, Ca… into space.

Leaves a neutron star or black hole.

Recipe

Star > ~8 Sun masses + iron core ~1.4 Suns → collapse → explosion.

~99% of the energy leaves as neutrinos (seen for SN 1987A).

Open

Exactly how the blast restarts is still modelled, not fully solved.

Comes from: Fusion in stars

Sources: Johnson 2019: Populating the periodic table (Science) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Burbidge, Burbidge, Fowler & Hoyle 1957 (B²FH) (paper · 1957 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

White-dwarf blasts

knownType Ia: makes iron · size White dwarf ~10⁷ m (Earth-size)

A dead star's core gains mass, then blows up.

Makes about half or more of the iron in your blood.

Also used to find dark energy.

Recipe

White dwarf near 1.4 Sun masses (or two merging) → thermonuclear blast.

Open

Which path (gaining gas, or a merger) is most common is debated.

Comes from: Stars

Sources: Johnson 2019: Populating the periodic table (Science) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Neutron-star crashes

knownr-process: gold, platinum · size Neutron star ~2×10⁴ m

Two dead stars spiral and crash.

Neutron flood makes gold, platinum, uranium.

Seen in 2017 (GW170817).

Recipe

2 neutron stars (~1.4 Suns each, ~20 km wide) → kilonova.

Rapid neutron catching (r-process): many neutrons per second.

Open

Are mergers the main source, or rare supernovae ("collapsars") too? Debated.

Comes from: Big-star supernovae

Sources: LIGO/Virgo 2017: GW170817 neutron-star merger + kilonova (preprint · 2017 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Watson et al. 2019: strontium made in a neutron-star merger (Nature) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Johnson 2019: Populating the periodic table (Science) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Black holes

knownEven light can't escape · size 10 Suns ≈ 6×10⁴ m wide

Left by some dead big stars.

First image in 2019 (M87*).

Waves from crashes seen since 2015.

Recipe

Leftover core heavier than ~2–3 Suns collapses with nothing to stop it.

Open

What happens at the centre and to information: unknown (needs quantum gravity).

Comes from: Big-star supernovae, Gravity

All 118 elements

knownTap for the table · size Atoms ~0.06–0.6 nm

94 found in nature, 24 only made in labs.

Each one = a number of protons (1–118).

Tap "Open the periodic table".

Recipe

Element = Z protons + some neutrons + Z electrons.

Where they come from:

Big Bang: H, He, (Li). Big stars: O to Fe. Dying giants: about half past Fe. Neutron-star crashes: gold, uranium. White dwarfs: most iron.

The abundance zig-zag

Oddo–Harkins rule: even-proton nuclei are usually more abundant than their odd-proton neighbours. Neutron–proton pairing makes even nuclei more tightly bound and stable, so nuclear physics, not chance, gives this pattern.

The numbers

By mass the Sun is ~73% H, ~25% He, ~2% everything else.

Most superheavy elements (104–118) last seconds or less; the longest-lived (dubnium-268) ~16–30 hours (measurements differ).

Source

Origins after Jennifer Johnson's chart (2019).

Comes from: First nuclei, Fusion in stars, Slow neutron catching, Big-star supernovae, White-dwarf blasts, Neutron-star crashes, Cosmic-ray splitting

Sources: Johnson 2019: Populating the periodic table (Science) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · SDSS blog: origin of the elements (Jennifer Johnson chart) (news / announcement · 2017 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · IUPAC periodic table (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Nuclear pairing and abundance: even–odd effects and binding-energy systematics (NDC review) (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive) · archived copy)

Chemical bonds

knownAtoms share or swap electrons · size Bond length 0.1–0.3 nm

Covalent: share electrons.

Ionic: give electrons away.

Hydrogen bonds: weak stickiness.

Recipe

Outer electrons + electric force + quantum rules → a bond.

Energies

C–C ~3.6 eV. O–H ~4.8 eV. H-bond ~0.2 eV (why water is sticky).

How we know

Quantum mechanics predicts them (Pauling, 1930s). Measured by spectroscopy.

Comes from: All 118 elements, Electromagnetism

Chem.) (paper · 2011 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia (extra): hydrogen bond (strengths, kJ/mol) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Dust & disks

knownRings around young stars · size Disk ~10¹³ m

New stars have a spinning disk of gas and dust.

Dust grains: silicates, carbon, ice.

Telescopes (ALMA) see gaps where planets form.

Recipe

A collapsing cloud keeps spinning → flat disk (~99% gas, ~1% dust).

How we know

ALMA images of young disks (e.g. HL Tauri, 2014) show rings and gaps.

Randomness

Where clumps form in the disk is chaotic.

Comes from: All 118 elements, Stars

Molecules

knownWater, CO₂, methane… · size Water ~0.28 nm

Atoms bonded into fixed shapes.

About 350 kinds found in space.

Water = 2 H + 1 O.

Recipes

2 H + 1 O → water (angle 104.5°).

1 C + 2 O → CO₂. 1 C + 4 H → methane.

Randomness

Collisions are random; energy and stability decide what lasts.

The numbers

~350 molecules found in space (CDMS: 349 listed, Nov 2026), incl. sugars and amino-acid cousins.

Planets

knownDust clumps into worlds · size Earth 1.27×10⁷ m

Dust sticks into pebbles, then rocks.

Rocks crash into planets.

Giants also grab gas.

Recipe

Dust → pebbles → km "planetesimals" → embryos → planets, over ~10–100 million years.

Randomness

Crashes and orbits are chaotic; each system turns out different.

Open

How dust grows past metre size quickly ("metre barrier") is still being worked out.

knownOur Sun and its family · size Neptune orbit ~9×10¹² m

Formed 4.57 billion years ago.

8 planets around the Sun.

Sun = 99.8% of the mass.

Recipe

1 star + disk → 8 planets, dwarf planets, moons, comets.

How we know

Earth

knownRocky, wet, just right · size 12,742 km wide

Formed ~4.54 billion years ago.

Right distance for liquid water.

Has a magnetic shield.

Recipe (by mass)

Iron ~32%, oxygen ~30%, silicon ~15%, magnesium ~14%.

Iron core + rocky mantle + thin crust.

How we know

Oldest minerals: zircons 4.4 billion years old.

Top-down

Seismic waves reveal the core; meteorites match its make-up.

The Moon

we thinkBorn in a giant crash? · size 3,474 km wide

A Mars-size body probably hit Earth.

The debris made the Moon.

Leading idea, details debated.

Why it matters

It steadies Earth's tilt and makes big tides.

Clue

Moon rocks have nearly the same oxygen-isotope mix as Earth. Iron too.

New (2025)

The impactor "Theia" came from the inner Solar System, a near neighbour. That helps explain the match.

Open

Variants of the crash idea are still argued.

Early-Earth chemistry

we thinkVents, ponds, lightning · size Molecules ~1 nm

Energy builds new molecules.

Where? Deep vents or shallow ponds?

Leading ideas, not settled.

Why orange

The chemistry is real, but which setting started life is unproven.

Recipe

Simple molecules (H₂O, CO₂, HCN, NH₃…) + energy (heat, UV, lightning) + minerals.

Ideas

Deep-sea hydrothermal vents (energy + minerals) vs. warm little ponds (wet–dry cycles, UV).

New (2026)

A PNAS paper argues ancient alkaline vents were weaker and shorter-lived than thought. Not settled.

How we know

Lab experiments make many of life's pieces under these conditions.

Open

No single setting makes all the pieces at once.

Comes from: Water & oceans, Molecules

Amino acids

knownProtein letters (20) · size ~0.5 nm

Made in lab sparks (Miller, 1953).

Found in meteorites and asteroid Bennu.

Life uses 20 (+2 rare).

Recipe

C + H + O + N (+ S): ~10–27 atoms each.

Glycine = C₂H₅NO₂ (10 atoms, the smallest).

How they form

Sparks, UV or heat on simple gases and water; also inside asteroids.

The numbers

20 standard + selenocysteine + pyrrolysine = 22 genetically coded.

Meteorite Murchison: dozens of amino acids. Bennu (2025): 14 of the 20.

Open

Life uses only "left-handed" amino acids. Why one hand won is not settled.

Comes from: Early-Earth chemistry

Nucleotides

we thinkRNA/DNA letters (4) · size ~1 nm

Base + sugar + phosphate.

Asteroid Bennu has all three parts (ribose found Dec 2025).

Joining them naturally: partly shown.

Recipe

1 base (A, G, C, U/T) + 1 sugar (ribose) + 1 phosphate.

Why orange

Lab routes exist (Powner, Gerland & Sutherland 2009), but they need special steps in a set order.

How we know

Bennu had all 5 bases (A, G, C, T, U) and phosphate; ribose (the RNA sugar) was found in Dec 2025 (Nature Geoscience). No deoxyribose (DNA sugar) was found.

Open

How whole nucleotides built up naturally on early Earth.

Sugars

knownRibose, glucose · size ~0.5 nm

Form from formaldehyde ("formose").

Ribose found in meteorites (2019).

Ribose is RNA's backbone sugar.

Recipe

Ribose = C₅H₁₀O₅ (5 carbons). Glucose = C₆H₁₂O₆.

How they form

Formaldehyde + a mineral catalyst ("formose reaction") makes a messy mix of sugars.

Open

Formose makes many sugars; how ribose was picked out is unclear (borate minerals may help).

Comes from: Early-Earth chemistry

Sources: Furukawa et al. 2019: ribose sugar in meteorites (PNAS) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Fatty molecules

knownOily tails, wet heads · size ~2.5 nm long

Found in meteorites.

In water they bunch together.

They make cell skins.

Recipe

A water-loving head + 1–2 oily tails of ~10–20 carbons.

How they form

Fischer–Tropsch-type reactions; also in meteorites.

Note

Modern cell membranes use phospholipids; the first ones were probably simpler fatty acids.

Comes from: Early-Earth chemistry

Sources: Szostak, Bartel & Luisi 2001: synthesizing life (Nature) (paper · 2001 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia (extra): protocell (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

First peptides

we thinkShort amino-acid chains · size ~1–5 nm

Amino acids can link on wet-dry cycles.

Short chains, random order.

Exactly how: leading ideas.

Recipe

n amino acids → a chain + (n−1) water molecules.

How they form

Wet–dry cycles, mineral surfaces, or hot vents can join them.

Randomness

Order of letters: random at first.

New (2025)

Amino acids were attached to RNA in plain water, then joined into peptides on RNA (Powner lab, Nature 2025).

Open

How a random chain gained a useful job.

Comes from: Amino acids

RNA world

we thinkRNA copied itself first? · size ~10 nm

RNA can store info AND do work.

Maybe it came before DNA and proteins.

Leading idea, not proven.

Recipe

Nucleotides → strands (maybe ~50–200 letters, a guess) that help copy each other.

Clue

The ribosome's core is RNA.

New (2026)

A tiny 45-letter RNA (QT45) copied itself and its partner strand (in separate steps), slowly: ~0.2% in 72 days, in ice.

Open

A full, self-running copy cycle is still missing.

Comes from: Nucleotides, Sugars

Fatty bubbles

knownMembranes form on their own · size ~0.1 µm

Fatty acids in water make bubbles.

They grow and split.

A "skin" for the first cells.

Recipe

~10⁵ (small bubble) to ~10⁷ (1 µm bubble) lipids → a double layer ~4–5 nm thick.

How we know

Szostak lab: fatty-acid bubbles grow, split and let small molecules in.

Protocells

we thinkA bubble around a copier · size ~1 µm

RNA inside a fatty bubble.

Copies can be selected.

Built in labs in parts.

Recipe

1 bubble + RNA + small molecules + energy.

How we know

Parts have been built in labs (RNA copying inside vesicles, partly).

2026: "SpudCells" copy DNA, grow and divide, but scientists feed them ribosomes. Their maker says they are not alive.

Open

A full self-copying, evolving protocell has not been made.

unknownChemistry → alive: how? · size Unknown (the first cells were probably ~1 µm)

How chemistry became life is unknown.

2026 lab cells grow and divide, but are not alive.

Many gaps remain.

A lab protocell that copies, evolves and runs a metabolism on its own.

LUCA

knownLast universal common ancestor · size ~1–2 µm

All life shares one ancestor.

~4.2 billion years ago (estimate).

Already had DNA, ribosomes, the code.

What it had

DNA genome, ribosomes, the genetic code, a membrane, ATP energy.

~2,600 protein families (estimate, Moody 2024).

Size

Probably like a bacterium: ~1–2 µm.

How we know

All life shares the same code and core genes, so one ancestor is the best fit.

Numbers

~4.2 billion years ago (range ~4.09–4.33, estimate).

Note

LUCA is the cell all life traces back to, not the very first cell.

Comes from: The first spark

Sources: Moody et al. 2024: LUCA ~4.2 billion years ago (Nat. Ecol. Evol.) (paper · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Proteins

knownFolded amino-acid chains · size ~2–10 nm

Chains of 20 kinds of amino acid.

Typical: ~300–500 long.

Longest (titin): ~34,000.

Recipe

~50–34,000 amino acids → a chain that folds into a 3D shape.

Median human protein: ~400 amino acids.

How they form

A ribosome reads mRNA, adding ~5–20 amino acids per second.

Numbers

Humans: ~20,000 protein-coding genes; titin is the longest (~34,350).

Open

Predicting the fold from the sequence is now mostly solved (AlphaFold, Nobel 2024); how the first proteins arose is not.

Comes from: LUCA, Amino acids

Sources: Labeit & Kolmerer 1995: titins, giant muscle proteins (Science) (paper · 1995 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Wikipedia (extra): titin, the longest protein (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Nurk et al. 2022: the complete human genome (Science) (paper · 2022 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Genetic code

known3 letters = 1 amino acid · size One codon (3 RNA letters) is ~1 nm long

64 codons → 20 amino acids + stop.

Almost the same in all life.

Read by ribosomes.

Recipe

4 letters (A, U, G, C) read 3 at a time = 64 codons.

61 code for 20 amino acids, 3 mean "stop".

Open

Why this exact code? A "frozen accident" or chemistry? Debated.

Comes from: LUCA

Sources: Crick et al. 1961: general nature of the genetic code (Nature) (paper · 1961 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia (extra): genetic code (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

DNA

knownLife's recipe book · size 2 nm wide; ~2 m per cell

Double helix, found in 1953.

Human: ~3.1 billion letter pairs.

Stores instructions for life.

Recipe

Pairs: A–T and G–C. 0.34 nm per pair, ~10.5 pairs per turn.

Human: ~3.1 billion pairs per set (×2 sets per cell ≈ 2 m of DNA).

How it forms

Copied by enzymes from an existing strand.

How we know

X-ray photo by Rosalind Franklin; model by Watson & Crick (1953).

Complete human genome finished 2022 (T2T, 3.055 billion pairs).

Open

Did RNA come first? Probably (orange RNA world).

Comes from: LUCA, Nucleotides

Sources: Nurk et al. 2022: the complete human genome (Science) (paper · 2022 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Bacteria & archaea

knownSimple cells, no nucleus · size ~1–2 µm

First fossils ~3.5 billion years (debated).

Still most of life by number.

E. coli: 4.6 million DNA pairs.

Recipe

1 membrane + DNA loop + ~4,000 genes + ribosomes.

E. coli: ~2 µm long, 4.6 million DNA pairs.

How we know

Stromatolites 3.48 billion years old (Dresser Formation). The very oldest fossil claims are debated; chemical hints maybe older.

Photosynthesis

knownSunlight → food + oxygen · size ~1 µm cells

First kinds made no oxygen.

Cyanobacteria then split water with light, giving off oxygen.

Later passed to algae and plants.

Recipe

6 CO₂ + 6 H₂O + light → sugar + 6 O₂.

Note

The earliest photosynthesis was anoxygenic (made no oxygen). Oxygen-making evolved later, in cyanobacteria.

Comes from: Bacteria & archaea

Oxygen builds up in the air.

Opens the way to bigger cells.

~2.4–2.1 billion years ago.

How we know

Rust bands and sulfur isotopes in old rocks.

Complex cells

knownCells with a nucleus · size ~10–100 µm

An archaeon took in a bacterium.

That bacterium became mitochondria.

~2 billion years ago (estimate).

Recipe

1 archaeal host (Asgard-type) + 1 swallowed bacterium → mitochondria.

Later, a cyanobacterium swallowed → chloroplasts (plants, algae).

Size

~10–100 µm: often 1,000× or more the volume of a bacterium.

Numbers

Oldest clear fossils ~1.6–1.8 billion years; origin ~2 billion (estimate, range wide).

Maths Map

knownCounting → open problems · size Not a physical scale

A complete second map: counting → proofs → frontier.

Opens as a Map or a Ring, in the same style.

Source-backed cards; established and open claims stay separate.

What is inside

Number, geometry, algebra, logic, analysis, probability, topology, combinatorics, category theory, computation, information, and open problems.

Status language

Green = established definition or theorem / solved landmark. Orange = partial or provisional claimed progress. Red = open problem, clearly stated.

Cross-links

Both maps link to each other from the relevant cards. Mathematics can describe or support a theory without proving it physical.

Comes from: Before the Big Bang?

Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Clay Mathematics Institute: Millennium Prize Problems (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

String theory

unknownRival: tiny vibrating strings · size Strings near the Planck length (~10⁻³⁵ m)

Particles = vibrating strings.

Needs extra hidden dimensions.

Rich maths, no test yet.

The idea

Every particle is one tiny string vibrating in a different way. Gravity comes out automatically.

It needs 10 dimensions (11 in M-theory); the extra ones would be curled up too small to see.

Where it stands

The biggest quantum-gravity programme. It gave holography (AdS/CFT), a key tool. But it allows a huge number of possible universes, so it has not picked out ours.

Why red

No experiment has seen strings, extra dimensions or supersymmetry (LHC so far).

Compared with Chain Theory

Strings start from objects in space; Chain Theory starts from links with no space at all.

Comes from: Quantum gravity

Test: LHC keeps searching for supersymmetry and extra dimensions (none so far).

Sources: Particle Data Group: Review of Particle Physics (review · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: Gravity-made entanglement in the lab (tests every quantum-gravity idea).

Sources: Nanodiamond interferometers for gravity-made entanglement (arXiv 2405.21029) (preprint · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: A distinctive string prediction at reachable energies.

Sources: Polchinski 1994: What is string theory? (lectures) (preprint · 1994 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sources: Polchinski 1994: What is string theory? (lectures) (preprint · 1994 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Maldacena 1997: the holographic (AdS/CFT) duality (preprint · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Particle Data Group: Review of Particle Physics (review · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Loop quantum gravity

unknownRival: space made of loops · size Smallest areas near the Planck scale

Space itself comes in tiny chunks.

No extra dimensions needed.

No test yet.

The idea

Quantise space itself: areas and volumes come in smallest units, woven from loops (spin networks).

It needs no extra dimensions and no strings.

Where it stands

A smaller programme than strings. Hard part: showing the smooth space we see comes back at large scales.

Why red

No measured effect yet. Some versions predict a "bounce" instead of a Big Bang start.

Compared with Chain Theory

Closest in spirit: both build space from a network. Loops carry geometry; Chain Theory's links carry entanglement.

Comes from: Quantum gravity

Test: Gravity-made entanglement in the lab (tests every quantum-gravity idea).

Sources: Nanodiamond interferometers for gravity-made entanglement (arXiv 2405.21029) (preprint · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Test: Test specific loop-cosmology scenarios against CMB data; proposed signatures depend on the model.

Sources: Barrau 2017: testing loop quantum cosmology (scenario-dependent CMB signatures) (preprint · 2017 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Maths map: from counting to open problems

Green: an established definition, theorem or solved landmark. Orange: partial or provisional progress, not a settled general answer. Red: an explicitly stated open problem.

Counting & number

known — established definition or theoremMany cultures · prehistory · Names for how many

Numbers describe size, order and position. Different societies built different symbols and counting systems.

What counts as known: A definition or theorem is known mathematics only with explicit assumptions and a valid proof. Checking many examples is not a proof for every case.

Where it began: Counting and measurement developed across many cultures; there is no single inventor of "numbers".

Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Place-value notation

known — established definition or theoremBabylonian, Indian & other traditions · Position carries value

The value of a digit depends on its position. This makes large numbers and efficient calculation much easier.

Examples: Babylonian mathematics used base 60; decimal place value with zero developed in India and travelled through other mathematical traditions.

Careful credit: Place-value systems arose independently in more than one place; the story is not one straight chain of invention.

Builds from: Counting & number

Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Arabic mathematics and al-Khwārizmī (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Arithmetic

known — established definition or theoremA shared foundation · +, −, ×, ÷

Arithmetic combines quantities and reverses those combinations. Its rules are the groundwork for calculation and algebra.

The core objects: Whole-number operations, order, divisibility and remainders can be treated rigorously within an axiom system.

Larger systems: Extending whole numbers gives negatives, fractions, decimals, real numbers and complex numbers.

Builds from: Place-value notation

Sources: OpenStax: Algebra and Trigonometry (textbook · 2015 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Fractions & ratio

known — established definition or theoremAncient to modern · Parts of a whole

A rational number is a ratio of two integers, with a nonzero denominator. Ratios compare quantities without needing a whole unit.

Equivalent forms: 2/3 and 4/6 name the same rational number. A fraction is a number, not a command to divide a physical object.

Beyond fractions: Numbers such as √2 cannot be written as a ratio of integers; the Greeks studied this tension geometrically.

Builds from: Arithmetic

Sources: OpenStax: Algebra and Trigonometry (textbook · 2015 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Zero & negatives

known — established definition or theoremBrahmagupta · 628 CE (rules) · Nothing becomes a number

Zero can be a placeholder and a number in its own right. Negative numbers extend calculation below zero.

Historical care: Brahmagupta gave arithmetic rules for zero and negatives in 628 CE, but zero as a concept and placeholder has an older, wider history.

Not an inventor claim: No single culture or person should be credited with every aspect of zero.

Builds from: Place-value notation

Sources: MacTutor: zero and Brahmagupta (history reference · 2000 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Real numbers

known — established definition or theoremRigorous constructions · 19th c. · A continuous number line

Real numbers fill the number line, including irrational values. Their completeness makes limits possible.

The key property: Every nonempty set of real numbers with an upper bound has a least upper bound. This is completeness.

Constructions: Dedekind cuts and equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences are standard constructions; they are different ways to build the same structure.

Builds from: Fractions & ratio, Zero & negatives

Sources: MacTutor: the real numbers and their history (history reference · 2005 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 1 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Complex numbers

known — established definition or theorem16th–19th c. · A real part + i × an imaginary part

Complex numbers include i, with i² = −1. They are points in a plane, not "less real" than other mathematical numbers.

Why they help: Every nonconstant complex polynomial has a root (the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra).

Geometry: Multiplication by a complex number combines scaling and rotation; Euler's formula links exponentials and trigonometry.

Builds from: Real numbers

Sources: MacTutor: the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra and complex numbers (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Algebra and Trigonometry (textbook · 2015 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Euclidean geometry

known — established definition or theoremEuclid · c. 300 BCE · Shape from explicit assumptions

Euclid's Elements organised geometry using definitions, postulates and proofs. Its lasting method matters as much as its individual results.

A model of proof: The parallel postulate cannot be replaced silently. Changing it leads to consistent non-Euclidean geometries.

Scope: "Euclidean" does not mean this is the only geometry of the physical universe.

Builds from: Counting & number

Sources: MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: non-Euclidean geometry (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Pythagoras’ theorem

known — established definition or theoremKnown in several ancient traditions · a² + b² = c²

In a right triangle, the squares of the two shorter sides have the same total area as the square of the hypotenuse.

A theorem, not a guess: There are many proofs within Euclidean geometry. The Euclidean assumption is essential.

Historical care: Babylonian mathematics used Pythagorean relationships long before the classical Greek tradition; do not treat the named Greek theorem as the whole history.

Builds from: Euclidean geometry

Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Coordinate geometry

known — established definition or theoremDescartes & Fermat · 17th c. · Geometry meets equations

Coordinates turn geometric objects into equations, and equations back into shapes.

Why it matters: Lengths, slopes, conics and motion can now be studied algebraically.

Bridge: This is the route from Euclidean geometry to functions, calculus and vector geometry.

Builds from: Euclidean geometry, Algebra

Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 1 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Non-Euclidean geometry

known — established definition or theorem19th c. · Lobachevsky, Bolyai & others · The parallel rule can change

Replacing Euclid's parallel postulate produces different consistent geometries. A sphere's shortest paths are one familiar model.

What changed: Geometry is about consequences of chosen rules, so "parallel lines" can behave differently in different settings.

Physical link: Later mathematics of curved spaces became essential for general relativity.

Builds from: Euclidean geometry

Sources: MacTutor: non-Euclidean geometry (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MIT OpenCourseWare: Geometry of Manifolds (textbook · 2004 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Trigonometry

known — established definition or theoremAstronomy across many cultures · Angles, triangles & waves

Trigonometry relates angles to lengths, and sine and cosine also describe periodic processes.

Unit circle: Cosine and sine give a point's x and y coordinates on the unit circle.

Applications: They describe oscillations, rotations, waves and Fourier analysis — not just triangles.

Builds from: Euclidean geometry

Sources: MacTutor: history of trigonometry (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Algebra and Trigonometry (textbook · 2015 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Vectors

known — established definition or theorem19th c. formalism · Size + direction

Vectors describe directed quantities such as displacement, but the abstract idea also includes functions and polynomials.

Operations: Addition, scaling, length, dot products and cross products each answer different geometric or physical questions.

Generalisation: A vector space is defined by rules for addition and scalar multiplication, not by arrows alone.

Builds from: Arithmetic

Sources: Axler: Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition (free author copy) (textbook · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 3 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Matrices & linear maps

known — established definition or theorem19th c. formalism · Transform several coordinates

A matrix records how a linear transformation acts on coordinates. Multiplication composes transformations.

Order matters: AB need not equal BA. A matrix is not just a rectangular table of numbers; its meaning depends on the chosen bases.

Uses: Linear maps describe rotations, projections, systems of equations and many approximations to nonlinear change.

Builds from: Vectors

Sources: Axler: Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition (free author copy) (textbook · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Linear algebra

known — established definition or theoremDeveloped across 19th–20th c. · Flat structure, many dimensions

Linear algebra studies vectors, linear maps and systems of linear equations in any finite or infinite dimension.

Core ideas: Linear independence, basis, dimension, rank, eigenvalues and eigenvectors explain what a transformation preserves and changes.

Why it matters: Large datasets, computer graphics, networks and physical models all use this language.

Builds from: Matrices & linear maps

Sources: Axler: Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition (free author copy) (textbook · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Eigenvalues & spectra

known — established definition or theoremSpectral theory · 19th–20th c. · Directions that only stretch

An eigenvector keeps its direction under a linear map, while its eigenvalue gives the scale change.

Spectra: Spectral theory generalises this idea to operators, including infinite-dimensional settings.

Spectrum has two jobs: It describes linear behaviour, but an operator's spectrum is not just a list of numbers in every infinite-dimensional setting.

Builds from: Linear algebra

Sources: Axler: Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition (free author copy) (textbook · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: spectrum of an operator (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Inner products

known — established definition or theoremFinite & infinite dimensions · Angles and length, generalised

An inner product supplies lengths, angles and orthogonal projection in a vector space.

Dot product is one example: Functions can also be multiplied and integrated to define an inner product.

Limit needed: A complete inner-product space is called a Hilbert space; finite-dimensional Euclidean spaces already satisfy this.

Builds from: Linear algebra, Limits & continuity

Sources: Axler: Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition (free author copy) (textbook · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Hilbert space (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Hilbert spaces

known — established definition or theoremHilbert school · early 20th c. · Geometry for quantum states

A Hilbert space is a complete inner-product space. It supports orthogonal decompositions and rigorous quantum state spaces.

Quantum mechanics: States are represented by vectors (strictly, usually rays) in a Hilbert space; observables use operators.

Caution: The space is a mathematical structure. Physical meaning comes from a model and its rules of interpretation.

Builds from: Inner products

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Hilbert space (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · MIT OpenCourseWare: Quantum Theory I — mathematical formalism (textbook · 2017 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Algebra

known — established definition or theoremal-Khwārizmī · 9th c. & wider traditions · Letters for unknowns & rules

Algebra uses symbols for arbitrary or unknown quantities, so relationships can be reasoned about independently of a particular value.

Not just x: Equation-solving, symbolic manipulation and later abstract structures all belong to algebra.

Historical care: Equation-solving is older than al-Khwārizmī; his work is a major landmark in a much broader global history.

Builds from: Arithmetic

Sources: MacTutor: Arabic mathematics and al-Khwārizmī (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Algebra and Trigonometry (textbook · 2015 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Equations & functions

known — established definition or theoremFunction idea · 17th–19th c. · Relations & input–output

An equation states a constraint; a function assigns exactly one output to each input in its domain.

Keep the meanings distinct: y = x² can define a function; x² + y² = 1 defines a circle but not a single global function y(x).

Structure: Composition, inverses, domain and range matter before learning formulas.

Builds from: Algebra

Sources: OpenStax: Algebra and Trigonometry (textbook · 2015 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 1 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Polynomials

known — established definition or theoremAncient problems → modern theory · Powers with coefficients

Polynomials combine a variable using addition, multiplication and nonnegative integer powers. Their roots and factors carry rich structure.

Degree: Linear and quadratic equations have classical solution methods; cubic and quartic formulas came later.

Limits of formulas: There is no general formula using only radicals for every polynomial of degree five or more.

Builds from: Equations & functions

Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Algebra and Trigonometry (textbook · 2015 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Galois theory (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Proof & deduction

known — established definition or theoremAxiomatic traditions · ancient & modern · Why a claim must follow

A proof establishes a conclusion from stated assumptions using valid logical steps. It can be checked without polling every example.

Methods: Direct proof, contradiction, contraposition, induction and construction are different tools.

Status language: On this map green means an established definition or theorem, not that every possible question in that branch is finished.

Builds from: Counting & number

Sources: Hammack: Book of Proof (open textbook) (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Formal logic

known — established definition or theoremAristotle → Frege → modern logic · Precise rules for reasoning

Logic formalises connectives, quantifiers, inference and truth. It distinguishes a statement's form from the topic being discussed.

Propositional & predicate: Predicate logic uses "for all" and "there exists" to talk about objects and relations.

Models: Semantics and proof theory are connected, but truth in a model and derivability from axioms are not identical ideas.

Builds from: Proof & deduction

Sources: Open Logic Project: formal logic textbooks (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: classical logic (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Set theory

known — established definition or theoremCantor · late 19th c. · Collections with membership

Sets collect objects under a membership relation. Modern axiomatic set theory provides a common foundation for much mathematics.

Infinity becomes mathematical: Cantor compared infinite sets using one-to-one correspondences rather than finite counting alone.

Do not allow arbitrary collections: The set of all sets that do not contain themselves is not a set in standard axiomatic set theory; unrestricted comprehension leads to paradox.

Builds from: Formal logic

Sources: Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: set theory (encyclopedia · 2023 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Levin: Discrete Mathematics — an Open Introduction (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Axioms & foundations

known — established definition or theoremZFC & alternatives · 20th c. · The starting rules are explicit

A foundation gives mathematical objects and proofs a shared framework. ZFC is common, but not the only foundation.

Choice matters: A theorem depends on its assumptions. A result proved using the axiom of choice should not be described as choice-free.

Not a theory of everything: Foundations organise mathematics; they do not decide which physical theory describes nature.

Builds from: Set theory, Formal logic

Sources: Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: set theory (encyclopedia · 2023 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: proof theory (encyclopedia · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sizes of infinity

known — established definition or theoremCantor · 1870s–1890s · Countable & uncountable

Infinite sets can have different cardinalities. The natural numbers are countable; the real numbers are uncountable.

Diagonal argument: Any proposed list of all real numbers can be used to construct a real number missing from that list.

Counterintuitive but proved: The integers, even integers and rationals are countable, despite proper inclusions between these sets.

Builds from: Set theory
