Sources: Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: set theory (encyclopedia · 2023 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Georg Cantor (history reference · 1998 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Gödel’s incompleteness

known — established definition or theoremGödel · 1931 · Some truths escape a formal system

Sufficiently strong, effectively axiomatised, consistent formal systems have statements they cannot prove or refute.

The qualifications matter: This concerns systems that can represent enough arithmetic; it is not "nothing can be proved".

Consistency: Under the stated hypotheses, such a system cannot prove its own consistency by its own methods. Frauds and oversimplifications often omit these conditions.

Builds from: Axioms & foundations

Sources: Stanford Encyclopedia: Gödel’s incompleteness theorems (encyclopedia · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Kurt Gödel (history reference · 2003 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Computability

known — established definition or theoremTuring, Church · 1930s · What can an algorithm decide?

Computability studies which functions and questions have effective procedures, independently of how fast a modern computer is.

Undecidability: There is no general algorithm that decides whether every possible program halts on every input.

Model of computation: Turing machines are a precise mathematical model, not a claim that every physical device is a Turing machine.

Builds from: Gödel’s incompleteness, Formal logic

Sources: Turing: On Computable Numbers (1936–37) (paper (archived copy) · 1936 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Stanford Encyclopedia: computability and complexity (encyclopedia · 2021 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Number theory

known — established definition or theoremAncient → modern · Patterns in integers

Number theory studies integers and their generalisations, including divisibility, primes, congruences and equations with integer solutions.

Proofs & patterns: Many statements can be checked for many examples yet remain unproved in general. Green results and red conjectures stay separate here.

Connections: The subject connects algebra, geometry, analysis, computation and cryptography.

Builds from: Fractions & ratio, Polynomials

Sources: MacTutor: history of arithmetic and number theory (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Levin: Discrete Mathematics — an Open Introduction (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Prime numbers

known — established definition or theoremInfinitude known in antiquity · Multiplicative building blocks

A prime greater than 1 has exactly two positive divisors. Every positive integer above 1 factors into primes uniquely, up to order.

Infinitely many: Euclid's proof uses a finite list of primes to construct a number requiring a prime outside that list.

Distribution: Infinitude is known; many detailed distribution patterns remain active research.

Builds from: Number theory

Sources: MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Levin: Discrete Mathematics — an Open Introduction (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Modular arithmetic

known — established definition or theoremGauss · 1801 systematic treatment · Arithmetic with remainders

Two integers are congruent modulo n when their difference is divisible by n. This is arithmetic on cyclic remainder classes.

Clock idea: The clock analogy helps, but modular arithmetic includes much more than hours.

Applications: Congruences underpin divisibility tests, cryptography and many parts of algebra and number theory.

Builds from: Prime numbers

Sources: MacTutor: Carl Friedrich Gauss (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Levin: Discrete Mathematics — an Open Introduction (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Khan Academy: modular arithmetic and publicly shared codes (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Fermat’s Last Theorem

known — solved theoremWiles & Taylor–Wiles · 1995 · A famous problem — solved

For n > 2, there are no positive integers a, b, c with aⁿ + bⁿ = cⁿ.

Breakthrough: Andrew Wiles published his proof in 1995; a gap in the initial approach was repaired with Richard Taylor.

Beyond the statement: The proof used deep links between elliptic curves and modular forms. The short elementary statement does not imply a short elementary proof.

Solved record: This is deliberately green. It is not an open bounty.

Builds from: Number theory, Elliptic curves

Sources: Wiles: Modular elliptic curves and Fermat’s Last Theorem (1995) (paper · 1995 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Taylor & Wiles: Ring-theoretic properties of Hecke algebras (1995) (paper · 1995 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Twin prime conjecture

unknown — open problemAncient-looking question · no accepted proof · Prime pairs two apart forever?

Are there infinitely many pairs of primes that differ by exactly 2? No accepted proof or disproof exists.

Real partial progress: Zhang proved bounded prime gaps in 2013; Maynard, Tao and the Polymath8 collaboration reduced a proven bound to 246.

Do not blur the gap: A bounded gap of 246 is not a gap of 2. The twin-prime statement remains open.

Builds from: Prime numbers

Sources: Maynard: Small gaps between primes (Annals of Mathematics, 2015) (paper · 2015 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · AMS: small and bounded prime gaps (survey) (paper · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive) · archived copy)

Goldbach’s conjecture

unknown — open problemGoldbach–Euler correspondence · 1742 · Even numbers as prime sums

The strong binary Goldbach conjecture says every even integer greater than 2 is a sum of two primes.

Checked is not proved: Computer checks over finite ranges do not settle every even integer.

Different statement: Results for sums of three primes or almost-primes are important but are not a proof of the binary conjecture.

Builds from: Prime numbers

Sources: MacTutor: Christian Goldbach (history reference · 2006 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wolfram MathWorld: selected unsolved problems and current status (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Collatz conjecture

unknown — open problemLothar Collatz · 1930s · A simple rule, no general proof

Halve an even positive integer; apply 3n + 1 to an odd one. Must every starting number eventually reach 1?

Simple to state: The rule is elementary. Extensive computation is not a proof for all positive integers.

Status: No accepted general proof or counterexample is documented by the reference here. This map assigns no probability to its truth.

Builds from: Number theory

Sources: Wolfram MathWorld: selected unsolved problems and current status (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Lagarias: The 3x+1 problem and its generalizations (1985) (lecture notes / web page · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Riemann hypothesis

unknown — open problemBernhard Riemann · 1859 · Zeros of the zeta function

The conjecture says every nontrivial zero of the Riemann zeta function has real part 1/2.

Why primes are involved: The zeta function relates analysis to the distribution of primes through products and explicit formulas.

Precision: "All zeros" is wrong without "nontrivial"; negative even integers are known trivial zeros. Numerical checks do not settle the conjecture.

Prize status: Clay lists the Riemann Hypothesis as unsolved, checked 6 Nov 2026.

Builds from: Prime numbers, Zeta & L-functions

Sources: Clay: Riemann Hypothesis (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Clay Mathematics Institute: Millennium Prize Problems (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Probability

known — established definition or theoremFermat & Pascal · 1650s → Kolmogorov · 1933 · A precise model of chance

Probability assigns numerical likelihoods to events under a specified model. It does not predict a guaranteed individual outcome.

Foundations: Modern probability uses a sample space, events and a probability measure satisfying Kolmogorov's axioms.

Interpretations vary: Frequency and degree-of-belief interpretations can motivate different uses of the same calculus.

Builds from: Arithmetic, Set theory

Sources: Kolmogorov: Foundations of the Theory of Probability (English translation) (paper (archived copy) · 1933 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Introductory Statistics (textbook · 2013 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Random variables

known — established definition or theoremModern probability · Chance mapped to numbers

A random variable assigns a numerical value to each outcome. Its distribution describes probabilities of possible values.

Not a fluctuating variable: The word random refers to the probability model. A distribution describes long-run or modelled probabilities.

Expectation: A mean is a weighted average, not a promise about a single observation or a finite run.

Builds from: Probability

Sources: OpenStax: Introductory Statistics (textbook · 2013 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Kolmogorov: Foundations of the Theory of Probability (English translation) (paper (archived copy) · 1933 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Statistics & inference

known — established definition or theoremMethods across 19th–20th c. · Learn from data without certainty

Statistics summarises data and infers properties of a model or population under assumptions. Uncertainty is quantified, not erased.

Uncertainty: A confidence interval has a precise repeated-sampling interpretation. A p-value is not the probability that the hypothesis is true.

Data limitations: Bias, sampling design, measurement, model mismatch and multiple testing can all affect conclusions.

Builds from: Random variables

Sources: OpenStax: Introductory Statistics (textbook · 2013 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · American Statistical Association: statement on p-values (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Conditional probability & Bayes

known — established definition or theoremBayes & Laplace · 18th–19th c. · Update with evidence

Conditional probability updates a model when information is known. Bayes' theorem reverses conditional relationships.

Formula: P(A|B) = P(B|A)P(A)/P(B), when P(B) > 0.

Important distinction: P(A|B) need not equal P(B|A): a test's sensitivity is not the same as the probability of having a condition after a positive test.

Builds from: Probability

Sources: OpenStax: Introductory Statistics (textbook · 2013 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Kolmogorov: Foundations of the Theory of Probability (English translation) (paper (archived copy) · 1933 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Information & entropy

known — established definition or theoremClaude Shannon · 1948 · Uncertainty in bits

Shannon entropy measures uncertainty in a probability distribution. For bits, H = −Σ p log₂ p.

What it means: Entropy is not a measure of semantic meaning or truth. The distribution and question matter.

Compression & channels: Information theory gives fundamental limits for compression and communication; practical implementations remain engineering design.

Builds from: Probability

Sources: Shannon: A Mathematical Theory of Communication (1948) (paper (archived copy) · 1948 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: information theory (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Codes & compression

known — established definition or theoremShannon theory & later practical codes · Represent information efficiently

Coding represents symbols so they can be stored, transmitted or recovered. Compression can exploit predictable patterns.

Two kinds: Lossless codes preserve exact data; lossy schemes discard some information under fidelity criteria.

Limits: No lossless method can shorten every possible input; pigeonhole rules forbid that universal promise.

Builds from: Information & entropy

Sources: Shannon: A Mathematical Theory of Communication (1948) (paper (archived copy) · 1948 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: information theory (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Error-correcting codes

known — established definition or theoremHamming & Shannon · mid-20th c. · Add structure to survive noise

Redundancy can let a receiver detect or correct transmission errors without receiving a clean original stream first.

Trade-offs: Rate, distance, noise and decoding complexity determine what a code can achieve.

Connections: Algebra, combinatorics, probability and geometry all contribute to modern coding theory.

Builds from: Information & entropy

Sources: Shannon: A Mathematical Theory of Communication (1948) (paper (archived copy) · 1948 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Hamming: Error Detecting and Error Correcting Codes (1950) (book / historical text · 1950 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Limits & continuity

known — established definition or theoremRigorous analysis · 19th c. · Approach a value precisely

A limit describes behaviour as inputs approach a point. Continuity rules out specified kinds of jumps at a point.

Epsilon–delta: The formal definition quantifies how close outputs must be and how close inputs need to be to achieve it.

Not just substitution: Direct substitution works for continuous functions, but limits also treat cases where the function is undefined at the point.

Builds from: Real numbers, Equations & functions

Sources: OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 1 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: the rise of calculus (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Derivatives

known — established definition or theoremNewton & Leibniz · late 17th c. · Instantaneous change

A derivative is a limit of average changes. It measures a function's local linear change.

Notation: dy/dx is notation for a derivative, not literally a fraction in every context; treating it as one without care can mislead.

Geometry & modelling: Slopes, velocities, sensitivities and optimisation all use derivatives.

Builds from: Limits & continuity

Sources: OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 1 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: the rise of calculus (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Integrals

known — established definition or theoremNewton & Leibniz → Riemann → Lebesgue · Accumulate small contributions

Integration combines local contributions into area, mass, probability and other totals. Different definitions handle different functions.

Fundamental theorem: Differentiation and integration are linked under clear hypotheses. Approximation, computation and antiderivatives are not always the same task.

Lebesgue theory: Measure and Lebesgue integration extend analysis to broad classes of functions and underpin modern probability.

Builds from: Derivatives, Limits & continuity

Sources: OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 2 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: the rise of calculus (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Lebesgue integral (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Sequences & series

known — established definition or theoremAncient → modern analysis · Add infinitely many terms, carefully

An infinite series has a sum when its sequence of partial sums converges. Rearrangement and approximation need care.

Power series: Some functions match a power series only on a stated interval; outside it, the same expression may diverge or represent something else.

Taylor series: A Taylor expansion can give local approximations, but existence of every derivative does not automatically guarantee equality to the series.

Builds from: Integrals

Sources: OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 2 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: series (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Differential equations

known — established definition or theoremCalculus → modern dynamical systems · Rules for how a system changes

An ordinary differential equation relates a function to its derivatives. It encodes a rule of change, not just a static shape.

Initial conditions: Existence and uniqueness depend on assumptions; a differential equation alone may have many solutions.

Qualitative behaviour: Stability, equilibrium and numerical approximation matter even without a formula for the solution.

Builds from: Derivatives

Sources: OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 2 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: ordinary differential equations (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Vector calculus

known — established definition or theorem19th c. · electromagnetism & fluids · Change through space

Gradient, divergence and curl describe how scalar and vector fields vary through space.

Integral theorems: Green, Stokes and divergence theorems connect boundary behaviour to accumulated change inside a region, with regularity assumptions.

Physics: Fields, flux and conservation laws use this language; the mathematics alone does not choose a physical law.

Builds from: Vectors, Integrals, Derivatives

Sources: OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 3 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: vector analysis (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Partial differential equations

known — established definition or theorem18th c. → modern analysis · Change across space and time

PDEs involve partial derivatives in more than one independent variable. Waves, heat and fluids are standard examples.

Different types: Elliptic, parabolic and hyperbolic equations have different behaviours, but real problems can mix types.

General open questions: Many individual PDE problems are solved. Others, including global regularity questions for particular equations, remain open.

Builds from: Vector calculus, Differential equations

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: partial differential equations (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Clay Mathematics Institute: Millennium Prize Problems (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Dynamical systems & chaos

known — established definition or theoremPoincaré → Lorenz → modern · Simple rules, hard predictions

Dynamical systems study how states evolve. Sensitive dependence can make long-range numerical prediction difficult even in deterministic models.

Not randomness by definition: Chaos is not a synonym for randomness. The equations can be deterministic while sensitive to initial conditions.

Measures of behaviour: Attractors, bifurcations and stability help describe whole families of trajectories.

Builds from: Differential equations, Computability

Sources: Lorenz: Deterministic nonperiodic flow (1963) (paper · 1963 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: dynamical system (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Groups

known — established definition or theorem19th c. · Galois & many others · One operation + symmetry

A group has an associative operation, an identity and inverses. It captures reversible symmetry, commutative or not.

Examples: Integer addition, nonzero real multiplication, rotations and permutations form groups under their specified operations.

Representation: A group can act on another object or vector space instead of appearing only as an abstract multiplication table.

Builds from: Algebra, Set theory

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: group (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · MacTutor: development of group theory (history reference · 2001 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Rings & fields

known — established definition or theorem19th–20th c. · Two operations, compatible rules

Rings support addition and multiplication under axioms. Fields also permit division by every nonzero element.

Examples: Integers form a ring; rational, real and complex numbers form fields. Matrix rings generally have noncommutative multiplication.

Ideals & quotients: Ideals generalise divisibility and let new structures be built from old ones.

Builds from: Groups

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: ring (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: field (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Galois theory

known — established definition or theoremÉvariste Galois · early 1830s · Symmetries of equations

Galois theory relates field extensions to groups of symmetries. It explains when roots can be expressed using radicals.

Degree-five boundary: There is no universal radical formula for all quintic polynomials, although many particular quintics are solvable by radicals.

Broader impact: The ideas reach far beyond the original root-formula question into number theory and geometry.

Builds from: Polynomials, Groups, Rings & fields

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Galois theory (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · MacTutor: Évariste Galois (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Lie groups & representations

known — established definition or theoremSophus Lie · late 19th c. · Continuous symmetry

Lie groups combine smooth geometry and group structure. Representations describe their actions as linear transformations.

Examples: Rotations, unitary groups and Lorentz transformations provide continuous symmetries.

Physics: The Standard Model uses gauge symmetry, including SU(3), SU(2) and U(1); this mathematical structure does not alone derive the measured particle content.

Builds from: Groups, Manifolds, Linear algebra

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Lie group (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · David Tong: Lectures on the Standard Model (lecture notes / web page · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site unreachable on check day; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-02-17 · archived copy)

Topology

known — established definition or theorem19th–early 20th c. · Closeness without a ruler

Topology studies continuity, neighbourhoods and properties preserved by continuous deformation, without requiring a metric.

Open sets: A topology specifies which sets are open; the resulting space can support continuity even when distance is undefined.

Important warning: Topological equivalence is not literally "tearing or gluing allowed". Homeomorphisms are continuous bijections with continuous inverse.

Builds from: Set theory, Euclidean geometry

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: general topology (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Munkres: Topology (Pearson textbook) (database record · 2000 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (rechecked with a plain client: HTTP 200))

Manifolds

known — established definition or theoremRiemann · 1854 & later formalism · Locally like flat space

A manifold is modelled locally on Euclidean space. It can be curved globally even though small coordinate patches look flat.

Charts & atlases: Coordinates describe patches. Smoothness comes from compatible transitions, not from one preferred coordinate grid.

Generalisation: Spheres, tori and many curved spaces are examples; manifolds may have any dimension.

Builds from: Topology, Coordinate geometry

Sources: MIT OpenCourseWare: Geometry of Manifolds (textbook · 2004 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: manifold (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Riemannian geometry

known — established definition or theoremRiemann · 1854 · Measure length on curved spaces

A Riemannian metric gives each tangent space an inner product that varies smoothly, enabling lengths, angles and curvature.

Relativity nuance: Spacetime in general relativity is Lorentzian, not Riemannian. It uses pseudo-Riemannian geometry, a generalisation with a different metric signature.

Cross-link: The map therefore labels the physics relationship "differential geometry → general relativity", not "Riemannian geometry is spacetime".

Builds from: Manifolds, Inner products

Sources: MIT OpenCourseWare: Geometry of Manifolds (textbook · 2004 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Riemannian geometry (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Sean Carroll: lecture notes on general relativity (preprint · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Curvature

known — established definition or theoremGauss & Riemann · 19th c. · Geometry that need not be flat

Curvature measures how a surface or space departs from flatness, and can be intrinsic rather than depending on an outside space.

Parallel transport: Moving a vector consistently around a curved space can change its direction after a loop.

General relativity: Curvature of spacetime enters Einstein's field equations; matter and energy are related to that curvature.

Builds from: Riemannian geometry, Derivatives

Sources: MIT OpenCourseWare: Geometry of Manifolds (textbook · 2004 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Sean Carroll: lecture notes on general relativity (preprint · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Knot theory

known — established definition or theorem19th c. → modern topology · Loops that cannot be untied

Mathematical knots are closed loops in space, studied up to continuous deformation of the surrounding space.

Invariants: Polynomials and other invariants can distinguish some knots, but a single invariant usually does not classify every knot.

Applications: Connections include topology, quantum field theory and molecular biology; not every proposed physical application is established.

Builds from: Topology

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: knot theory (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Adams: The Knot Book (AMS) (book / historical text · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive) · archived copy)

Poincaré conjecture

known — solved theoremPerelman · 2002–03; recognised by Clay · A three-dimensional sphere test — solved

Every simply connected, closed three-manifold is homeomorphic to the three-sphere.

Proof route: Perelman used Hamilton's Ricci-flow programme, with results announced in 2002 and 2003.

Bigger result: The work also proves Thurston's geometrisation conjecture, which has the Poincaré conjecture as a special case.

Status: Clay recognises this as the solved Millennium Prize problem. Checked 6 Nov 2026.

Builds from: Topology, Manifolds

Sources: Clay: Poincaré Conjecture (solved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Perelman: The entropy formula for the Ricci flow (2002) (preprint · 2002 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Combinatorics

known — established definition or theoremAncient → modern · Counting structured possibilities

Combinatorics counts, arranges and analyses discrete structures without necessarily listing every possibility.

Examples: Permutations, combinations, binomial coefficients, inclusion–exclusion and recurrence relations answer different counting questions.

Exact vs asymptotic: An exact count and a large-size estimate are different, both valuable, kinds of result.

Builds from: Counting & number, Algebra

Sources: Levin: Discrete Mathematics — an Open Introduction (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: combinatorial analysis (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Graph theory

known — established definition or theoremEuler · 1736 (Königsberg) · Things and their connections

A graph has vertices and edges. It models networks, paths, colourings and connectivity independently of its physical layout.

Simple definitions vary: Some definitions allow loops or multiple edges; an exact problem statement must say which graphs are allowed.

Algorithms: Searching, shortest paths, flow and matching combine structural theory with computation.

Builds from: Combinatorics, Set theory

Sources: Euler: Solutio problematis ad geometriam situs pertinentis (1736; Stony Brook translation of the original paper) (paper (archived copy) · 1736 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Levin: Discrete Mathematics — an Open Introduction (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Trees & spanning structures

known — established definition or theoremGraph theory · 19th c. onward · Connected without cycles

A finite tree is a connected acyclic graph. Between any two vertices is exactly one path.

Equivalent viewpoint: A finite connected graph with n vertices and n−1 edges is a tree.

Map analogy: The maths tree is a curated learning map, not a literal classification of every theorem or field of mathematics.

Builds from: Graph theory

Sources: Levin: Discrete Mathematics — an Open Introduction (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: tree (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Four-colour theorem

known — solved theoremAppel & Haken · 1976 · Map regions need only four

Every planar map can be coloured with four colours so neighbouring regions do not share a colour.

Precise assumptions: Regions must be contiguous, with finite border requirements as formalised in graph theory. Countries with disconnected pieces are a different problem.

Computer-assisted proof: The proof was an influential landmark for computer checking in mathematics, not a mere exhaustive drawing of every map.

Builds from: Graph theory

Sources: Appel & Haken: Every planar map is four colorable, Part I (1977) (paper · 1977 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopaedia Britannica: four-colour map problem (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-11-01) · archived copy)

Ramsey theory

known — established definition or theoremFrank Ramsey · 1930 · Order inside enough disorder

Ramsey theory asks when sufficiently large structures must contain specified ordered substructures.

Famous example: At a party of six people, some three people are mutual acquaintances or mutual strangers, for the standard complete-graph two-colouring version.

Still active: Many exact Ramsey numbers remain unknown despite the existence theorems being proved.

Builds from: Graph theory, Combinatorics

Sources: Ramsey: On a Problem of Formal Logic (1930; Oxford Academic) (paper · 1930 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive) · archived copy) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Ramsey theorem (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Optimisation

known — established definition or theoremCalculus → operations research · Best choice under constraints

Optimisation seeks an allowed input that minimises or maximises a specified objective.

Local ≠ global: A stationary point is not necessarily a global optimum; convexity can supply stronger guarantees.

Discrete & continuous: Linear programming, convex optimisation and combinatorial optimisation have different tools and limits.

Builds from: Derivatives, Linear algebra, Graph theory

Sources: Boyd & Vandenberghe: Convex Optimization (free author copy) (lecture notes / web page · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenStax: Calculus, Volume 1 (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Fourier analysis

known — established definition or theoremJoseph Fourier · early 19th c. · Waves built from simpler waves

Fourier methods represent functions using oscillations or frequency components. They connect signals, heat, probability and differential equations.

Convergence is nuanced: Pointwise, uniform and mean-square convergence are different; an infinite trigonometric series does not automatically equal its originating function everywhere.

Transforms: Fourier transforms can turn differentiation into multiplication, revealing frequency structure and simplifying some equations.

Builds from: Trigonometry, Sequences & series, Integrals

Sources: MacTutor: Joseph Fourier (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Fourier series (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Complex analysis

known — established definition or theoremCauchy, Riemann, Weierstrass · 19th c. · Differentiation in the complex plane

Complex differentiability has much stronger consequences than real differentiability. Analytic functions are deeply linked to contour integration.

Cauchy theory: Under stated conditions, contour integrals relate to enclosed singularities through residues.

Connections: Analytic continuation, entire functions and poles are essential tools in number theory and mathematical physics.

Builds from: Complex numbers, Derivatives

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: analytic function (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · ComplexAnalysis.org: a free complex-analysis course (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Zeta & L-functions

known — established definition or theoremEuler & Riemann · 18th–19th c. · Analysis encodes arithmetic

Dirichlet series associate analytic functions with arithmetic sequences. The Riemann zeta function is a central example.

Euler product: For Re(s)>1, ζ(s)=Σ1/nˢ equals a product over primes. This is one precise link between primes and analysis.

Analytic continuation: The zeta function extends beyond the original series domain, but the series itself does not converge everywhere.

Builds from: Complex analysis, Prime numbers, Sequences & series

Sources: Clay: Riemann Hypothesis (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: zeta-function (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Elliptic curves

known — established definition or theoremDiophantus → modern arithmetic geometry · Cubic equations with group structure

Elliptic curves are smooth cubic curves with a specified base point and a geometric group law. Over different fields they have different behaviours.

Arithmetic: Their rational points connect number theory, algebra and complex analysis. They are not the same thing as ellipses.

Cryptography: Finite-field elliptic curves support cryptographic protocols; their security depends on implementation and computational assumptions.

Builds from: Polynomials, Groups, Complex analysis

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: elliptic curve (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Clay: Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer Conjecture (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer

unknown — open problemBirch & Swinnerton-Dyer · 1960s · Rational points from an L-function?

The conjecture relates the rank of an elliptic curve's rational points to the order of vanishing of its L-function at s=1.

A bridge: It links a discrete arithmetic invariant to an analytic object. Important special cases are proved; the full conjecture is not.

Status: Clay lists the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer Conjecture as unsolved, checked 6 Nov 2026.

Builds from: Elliptic curves, Zeta & L-functions

Sources: Clay: Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer Conjecture (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Clay Mathematics Institute: Millennium Prize Problems (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Algebraic geometry

known — established definition or theoremClassical geometry → Grothendieck school · Geometry of polynomial solutions

Algebraic geometry studies solution sets of polynomial equations using algebra, geometry and modern structural languages.

Fields matter: Solutions over the complex numbers, real numbers, rational numbers and finite fields can behave quite differently.

Modern language: Varieties, ideals, schemes and sheaves are successive tools; the map only introduces the landscape.

Builds from: Polynomials, Manifolds, Rings & fields

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: algebraic geometry (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · The Stacks Project: open algebraic-geometry reference (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Hodge conjecture

unknown — open problemW. V. D. Hodge · mid-20th c. · Topology from algebraic cycles?

For a smooth complex projective variety, the conjecture characterises certain rational cohomology classes as rational combinations of classes of algebraic cycles.

Not all topology: It does not claim every feature of every space comes from algebraic equations. Smooth projective complex varieties and Hodge classes matter.

Known cases ≠ general proof: Some special cases are established. Clay lists the general conjecture as unsolved, checked 6 Nov 2026.

Builds from: Algebraic geometry, Topology, Complex analysis

Sources: Clay: Hodge Conjecture (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Clay Mathematics Institute: Millennium Prize Problems (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Category theory

known — established definition or theoremEilenberg & Mac Lane · 1940s · Objects + arrows + composition

Category theory studies objects through their relationships, with arrows that compose associatively and identities at each object.

Structure-preserving maps: Examples include sets with functions, groups with homomorphisms, and topological spaces with continuous maps.

Not just abstraction: Functors, natural transformations and universal properties reveal shared patterns across fields without claiming all objects are the same.

Builds from: Set theory, Groups

Sources: Mac Lane: Categories for the Working Mathematician (Springer) (paper · 1978 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · nLab: category — definition and examples (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Topos & categorical logic

known — established definition or theoremGrothendieck & Lawvere · 20th c. · A setting for variable sets

A topos shares useful properties with the category of sets and can interpret a form of higher-order logic.

Different logic: A general topos need not obey classical logic or the axiom of choice. One should not assume every set-theoretic rule transfers unchanged.

Applications: The language appears in algebraic geometry, topology and foundations. This card is a pointer, not a complete definition.

Builds from: Category theory, Formal logic, Set theory

Sources: Mac Lane & Moerdijk: Sheaves in Geometry and Logic (Springer) (paper · 1994 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · nLab: topos — definition and context (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Homotopy & higher structure

known — established definition or theoremPoincaré → modern homotopy theory · Deform paths and maps

Homotopy compares maps by continuous deformation. Homotopy groups record holes and higher-dimensional structure.

Fundamental group: Loops based at a point, up to homotopy, form a group under composition.

Frontier: Higher-category and homotopy-invariant methods are active tools across topology, algebra and mathematical physics; this map introduces them only as established frameworks.

Builds from: Topology, Category theory

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: homotopy group (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Hatcher: Algebraic Topology (author’s free text) (lecture notes / web page · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Algorithms

known — established definition or theoremAncient procedures → modern theory · Finite procedures for a task

An algorithm is an effective procedure with explicit input, steps and output. Correctness and resource use require separate analysis.

Not just code: Euclidean division is an algorithm; a Turing machine is one formal model for expressing algorithms.

Analysis: Worst-case, average-case and amortised bounds can differ. A fast empirical test does not replace a complexity statement.

Builds from: Computability, Graph theory

Sources: Levin: Discrete Mathematics — an Open Introduction (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Cormen, Leiserson, Rivest & Stein: Introduction to Algorithms (book / historical text · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-10-27) · archived copy)

Computational complexity

known — established definition or theoremFormal theory · 1960s onward · Resources needed to solve

Complexity theory classifies problems by resources such as time, memory or communication, as input size grows.

Problems, not programs: The target is intrinsic resource requirements under a model, not whether one implementation seems fast.

Asymptotic notation: Big-O describes an upper growth bound, not an exact runtime or a guarantee for small inputs.

Builds from: Algorithms

Sources: Cook: The Complexity of Theorem-Proving Procedures (1971) (paper · 1971 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Arora & Barak: Computational Complexity (author page) (lecture notes / web page · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

P versus NP

unknown — open problemCook & Levin · early 1970s · Easy to check means easy to solve?

P is efficiently solvable decision problems; NP is decision problems with efficiently verifiable certificates. Does P = NP?

Not a speed benchmark: "Efficient" has a precise polynomial-time meaning here. NP does not stand for "not polynomial".

Consequences: If one NP-complete problem has a polynomial-time algorithm then all NP problems do. No accepted general resolution is known.

Status: Clay lists P vs NP as unsolved, checked 6 Nov 2026.

Builds from: Computational complexity, Computability

Sources: Clay: P versus NP (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Cook: The Complexity of Theorem-Proving Procedures (1971) (paper · 1971 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

Cryptography

known — established definition or theoremClassical ciphers → modern proofs · Security from hard structure

Modern cryptography designs protocols with explicit security goals, adversary models and computational assumptions.

Public key: Some systems depend on the presumed difficulty of computational problems. That is not the same as proving those problems hard.

Quantum impact: Shor's algorithm would break widely used factoring and discrete-log schemes on a sufficiently capable fault-tolerant quantum computer; that machine is not assumed to exist here.

Builds from: Modular arithmetic, Computational complexity, Elliptic curves

Sources: Katz & Lindell: Introduction to Modern Cryptography (lecture notes / web page · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Shor: Polynomial-Time Algorithms for Prime Factorization and Discrete Logarithms (paper · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · NIST: post-quantum cryptography standardisation (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Quantum information

known — established definition or theoremLate 20th c. → present · Bits become quantum states

Quantum information studies storage, transmission and processing using quantum states, measurement and entanglement.

Different resource: Qubits are described in Hilbert spaces. Measurement outcomes are probabilistic under the rules of quantum mechanics.

Quantum entropy: Von Neumann entropy is the quantum analogue for density operators. It has different operational meanings from ordinary Shannon entropy.

Builds from: Hilbert spaces, Information & entropy, Computability

Sources: Nielsen & Chuang: Quantum Computation and Quantum Information (book / historical text · 2010 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: quantum information (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

Numerical analysis

known — established definition or theoremAncient approximation → modern computing · Approximate answers reliably

Numerical analysis designs algorithms for approximation and studies accuracy, stability, convergence and computational cost.

Rounding: Floating-point arithmetic is not exact real arithmetic. More digits can help but do not automatically cure an unstable method.

Verification: Error bounds, conditioning and independent tests matter more than an impressive number of displayed decimal places.

Builds from: Derivatives, Linear algebra, Algorithms

Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: numerical analysis (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Burden & Faires: Numerical Analysis (Cengage) (book / historical text · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Yang–Mills & the mass gap

unknown — open problemMillennium formulation · 2000 · Make quantum gauge theory rigorous

The problem asks for a mathematically rigorous quantum Yang–Mills theory on four-dimensional space with a positive mass gap and established axioms.

Physics vs mathematics: Quantum gauge theories are enormously successful physics. That does not amount to the rigorous construction required by this mathematical problem.

Status: Clay lists Yang–Mills & the Mass Gap as unsolved, checked 6 Nov 2026.

Builds from: Groups, Partial differential equations, Quantum information

Sources: Clay: Yang–Mills & the Mass Gap (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Clay Mathematics Institute: Millennium Prize Problems (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Navier–Stokes: 3D smoothness

we think — partial or provisional claimed progressAnnounced 8 Sep 2026 · checked 6 Nov 2026 · Claimed breakthrough; checking continues

The general Millennium problem concerns existence, uniqueness and smoothness or breakdown of 3D Navier–Stokes solutions under precise formulations.

New claim: An AI-generated proof was announced on 8 Sep 2026, claiming singular behaviour in a particular forced formulation. Clay described the problem on 11 Sep as "apparently settled" and said the assessment would be unhurried.

Why not green: Later expert reporting and a preprint question whether the Lean formalisation faithfully represents the natural-language proof. The announcement is not treated here as independent confirmation of the full original problem.

Orange = provisional: This node records an important claimed result under scrutiny, not a settled solution or a settled failure. No prize or certainty is inferred.

Builds from: Partial differential equations, Numerical analysis

Sources: Clay: Navier–Stokes announcement (11 Sep 2026) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · OpenAI: On the Navier–Stokes Millennium Prize Problem (8 Sep 2026; announcement, not independent validation) (news / announcement · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-11-04) · archived copy) · Preprint: Navier–Stokes lost in translation (8 Oct 2026) (preprint · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · King’s College London: questions about the announced Navier–Stokes proof and its formalisation (news / announcement · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Kepler conjecture

known — solved theoremHales & Ferguson · published 2005–06 · The densest sphere packing — solved

No packing of equal spheres in ordinary three-dimensional space has greater density than the face-centred cubic arrangement.

Density: The maximum density is π/√18, about 74%. The proof combines geometry with extensive computation.

Formal checking: The Flyspeck project later produced a formal proof of the result. Publication and formal proof are related but distinct milestones.

Builds from: Manifolds, Optimisation

Sources: Hales: A proof of the Kepler conjecture (Annals, 2005) (paper · 2005 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Hales et al.: A formal proof of the Kepler conjecture (2017) (paper · 2017 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Maths History: dated landmarks

A selected dated landmark for every map node. A publication date is not always an invention or discovery date. Broad subjects retain a landmark or approximate range; the chart does not measure completeness or certify priority between simultaneous contributors. Scale: Non-linear proportional time within eras; cumulative selected publication/proposal milestones, not completeness Violet/pink in the interactive timeline indicates a proposed idea; circle markers indicate publication/proof landmarks. Historical approximate dates and broad field development are written explicitly.

ANCIENT RECORDS · c. 20,000 BCE → 400 CE

10 selected dated milestones; cumulative 10 / 92. Timelines are proportional time within separately sized eras, not linear global centuries.

  1. Counting & number · c. 20,000 BCE (fact; approximate)

    Ishango bone: an approximate, debated record associated with early counting — not a claimed first mathematical act.

    Sources: Ishango bone — historical overview and alternative interpretations (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  2. Place-value notation · c. 1800 BCE (fact; approximate)

    Old Babylonian place-value systems are a documented landmark. Indian and other decimal traditions are not collapsed into this one date.

    Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Arabic mathematics and al-Khwārizmī (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  3. Pythagoras’ theorem · c. 1800 BCE · selected tradition (fact; approximate)

    Ancient mathematical traditions used right-triangle relationships. This is not a claimed discovery year assigned to Pythagoras.

    Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Pythagoras in context — Britannica (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-10-13) · archived copy)

  4. Fractions & ratio · c. 1800 BCE · ancient fractions (fact; approximate)

    Ancient arithmetic records include fractions and ratios. The modern number concept evolved across cultures.

    Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  5. Trigonometry · c. 5th century BCE → medieval astronomy (fact; approximate)

    Trigonometry developed through Greek, Indian, Islamic and other astronomical traditions. No single inventor/date is assigned.

    Sources: MacTutor: history of trigonometry (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  6. Euclidean geometry · c. 300 BCE (fact; approximate)

    Euclid’s Elements — an approximate traditional date, not a certificate that all its ideas originated with Euclid.

    Sources: MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  7. Prime numbers · c. 300 BCE (fact; approximate)

    Euclid’s proof that there are infinitely many primes is a classical anchor for prime-number mathematics.

    Sources: MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  8. Proof & deduction · c. 300 BCE (fact; approximate)

    Euclid’s Elements becomes a durable model of deductive proof. Logical inquiry already had many cultural and historical roots.

    Sources: MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  9. Algorithms · c. 300 BCE (fact; approximate)

    Euclid’s algorithm for greatest common divisors supplies the landmark; the later word “algorithm” has a separate etymology.

    Sources: MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Arabic mathematics and al-Khwārizmī (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  10. Number theory · c. 300 BCE · Euclid; Gauss 1801 · modern number theory (fact; approximate)

    Euclid provides an ancient landmark; Gauss’s 1801 Disquisitiones is a separate modern systematic landmark.

    Sources: MacTutor: Euclid and the Elements (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Gauss — Disquisitiones Arithmeticae (book / historical text · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

TRANSMISSION & SYMBOLS · c. 400 → 1600

3 selected dated milestones; cumulative 13 / 92. Timelines are proportional time within separately sized eras, not linear global centuries.

  1. Zero & negatives · 628 (fact)

    Brahmagupta gives arithmetic rules for zero as a number.

    Sources: Brahmagupta and zero — MacTutor (history reference · 2000 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  2. Algebra · c. 820 (fact; approximate)

    Al-Khwarizmi’s algebra treatise; the date is approximate. Algebra has multiple textual traditions and later developments.

    Sources: Al-Khwarizmi — MacTutor (history reference · 1999 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  3. Polynomials · 1545 (fact)

    Cardano publishes Ars Magna, including cubic and quartic solution methods, amid a long older history of polynomial problems.

    Sources: Cardano: Ars Magna (1545) — digitised original (book / historical text · 1545 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

METHODS & MOTION · 1600 → 1800

15 selected dated milestones; cumulative 28 / 92. Timelines are proportional time within separately sized eras, not linear global centuries.

  1. Kepler conjecture · 1611 (idea)

    Johannes Kepler proposes the sphere-packing conjecture.

    Sources: Hales: A proof of the Kepler conjecture (Annals, 2005) (paper · 2005 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  2. Fermat’s Last Theorem · 1637 (idea)

    Fermat records his famous assertion in the margin.

    Sources: Wiles: Modular elliptic curves and Fermat’s Last Theorem (1995) (paper · 1995 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  3. Coordinate geometry · 1637 (fact)

    Descartes publishes La Géométrie; Fermat’s related work circulated in the same period.

    Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  4. Arithmetic · 16th–17th-century notation · arithmetic older (fact; approximate)

    This node uses an approximate symbolic-notation range, not an invention date for arithmetic itself.

    Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  5. Probability · 1654 (fact)

    Fermat and Pascal’s correspondence on the problem of points is a landmark in probability theory.

    Sources: Fermat–Pascal correspondence (1654) — source transcription (paper (archived copy) · 1654 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  6. Combinatorics · 1654 · Pascal–Fermat correspondence; broader combinatorics older (fact)

    This paper’s selected combinatorics anchor is the Pascal–Fermat problem-of-points correspondence. Discrete enumeration itself has older traditions.

    Sources: Fermat–Pascal correspondence (1654) — source transcription (paper (archived copy) · 1654 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: combinatorial analysis (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

  7. Equations & functions · 1673 (fact)

    Leibniz’s term “function” is a notation/concept landmark. Functions evolved beyond that early use.

    Sources: MacTutor: an overview of the history of mathematics (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  8. Derivatives · 1684 · Newton/Leibniz wider development (fact)

    Leibniz’s printed differential-calculus work is the selected anchor; Newton’s work and notation are separate parts of the history.

    Sources: MacTutor: the rise of calculus (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  9. Complex numbers · 1685–1698 (fact)

    Wallis presents a geometric representation of complex numbers. A landmark, not the final modern theory.

    Sources: MacTutor: the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra and complex numbers (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  10. Integrals · 1686 · later integral theories follow (fact)

    This is the early-calculus publication anchor, not a claim that all integrals date from Leibniz’s paper. Riemann and Lebesgue milestones are later.

    Sources: MacTutor: the rise of calculus (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  11. Graph theory · 1736 (fact)

    Euler publishes his Königsberg bridge problem analysis.

    Sources: Euler: Solutio problematis ad geometriam situs pertinentis (1736; Stony Brook translation of the original paper) (paper (archived copy) · 1736 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  12. Goldbach’s conjecture · 1742 (idea)

    Christian Goldbach and Leonhard Euler’s correspondence gives the statement now associated with Goldbach’s name.

    Sources: MacTutor: Christian Goldbach (history reference · 2006 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  13. Differential equations · 1747 (fact)

    D’Alembert’s vibrating-string work is a dated differential-equation example. Ordinary differential equations arose through wider calculus work.

    Sources: MacTutor: Jean d’Alembert (1747 vibrating-string work) (history reference · 1998 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: ordinary differential equations (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

  14. Partial differential equations · 1747 (fact)

    D’Alembert publishes his vibrating-string analysis containing the wave equation; the full subject develops much later.

    Sources: MacTutor: Jean d’Alembert (1747 vibrating-string work) (history reference · 1998 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  15. Conditional probability & Bayes · 1763 (fact)

    Thomas Bayes’s essay is published posthumously by Richard Price.

    Sources: Bayes and Price (1763) — Royal Society original (paper · 1763 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

STRUCTURE & RIGOUR · 1800 → 1900

30 selected dated milestones; cumulative 58 / 92. Timelines are proportional time within separately sized eras, not linear global centuries.

  1. Modular arithmetic · 1801 (fact)

    Gauss’s Disquisitiones gives a systematic treatment of congruences and arithmetic residues.

    Sources: Gauss — Disquisitiones Arithmeticae (book / historical text · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: Carl Friedrich Gauss (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  2. Fourier analysis · early 19th century · 1807/1822 (fact)

    Fourier’s work on heat developed through a memoir and later 1822 publication. The range makes the chronology explicit.

    Sources: MacTutor: Joseph Fourier (history reference · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  3. Limits & continuity · 1815–1821 (fact)

    Cauchy’s approach to rigorous limits is a historical progression, culminating here in the 1821 Cours d’analyse.

    Sources: Cauchy’s Calculus (1821) — MacTutor (history reference · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  4. Sequences & series · 1821 (fact)

    Cauchy’s Cours d’analyse is a landmark in rigorous convergence and series theory.

    Sources: Cauchy’s Calculus (1821) — MacTutor (history reference · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  5. Complex analysis · 1821 (fact)

    Cauchy’s rigorous calculus work is a publication anchor for the later theory of complex analysis, not the end of the story.

    Sources: Cauchy’s Calculus (1821) — MacTutor (history reference · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  6. Curvature · 1825–1828 survey; 1854 Riemann follows (fact)

    Gauss’s investigations of curved surfaces are a landmark. Riemann’s 1854 geometry lecture is a separate anchor.

    Sources: Gauss: General Investigations of Curved Surfaces — translation with original context (paper (archived copy) · 1827 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  7. Vector calculus · 1828 (fact)

    Green’s mathematical-analysis essay is a dated landmark in the history of integral field theorems.

    Sources: George Green’s 1828 essay (book / historical text · 1828 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive) · archived copy)

  8. Non-Euclidean geometry · 1829 (fact)

    Lobachevsky publishes non-Euclidean geometry in 1829; Bolyai follows in 1832.

    Sources: MacTutor: non-Euclidean geometry (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: non-Euclidean geometry (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  9. Groups · 1832 · manuscript; 1846 published (fact)

    Galois leaves his group-theoretic ideas in a manuscript in 1832; Liouville publishes Galois’s work in 1846.

    Sources: MacTutor: Évariste Galois (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  10. Galois theory · 1832 · Galois manuscript; published 1846 (fact)

    Galois’s manuscript is dated before his death; Liouville’s 1846 publication is a separate dissemination milestone.

    Sources: MacTutor: Évariste Galois (history reference · 1996 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  11. Zeta & L-functions · 1737 → 1837 → 1859 (fact)

    Euler’s zeta product, Dirichlet’s 1837 L-functions, and Riemann’s 1859 paper are separate landmarks for this broad node.

    Sources: A history of L-functions — LMFDB, reviewed (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Clay: Riemann Hypothesis (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  12. Vectors · 1844 (fact)

    Grassmann’s Ausdehnungslehre is a landmark in the development of vector-space ideas, not the invention of every vector notion.

    Sources: Grassmann’s Ausdehnungslehre (1844) — Deutsches Textarchiv (book / historical text · 1844 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  13. Linear algebra · 1844 (fact)

    Grassmann’s extension theory is a landmark for abstract vector-space ideas, not their only possible origin.

    Sources: Grassmann’s Ausdehnungslehre (1844) — Deutsches Textarchiv (book / historical text · 1844 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Axler: Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition (free author copy) (textbook · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  14. Navier–Stokes: 3D smoothness · 1822 → 1845 (fact)

    Navier and Stokes contribute to the equations; these are publication dates.

    Sources: Clay: Navier–Stokes announcement (11 Sep 2026) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  15. Trees & spanning structures · 1847–1857 (fact)

    Kirchhoff’s circuit-paper tree ideas and Cayley’s tree terminology are linked but distinct nineteenth-century milestones.

    Sources: Kirchhoff (1847) — original paper on circuit equations (paper · 1847 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

  16. Twin prime conjecture · 1849 (idea)

    Alphonse de Polignac publishes a more general prime-gap conjecture that includes infinitely many prime pairs differing by two.

    Sources: Polignac’s conjecture (1849): overview and original reference (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  17. Lie groups & representations · 1850–1920 (fact; approximate)

    Lie-group and representation theory are treated as parts of a broader development, not as simultaneous discoveries.

    Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Lie group (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

  18. Four-colour theorem · 1852 (idea)

    Francis Guthrie poses the four-colour question.

    Sources: Encyclopaedia Britannica: four-colour map problem (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-11-01) · archived copy)

  19. Riemannian geometry · 1854 (fact)

    Riemann’s geometry lecture is the historical anchor for the card.

    Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Riemannian geometry (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

  20. Manifolds · 1854 (fact)

    Riemann’s geometry lecture supplies the map’s historical anchor for manifold ideas.

    Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Riemannian geometry (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

  21. Matrices & linear maps · 1858 (fact)

    Cayley publishes A Memoir on the Theory of Matrices.

    Sources: Axler: Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition (free author copy) (textbook · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  22. Eigenvalues & spectra · 1858 (fact)

    Cayley’s matrix memoir includes the characteristic-equation result named for Cayley and Hamilton.

    Sources: Axler: Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition (free author copy) (textbook · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  23. Riemann hypothesis · 1859 (idea)

    Bernhard Riemann publishes the hypothesis about the nontrivial zeros of the zeta function.

    Sources: Clay: Riemann Hypothesis (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Riemann hypothesis — Clay Mathematics Institute (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  24. Real numbers · 1872 (fact)

    Dedekind publishes a rigorous construction of real numbers using cuts.

    Sources: Dedekind (1872) — continuity and irrational numbers (in Essays on the Theory of Numbers, Project Gutenberg) (book / historical text · 1872 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · MacTutor: the real numbers and their history (history reference · 2005 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  25. Set theory · 1874 (fact)

    Cantor’s first set-theory paper establishes a landmark in the theory of infinite sets.

    Sources: Cantor (1874) — original journal record (paper · 1874 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  26. Knot theory · 1877 (fact)

    Tait’s On Knots is the map’s dated knot-table landmark, not a claim that knots had never been studied before.

    Sources: Tait — Scientific Papers (includes On Knots) (paper (archived copy) · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  27. Formal logic · 1879 (fact)

    Frege publishes Begriffsschrift. Aristotle is not replaced as a historical root by this publication milestone.

    Sources: Frege, Gottlob and the Formula Language of Pure Thinking — MacTutor (history reference · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: classical logic (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  28. Sizes of infinity · 1891–1892 (fact; approximate)

    Cantor’s diagonal argument is presented and appears in a volume dated 1890–91, later printed. The range preserves that bibliographic nuance.

    Sources: Cantor’s diagonal argument — original text and translation (book / historical text · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  29. Topology · 1895 (fact)

    Poincaré’s Analysis Situs is a foundational landmark in algebraic topology.

    Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: general topology (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Hatcher: Algebraic Topology (author’s free text) (lecture notes / web page · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  30. Homotopy & higher structure · 1895 (fact)

    Poincaré’s fundamental-group and topology work is a landmark; higher homotopy theory developed later.

    Sources: Poincaré conjecture: 1904 proposal; 2002–03 announcement — Clay (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopedia of Mathematics: homotopy group (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

MODERN FRONTIERS · 1900 → today

34 selected dated milestones; cumulative 92 / 92. Timelines are proportional time within separately sized eras, not linear global centuries.

  1. Numerical analysis · 1900–1950 (fact; approximate)

    Numerical analysis is a broad development, not a single dated invention.

    Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: numerical analysis (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

  2. Statistics & inference · 1900 (fact)

    Pearson’s chi-squared goodness-of-fit paper is a landmark for this node, not a claimed origin of all statistics.

    Sources: Pearson (1900) — journal DOI (paper · 1900 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax: Introductory Statistics (textbook · 2013 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  3. Poincaré conjecture · 1904 (idea)

    Henri Poincaré poses the conjecture in his fifth complement to Analysis Situs.

    Sources: Poincaré conjecture: 1904 proposal; 2002–03 announcement — Clay (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  4. Hilbert spaces · 1904–1910 (fact)

    Hilbert’s integral-equation papers are a dated landmark in Hilbert-space methods; physical interpretation comes from later quantum mechanics.

    Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Hilbert space (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · MIT OpenCourseWare: Quantum Theory I — mathematical formalism (textbook · 2017 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  5. Inner products · 1904–1910 (fact)

    The Hilbert-school work supplies a landmark for infinite-dimensional inner-product methods; finite-dimensional geometry is older.

    Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: Hilbert space (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy)

  6. Axioms & foundations · 1908 → 1922 (fact)

    Zermelo’s axiomatisation (1908); Fraenkel and Skolem’s contributions (1922) lead to ZFC.

    Sources: Zermelo (1908) — journal source (paper · 1908 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: set theory (encyclopedia · 2023 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  7. Rings & fields · 1914 · ideal-theory landmark: 1921 (fact)

    Fraenkel’s abstract-ring paper is a publication milestone. The card can be read without treating ring theory as beginning there.

    Sources: From Numbers to Rings: The Early History of Ring Theory (paper · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  8. Ramsey theory · 1930 (fact)

    Frank Ramsey publishes On a Problem of Formal Logic.

    Sources: Ramsey: On a Problem of Formal Logic (1930; Oxford Academic) (paper · 1930 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive) · archived copy)

  9. Gödel’s incompleteness · 1931 (fact)

    Gödel’s incompleteness theorem is published.

    Sources: Stanford Encyclopedia: Gödel’s incompleteness theorems (encyclopedia · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  10. Random variables · 1933 (fact)

    Kolmogorov’s axioms are one dated formal anchor for randomness; philosophical and algorithmic definitions have other histories.

    Sources: Kolmogorov: Foundations of the Theory of Probability (English translation) (paper (archived copy) · 1933 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Kolmogorov: Foundations of the Theory of Probability (English translation) (paper (archived copy) · 1933 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  11. Computability · 1936 (fact)

    Turing and Church formalise computability independently in the 1930s.

    Sources: Turing: On Computable Numbers (1936–37) (paper (archived copy) · 1936 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Church (1936) — unsolvable problem (paper · 1936 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  12. Collatz conjecture · c. 1937 · first journal record traced to 1971 (idea; approximate)

    The Collatz problem circulated in the twentieth century and is often dated to 1937; the first journal appearance is later. The date is not presented as exact.

    Sources: Lagarias: The 3x+1 problem and its generalizations (1985) (lecture notes / web page · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  13. Category theory · 1945 (fact)

    Eilenberg and Mac Lane publish General Theory of Natural Equivalences.

    Sources: Mac Lane: Categories for the Working Mathematician (Springer) (paper · 1978 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  14. Optimisation · 1947 (fact)

    Dantzig develops linear programming and proposes the simplex method.

    Sources: Dantzig and the simplex method — INFORMS (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2024-09-10) · archived copy)

  15. Information & entropy · 1948 (fact)

    Shannon’s information-entropy formalism is published, with its thermodynamic context acknowledged in the card.

    Sources: Shannon: A Mathematical Theory of Communication (1948) (paper (archived copy) · 1948 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  16. Codes & compression · 1948 (fact)

    Shannon’s information theory establishes limits for compression and communication.

    Sources: Shannon: A Mathematical Theory of Communication (1948) (paper (archived copy) · 1948 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  17. Elliptic curves · 19th–20th century · selected landmark: modern arithmetic geometry (fact; approximate)

    Elliptic-curve arithmetic has many historical layers; this range marks their modern development without crediting all of them to one person.

    Sources: Encyclopedia of Mathematics: elliptic curve (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: site down on check day (HTTP 502); page confirmed in Web Archive · archived copy) · Clay: Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer Conjecture (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  18. Hodge conjecture · 1950 · proceedings published 1952 (idea)

    W. V. D. Hodge presents the conjecture at the International Congress of Mathematicians; its proceedings appear later.

    Sources: Clay: Hodge Conjecture (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  19. Error-correcting codes · 1950 (fact)

    Hamming’s Error Detecting and Error Correcting Codes is the selected published milestone.

    Sources: Hamming: Error Detecting and Error Correcting Codes (1950) (book / historical text · 1950 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  20. Yang–Mills & the mass gap · 1954 (fact)

    Yang and Mills publish their equations.

    Sources: Clay: Yang–Mills & the Mass Gap (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  21. Algebraic geometry · 1960 · after classical algebraic geometry (fact)

    Grothendieck and Dieudonné publish EGA I. This modern language does not erase earlier classical algebraic geometry.

    Sources: Grothendieck & Dieudonné: Éléments de géométrie algébrique I (1960) (paper · 1960 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · The Stacks Project: open algebraic-geometry reference (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  22. Dynamical systems & chaos · 1963 (fact)

    Lorenz publishes Deterministic nonperiodic flow. Poincaré and other earlier dynamical work remains acknowledged.

    Sources: Lorenz: Deterministic nonperiodic flow (1963) (paper · 1963 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

  23. Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer · 1963–1965 (idea)

    Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer develop and publish the conjecture as a range of work, not a single-day announcement.

    Sources: Clay: Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer Conjecture (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  24. Topos & categorical logic · 1960–1970 (fact)

    Grothendieck’s categorical geometry and Lawvere’s categorical-logic programme converge in the development of topos theory. It is a range, not a single-person invention.

    Sources: Mac Lane & Moerdijk: Sheaves in Geometry and Logic (Springer) (paper · 1994 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  25. P versus NP · 1971 (idea)

    Cook’s theorem gives a precise anchor for the P versus NP problem; independent Soviet work also matters.

    Sources: P versus NP problem — Clay Mathematics Institute (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  26. Computational complexity · 1971 · Cook; Levin independently (fact)

    Cook’s theorem is a landmark in computational-complexity theory; Levin’s independent formulation is also included.

    Sources: Cook: The Complexity of Theorem-Proving Procedures (1971) (paper · 1971 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · P versus NP problem — Clay Mathematics Institute (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  27. Four-colour theorem · 1976 (fact)

    Appel and Haken publish the computer-assisted proof. The original paper appeared in 1977; later formal verification is a separate milestone.

    Sources: Appel & Haken: Every planar map is four colorable, Part I (1977) (paper · 1977 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Encyclopaedia Britannica: four-colour map problem (encyclopedia · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok (site blocks automatic checks; page confirmed in Web Archive, snapshot 2026-11-01) · archived copy)

  28. Cryptography · 1978 (fact)

    RSA is published by Rivest, Shamir and Adleman. Cryptography itself and earlier public-key research are older.

    Sources: Rivest, Shamir and Adleman (1978) — original paper (paper · 1978 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

  29. Quantum information · 1994 (fact)

    Shor’s algorithm is a milestone for quantum information and computational complexity; not a completed large-scale quantum computer.

    Sources: Shor (1994): Algorithms for quantum computation (paper · 1994 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Shor: Polynomial-Time Algorithms for Prime Factorization and Discrete Logarithms (paper · 1997 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks))

  30. Fermat’s Last Theorem · 1995 (fact)

    Andrew Wiles and Richard Taylor publish the corrected proof. The 1993 announcement and 1994 correction are separate milestones.

    Sources: Wiles: Modular elliptic curves and Fermat’s Last Theorem (1995) (paper · 1995 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Taylor & Wiles: Ring-theoretic properties of Hecke algebras (1995) (paper · 1995 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  31. Navier–Stokes: 3D smoothness · 2000 (idea)
