    Clay formulates the existence-and-smoothness prize problem. Later claimed work must not be treated as accepted resolution.

    Sources: Clay: Navier–Stokes announcement (11 Sep 2026) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  32. Yang–Mills & the mass gap · 2000 (idea)

    Clay formulates the rigorous quantum Yang–Mills existence and mass-gap prize problem.

    Sources: Clay: Yang–Mills & the Mass Gap (unsolved) (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  33. Poincaré conjecture · 2002–2003 (fact)

    Grigori Perelman announces the proof in preprints posted in 2002 and 2003.

    Sources: Poincaré conjecture: 1904 proposal; 2002–03 announcement — Clay (institute · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

  34. Kepler conjecture · 1998 announced · 2005–2006 publication (fact)

    The proof announcement and its formal publication are separated. Flyspeck completed a formal proof later.

    Sources: Hales: A proof of the Kepler conjecture (Annals, 2005) (paper · 2005 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Hales et al.: A formal proof of the Kepler conjecture (2017) (paper · 2017 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Typical human cell (generalised)

we think

A general human body cell, about 20 µm across: it has the parts most human cells share.

No single real cell type looks exactly like this; the shape is simplified.

Real human cells range from about 7 µm (red blood cells) to over 100 µm, and from round to long and branched.

Sizes and speeds of the parts inside are real measured values, drawn to scale.

Some counts are reduced so you can see between things: proteins at ~1/8 of real crowding, 30 of ~10,000 vaults, fewer microtubules and ribosomes.

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.3 Eukaryotic Cells (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Cell (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Cell Biology by the Numbers (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Cell membrane

known

A skin of fat molecules two layers thick, about 5 nm.

It lets some things in and keeps others out.

Made of roughly a billion lipid molecules, with proteins floating in it.

In cells that crawl, the front edge ruffles and moves forward a few µm per minute (a lamellipodium).

Zoom right in to see the two layers of lipids.

Sources: Singer & Nicolson 1972: The Fluid Mosaic Model of the Structure of Cell Membranes (Science) (paper · 1972 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 3.1 The Cell Membrane (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Cell membrane (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Lamellipodium (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Nucleus

known

The control centre: about 10 × 8 µm here (typically 5–10 µm).

It holds 46 chromosomes: about 2 metres of DNA.

About 6.2 billion DNA letters (base pairs) in two copies of the genome.

Wrapped in a double membrane with a few thousand pores.

Under a microscope (🔬 button) it glows blue, like the DAPI dye biologists use to stain DNA.

Sources: International Human Genome Sequencing Consortium 2001: Initial sequencing and analysis of the human genome (Nature) (paper · 2001 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 3.3 The Nucleus and DNA Replication (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Cell nucleus (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Human genome (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Chromatin (DNA)

known

DNA wound on protein spools (nucleosomes).

Each spool holds 147 DNA letters, wrapped almost twice.

A nucleosome is about 11 nm wide and made of 8 histone proteins.

About 30 million nucleosomes pack the genome into the nucleus.

In living cells the beads fold into irregular chains 5–24 nm wide, not a neat uniform 30 nm fibre.

Sources: Ou et al. 2017: ChromEMT: Visualizing 3D chromatin structure and compaction in interphase and mitotic cells (Science) (paper · 2017 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 14.2 DNA Structure and Sequencing (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Nucleosome (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Nucleosome

known

A spool of 8 histone proteins with DNA wrapped around it.

About 11 nm wide.

147 base pairs wrap about 1.65 turns.

Linker DNA joins one spool to the next, like beads on a string.

Drawn simplified: DNA wrapped around histone beads. The two twisted strands are the 2 nm double helix; the wrapping around each bead is not drawn.

Sources: Luger et al. 1997: Crystal structure of the nucleosome core particle at 2.8 Å resolution (Nature) (paper · 1997 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Ou et al. 2017: ChromEMT: Visualizing 3D chromatin structure and compaction in interphase and mitotic cells (Science) (paper · 2017 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Nucleosome (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Nucleolus

known

A dense spot inside the nucleus, 1–3 µm.

It builds the parts of ribosomes.

Most human cells have 1–4. It has no membrane: it forms where ribosome genes cluster.

Ribosome pieces leave through the nuclear pores.

Sources: Boisvert et al. 2007: The multifunctional nucleolus (Nat Rev Mol Cell Biol) (review · 2007 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Nucleolus (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Nuclear pore

known

A gate in the nucleus wall, about 120 nm across.

mRNA goes out, proteins come in.

One of the biggest machines in the cell: ~1,000 proteins of ~30 kinds, in 8-fold symmetry.

A human nucleus has roughly 2,000–5,000 pores.

Sources: Kim et al. 2018: Integrative structure and functional anatomy of a nuclear pore complex (Nature) (paper · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Nuclear pore (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Ribosome

known

A protein-building machine, about 25–30 nm.

It reads mRNA and adds about 5–6 amino acids per second.

A human cell has millions (estimates around 10 million in fast-growing cells).

Two parts: a small (40S) and a large (60S) subunit.

At 1× speed you can watch the new chain grow at the real rate.

Sources: Ribosome (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · BioNumbers: translation rate (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Endoplasmic reticulum

known

A lace of tubes and sheets spreading through the cell.

Makes proteins and fats, stores calcium.

Rough ER (with ribosomes) sits near the nucleus; smooth tubes reach out to the edge.

Tubes are about 50–100 nm wide and keep moving and re-joining.

Sources: Palade 1975: Intracellular Aspects of the Process of Protein Synthesis (Science) (paper · 1975 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.4 The Endomembrane System and Proteins (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Endoplasmic reticulum (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Golgi apparatus

known

Stacks of flat sacs next to the nucleus: the post office.

It sorts, tags and packs proteins.

In human cells the stacks join into a ribbon of several µm near the centrosome.

Each stack has about 4–8 sacs (cisternae).

Cells that secrete a lot, like fibroblasts making collagen, have big Golgi.

Sources: Palade 1975: Intracellular Aspects of the Process of Protein Synthesis (Science) (paper · 1975 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.4 The Endomembrane System and Proteins (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Golgi apparatus (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Vesicle

known

A small bubble (50–100 nm) carrying cargo.

Motor proteins walk it along microtubules at about 1 µm per second.

Kinesin walks outwards (~0.8 µm/s), dynein inwards (~1 µm/s).

Off the track, a vesicle only jiggles slowly: the cytoplasm is very crowded.

Sources: Novick et al. 1980: Identification of 23 complementation groups required for post-translational events in the yeast secretory pathway (Cell) (paper · 1980 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.4 The Endomembrane System and Proteins (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Vesicle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Kinesin (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Mitochondrion

known

The power station: makes ATP.

A tube about 0.5 µm wide and 1–10 µm long; a human cell has hundreds.

They form a network that splits and joins every few minutes.

The folds inside (cristae) hold ATP synthase, a spinning motor.

It has its own small DNA: it came from a bacterium long ago.

Sources: Mitchell 1961: Coupling of Phosphorylation to Electron and Hydrogen Transfer by a Chemi-Osmotic type of Mechanism (Nature) (paper · 1961 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 7.4 Oxidative Phosphorylation (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Mitochondrion (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

ATP synthase

known

A rotary motor about 10 nm wide in the cristae.

It spins over 100 times a second to make ATP.

Protons flowing through it turn the rotor.

Each turn makes 3 ATP.

Sources: Abrahams et al. 1994: Structure at 2.8 Å resolution of F1-ATPase from bovine heart mitochondria (Nature) (paper · 1994 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Noji et al. 1997: Direct observation of the rotation of F1-ATPase (Nature) (paper · 1997 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · ATP synthase (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

ATP

known

The cell’s energy coin.

Made in mitochondria, spent everywhere.

A human body makes and recycles roughly its own weight in ATP every day.

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.4 ATP: Adenosine Triphosphate (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Adenosine triphosphate (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Lysosome

known

A recycling bag full of enzymes, 0.1–1 µm.

A cell has a few hundred.

Its inside is acidic (pH about 4.5–5); a pump uses ATP to push protons in.

Its enzymes work best in acid, so any that leak into the near-neutral cytoplasm (pH ~7.2) do little harm.

Sources: de Duve et al. 1955: Tissue fractionation studies. 6. Intracellular distribution patterns of enzymes in rat-liver tissue (Biochemical Journal) (paper · 1955 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.4 The Endomembrane System and Proteins (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Lysosome (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Peroxisome

known

A small bag (0.1–0.5 µm) that burns fats.

It makes and destroys hydrogen peroxide.

Human cells have a few hundred.

Also helps make some special fats for the brain.

Sources: De Duve & Baudhuin 1966: Peroxisomes (microbodies and related particles). (Physiological Reviews) (review · 1966 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Peroxisome (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Lipid droplet

known

A store of fat, 0.5–2 µm.

Wrapped in a single layer of lipids.

Cells use them as an energy store and to make membranes.

Sources: Walther & Farese 2012: Lipid Droplets and Cellular Lipid Metabolism (Annu. Rev. Biochem.) (review · 2012 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Lipid droplet (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Centrosome

known

Two tiny barrels (centrioles), 250 nm wide and 500 nm long.

Microtubules grow out from here.

Each barrel is 9 groups of 3 tubes.

In most human cells the older barrel also grows a primary cilium, a tiny antenna.

It helps pull chromosomes apart when the cell divides.

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.5 The Cytoskeleton (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Centrosome (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Microtubule

known

Hollow tubes 25 nm wide: roads and scaffolding.

Each tip grows ~12 µm/min, then suddenly shrinks.

This "dynamic instability" is simulated here with measured rates.

The bright tip is where new tubulin is added (biologists see it with EB1 tags).

Kinesin and dynein walk on them carrying cargo.

Sources: Nogales et al. 1998: Structure of the αβ tubulin dimer by electron crystallography (Nature) (paper · 1998 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.5 The Cytoskeleton (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Microtubule (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Dynamic instability (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Actin stress fibres

known

Bundles of actin filaments (each 7 nm) running across the cell.

They pull on the ground like ropes.

They end at focal adhesions, the cell’s "feet".

A single filament is two strands twisted, crossing every 37 nm.

Sources: Kabsch et al. 1990: Atomic structure of the actin: DNase I complex (Nature) (paper · 1990 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.5 The Cytoskeleton (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Stress fiber (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Microfilament (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Focal adhesion

known

A cell’s “foot” that grips the surface, 1–5 µm long.

Stress fibres pull on it.

Made of integrins and many linking proteins.

Feet at the front form and mature in minutes.

Sources: Geiger et al. 2001: Transmembrane crosstalk between the extracellular matrix and the cytoskeleton (Nat Rev Mol Cell Biol) (review · 2001 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Focal adhesion (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Vimentin filaments

known

Rope-like filaments, 10 nm wide.

They cushion the nucleus and help the cell resist stretching.

Vimentin is the main intermediate filament of many cell types (e.g. fibroblasts); others use keratins.

Sources: Herrmann et al. 2007: Intermediate filaments: from cell architecture to nanomechanics (Nat Rev Mol Cell Biol) (review · 2007 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.5 The Cytoskeleton (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Vimentin (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Vault

we think

A hollow barrel of protein and RNA, about 40 × 70 nm.

What it does is still not clear.

About 10,000 per cell (some cells have more).

Ideas include transport or defence, but no job is proven.

Sources: Kedersha & Rome 1986: Isolation and characterization of a novel ribonucleoprotein particle: large structures contain a single species of small RNA. (The Journal of cell biology) (paper · 1986 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Tanaka et al. 2009: The Structure of Rat Liver Vault at 3.5 Angstrom Resolution (Science) (paper · 2009 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Vault (organelle) (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Cytosol

known

The gel that fills the cell: about 70% water.

Packed with roughly 3 million proteins per cubic µm.

So crowded that big things barely drift unless motors carry them.

Small molecules still diffuse fast: across the cell in seconds.

Sources: Cytosol (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Cell Biology by the Numbers (textbook · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Water molecule

known

One oxygen atom and two hydrogen atoms (H₂O).

About 99 of every 100 molecules in a cell are water.

Real water molecules jiggle and tumble trillions of times a second; here they are shown frozen or hugely slowed.

Water’s slightly charged ends help proteins fold.

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 2.2 Water (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Properties of water (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Glucose

known

A sugar: a ring of carbon and oxygen.

Cells break it down for energy.

Glycolysis in the cytosol starts the job; mitochondria finish it and make ATP.

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 7.2 Glycolysis (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 3.2 Carbohydrates (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Glucose (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Phospholipid

known

A water-loving head and two oily tails, about 2.5 nm long.

Two layers of them make every membrane.

The tails hide from water, so lipids form two layers by themselves.

A kink in one tail keeps the membrane fluid.

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 3.3 Lipids (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Singer & Nicolson 1972: The Fluid Mosaic Model of the Structure of Cell Membranes (Science) (paper · 1972 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Phospholipid (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Base pair (DNA)

known

Two DNA letters joined in the middle.

Each side hangs on a sugar-phosphate backbone.

A pairs with T, G pairs with C, held by weak hydrogen bonds.

Pairs are stacked 0.34 nm apart along the helix.

Sources: Watson & Crick 1953: Molecular Structure of Nucleic Acids: A Structure for Deoxyribose Nucleic Acid (Nature) (paper · 1953 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 14.2 DNA Structure and Sequencing (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Base pair (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Protein chain

known

Amino acids linked in a chain by a ribosome.

The chain then folds into a working shape.

There are 20 kinds of amino acid; the gene sets their order.

The backbone repeats N–C–C; side chains stick out.

Sources: Pauling et al. 1951: The structure of proteins: Two hydrogen-bonded helical configurations of the polypeptide chain (Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. U.S.A.) (paper · 1951 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 3.4 Proteins (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Peptide (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Protein

known

A folded chain of thousands of atoms, typically 3–10 nm.

Proteins do most of the work in a cell.

Enzymes, motors, pumps and scaffolds are all proteins.

Shown as simple blobs: real shapes are far more detailed.

Sources: Milo 2013: What is the total number of protein molecules per cell volume? A call to rethink some published values (BioEssays) (paper · 2013 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 3.4 Proteins (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Protein (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Hydrogen atom

known

The smallest atom: 1 proton, 1 electron.

Made just after the Big Bang.

About 6 in 10 atoms in your body are hydrogen.

Sources: OpenStax Chemistry 2e, 2.3 Atomic Structure and Symbolism (textbook · 2019 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Hydrogen (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Carbon atom

known

6 protons. The backbone of life’s molecules.

Made inside stars.

Carbon can bond to four other atoms, so it builds chains and rings.

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 2.3 Carbon (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Carbon (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Nitrogen atom

known

7 protons.

Found in every amino acid and DNA letter.

Made inside stars (the CNO cycle).

Sources: OpenStax Chemistry 2e, 2.3 Atomic Structure and Symbolism (textbook · 2019 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Nitrogen (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Oxygen atom

known

8 protons.

In water, sugars and the phosphate groups of ATP.

By mass, most of your body is oxygen (in water).

Sources: OpenStax Chemistry 2e, 2.3 Atomic Structure and Symbolism (textbook · 2019 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Oxygen (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Phosphorus atom

known

15 protons.

At the heart of ATP, DNA’s backbone and membrane lipids.

Made in massive stars and spread by supernovae.

Sources: OpenStax Chemistry 2e, 2.3 Atomic Structure and Symbolism (textbook · 2019 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Phosphorus (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Electron cloud

known

Electrons are not little planets.

The cloud shows where an electron is likely to be found.

This comes from quantum mechanics (orbitals).

An atom is almost all empty space.

Sources: Schrödinger 1926: An Undulatory Theory of the Mechanics of Atoms and Molecules (Phys. Rev.) (paper · 1926 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Chemistry 2e, 6.3 Development of Quantum Theory (textbook · 2019 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Atomic orbital (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Atomic nucleus

known

Protons and neutrons packed tight.

It holds over 99.9% of the atom’s mass.

Really about 100,000 times smaller than the atom; drawn far too big here.

Protons and neutrons are made of quarks.

Sources: Rutherford 1911: The scattering of α and β particles by matter and the structure of the atom (Philosophical Magazine) (paper · 1911 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Chemistry 2e, 21.1 Nuclear Structure and Stability (textbook · 2019 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Atomic nucleus (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Human Body

Human body

known

About 1.65 m tall here, close to the average adult height worldwide.

Made of about 30 trillion human cells.

Proportions follow standard adult measurements: the head is about 1/7.5 of the height, the navel about 60% of the way up.

Drawing: Front view; one typical body, real people vary a lot.

Sources: Sender et al. 2016: Revised Estimates for the Number of Human and Bacteria Cells in the Body (PLoS Biol) (paper · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Ncd Risk Factor Collaboration (NCD-RisC) 2016: A century of trends in adult human height (eLife) (paper · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 1.2 Structural Organization of the Human Body (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Human body (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Skin

known

Your largest organ: about 1.6–1.8 m² and 3–4 kg.

It keeps water in and germs out.

Epidermis: about 0.1 mm thick (up to 1.5 mm on the soles).

Dermis: about 1–4 mm, with collagen made by fibroblast cells.

Under it is a fat layer (hypodermis) of varying thickness.

Drawing: Shown as a smooth outline with no surface detail.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 5.1 Layers of the Skin (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Human skin (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Face

known

Eyes, nose, mouth and ears: most of the senses sit in the head, close to the brain.

The face has about 20 small muscles that make expressions.

Drawing: Simple outline, not a portrait.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.3 Axial Muscles of the Head, Neck, and Back (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Face (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Breast

known

Made of fat, supporting tissue and the mammary gland, on top of the chest muscle (pectoralis major).

They develop at puberty; size and shape vary a lot between people and are not linked to how much milk they can make.

In pregnancy the breasts grow and prepare to make milk.

After birth they make milk to feed the baby.

Drawing: Simple contour lines, typical adult size.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 27.2 Anatomy and Physiology of the Ovarian Reproductive System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 28.6 Lactation (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Breast (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Nipple and areola

known

The nipple has several tiny openings (often about 5–10) where milk ducts end.

The darker ring around it is the areola, typically 3–6 cm across.

Small glands in the areola (Montgomery glands) keep the skin moist.

In pregnancy the areola often gets darker and larger; a newborn finds it more easily.

Drawing: Drawn as plain circles.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 28.6 Lactation (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Nipple (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Areola (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Collarbone (clavicle)

known

A long S-shaped bone you can see and feel under the skin, from the top of the breastbone to the shoulder.

It links the arm to the trunk.

It is the first bone to start hardening in a growing embryo, about 5–6 weeks after fertilisation.

Drawing: Surface line only.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.1 The Pectoral Girdle (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Clavicle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Knee (front)

known

The kneecap (patella) sits in the tendon of the thigh muscle, in front of the knee joint.

It protects the joint and helps the thigh muscle straighten the leg.

The kneecap is still soft cartilage at birth and turns to bone at about 3–6 years old.

Drawing: Simple outline of the kneecap.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 9.6 Anatomy of Selected Synovial Joints (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Patella (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Mons pubis

known

A rounded pad of fat over the pubic bone, at the front of the vulva.

It cushions the pubic bone; hair grows here after puberty (not drawn).

The creases at each side are the groin (inguinal) folds, where the thigh meets the trunk.

Drawing: Simple outline with light shading.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 27.2 Anatomy and Physiology of the Ovarian Reproductive System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Mons pubis (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Navel (umbilicus)

known

A scar where the umbilical cord was attached before birth.

In the womb the cord carried blood between the baby and the placenta: food and oxygen in, waste out.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 1.6 Anatomical Terminology (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Navel (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Vulva (external genitals)

known

The outer parts: the mons pubis, the outer and inner lips (labia majora and minora), the clitoris, and the separate openings of the urethra (for urine) and the vagina.

The labia protect the openings.

At birth the baby comes out through the vaginal opening, which stretches a great deal.

Shown here in a simple front view; much of it is hidden between the thighs when standing.

Drawing: Simplified clinical outline; inner parts not drawn in detail.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 27.2 Anatomy and Physiology of the Ovarian Reproductive System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Vulva (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Mammary glands

known

Each breast has about 15–20 lobes. Each lobe has many small lobules that can make milk.

Ducts carry milk from the lobes to the nipple.

In pregnancy hormones (oestrogen, progesterone) grow the ducts and lobules.

After birth the hormone prolactin starts milk production; oxytocin squeezes milk out when the baby feeds (the let-down reflex).

Drawing: Lobes and ducts drawn as a simple pattern.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 28.6 Lactation (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Mammary gland (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Lactation (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Vagina

known

A stretchy muscular tube about 7–10 cm long, from the cervix to the outside.

Menstrual blood leaves the body through it.

It is the birth canal: in childbirth it stretches to let the baby through.

Helpful bacteria (lactobacilli) keep it acidic, about pH 3.8–4.5, which protects against infection.

Drawing: It runs up and backwards, so from the front it looks much shorter; shown over the bladder so you can see it.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 27.2 Anatomy and Physiology of the Ovarian Reproductive System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Vagina (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Cervix

known

The narrow lower end of the uterus, about 2.5–3 cm long, opening into the vagina.

It makes mucus that changes through the monthly cycle.

In pregnancy it stays closed with a mucus plug that protects the baby.

In labour it opens (dilates) to about 10 cm so the baby can be born.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 27.2 Anatomy and Physiology of the Ovarian Reproductive System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Cervix (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Fallopian tubes

known

Two tubes about 10–12 cm long from the uterus towards the ovaries.

Their finger-like ends catch the egg when it is released.

Fertilisation (egg meets sperm) usually happens inside the tube.

Tiny hairs (cilia) move the early embryo to the uterus over about 3–5 days.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 27.2 Anatomy and Physiology of the Ovarian Reproductive System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Fallopian tube (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Skull

known

22 bones; 8 make the braincase around the brain.

The jaw (mandible) is the only one that moves.

Adult skull is about 18–20 cm from front to back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 7.2 The Skull (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Human skull (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Spine (vertebral column)

known

33 vertebrae: 7 neck, 12 chest, 5 lower back, then the fused sacrum (5) and tailbone (about 4).

It holds the spinal cord.

In an adult woman it is about 60–70 cm long.

Seen from the front it is mostly hidden behind the organs.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 7.3 The Vertebral Column (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Vertebral column (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Rib cage

known

12 pairs of ribs. The top 7 join the breastbone (sternum) directly.

Pairs 11 and 12 float free at the front.

It protects the heart and lungs and moves with every breath.

Drawing: Rib curves simplified.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 7.4 The Thoracic Cage (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Rib cage (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sternum (breastbone)

known

A flat bone about 15–17 cm long in the middle of the chest.

Three parts: manubrium, body and the small xiphoid process.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 7.4 The Thoracic Cage (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Sternum (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Clavicle (collarbone)

known

Joins the breastbone to the shoulder blade.

About 14–15 cm long in women.

One of the most commonly broken bones.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.1 The Pectoral Girdle (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Clavicle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Humerus

known

The upper arm bone, about 30 cm long in women.

Its round head fits the shallow shoulder socket, so the shoulder moves a lot but dislocates easily.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Humerus (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Radius and ulna

known

The two forearm bones. The radius is on the thumb side.

The radius turns around the ulna to rotate your palm.

About 22–25 cm long.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Radius (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Hand bones

known

27 bones: 8 wrist bones (carpals), 5 metacarpals, 14 finger bones (phalanges).

Both hands together hold over a quarter of the body’s bones.

Drawing: Wrist bones shown as one group.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Hand (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Pelvis

known

Two hip bones plus the sacrum, forming a ring.

In women it is wider and rounder, making room for childbirth.

The female pelvic inlet is oval and wide; the angle under the pubic bones is usually over 80°.

Drawing: Outline simplified.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.3 The Pelvic Girdle and Pelvis (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Pelvis (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Femur (thigh bone)

known

The longest and strongest bone: about 42–45 cm in a 1.65 m adult (about 26–27% of height).

It slants inward to the knee; the slant is larger in women because the pelvis is wider.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.4 Bones of the Lower Limb (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Femur (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Patella (kneecap)

known

A bone that grows inside the tendon of the thigh muscle.

About 5 cm across.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.4 Bones of the Lower Limb (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Patella (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Tibia and fibula

known

The shin bone (tibia) carries the weight; the thin fibula is on the outside.

The tibia is about 35–37 cm long in women.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.4 Bones of the Lower Limb (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Tibia (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Foot bones

known

26 bones: 7 tarsals, 5 metatarsals, 14 phalanges.

Seen from the front, the foot is foreshortened.

Drawing: Foot shown foreshortened and simplified.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 8.4 Bones of the Lower Limb (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Foot (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Brain

known

About 1.2–1.3 kg in adult women, with about 86 billion neurons.

Uses about 20% of the body’s energy.

How it makes thoughts and feelings is still being worked out.

Sources: Azevedo et al. 2009: Equal numbers of neuronal and nonneuronal cells make the human brain an isometrically scaled‐up primate brain (J. Comp. Neurol.) (paper · 2009 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 13.2 The Central Nervous System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Human brain (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Eye

known

About 24 mm across.

The retina has about 120 million rods and 6 million cones.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 14.1 Sensory Perception (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Human eye (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Thyroid gland

known

A butterfly-shaped gland in the neck, about 5 cm wide.

Its hormones set how fast the body uses energy.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 17.4 The Thyroid Gland (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Thyroid (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Lungs

known

The right lung has 3 lobes, the left has 2 and a notch for the heart.

About 300–500 million air sacs (alveoli).

At rest you breathe about 12–16 times a minute, about 0.5 L each time.

Their inner surface is about 50–75 m².

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 22.2 The Lungs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Lung (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Heart

known

About the size of a fist, 250–300 g in women.

Beats about 60–100 times a minute; shown at 70.

Pumps about 5 litres of blood a minute at rest.

It sits in the middle of the chest, tilted to the person’s left.

Drawing: Outline simplified. Zoom in to see the 4 chambers and 4 valves (textbook cut-away).

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Heart (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Right atrium

known

Collects oxygen-poor blood from the body through the two venae cavae.

Holds the sinoatrial node, the heart’s natural pacemaker.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Atrium (heart) (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Right ventricle

known

Pumps oxygen-poor blood to the lungs through the pulmonary valve.

Wall about 3–5 mm thick; pressure about 25 mmHg when it squeezes.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Ventricle (heart) (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Left atrium

known

Collects oxygen-rich blood from the lungs through 4 pulmonary veins.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Atrium (heart) (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Left ventricle

known

Pumps oxygen-rich blood to the whole body through the aorta.

Thickest wall, about 1 cm; pressure about 120 mmHg when it squeezes.

About 70 mL leaves it each beat at rest.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Ventricle (heart) (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Septum

known

The wall between the left and right sides, so the two bloods do not mix.

Carries the electrical bundle that makes the ventricles squeeze together.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Interventricular septum (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Tricuspid valve

known

3 flaps between the right atrium and right ventricle.

Shown opening while the ventricle fills, shutting when it squeezes.

Its closing (with the mitral valve) is the first heart sound, “lub”.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Tricuspid valve (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Mitral valve

known

2 flaps between the left atrium and left ventricle.

Shown opening while the ventricle fills, shutting when it squeezes.

Its closing (with the tricuspid) is the first heart sound, “lub”.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Mitral valve (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Pulmonary valve

known

3 half-moon flaps from the right ventricle to the lungs’ artery.

Shown opening when the ventricle squeezes.

Its closing (with the aortic valve) is the second heart sound, “dub”.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Pulmonary valve (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Aortic valve

known

3 half-moon flaps from the left ventricle into the aorta.

Shown opening when the ventricle squeezes.

Its closing (with the pulmonary valve) is the second heart sound, “dub”.

Drawing: Textbook cut-away (coronal section), simplified. In real life the right ventricle faces the front and the left atrium sits at the back.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 19.1 Heart Anatomy (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Aortic valve (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Liver

known

The largest internal organ, about 1.2–1.5 kg.

Mostly on the person’s right, under the ribs.

Does over 500 jobs, from cleaning blood to making bile.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 23.6 Accessory Organs in Digestion: The Liver, Pancreas, and Gallbladder (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Liver (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Gallbladder

known

A small bag under the liver that stores bile, about 50 mL.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 23.6 Accessory Organs in Digestion: The Liver, Pancreas, and Gallbladder (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Gallbladder (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Stomach

known

J-shaped, on the person’s left under the ribs.

Holds about 1–1.5 L after a meal.

Its acid has a pH of about 1.5–3.5.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 23.4 The Stomach (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Stomach (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Spleen

known

About 11 cm long, on the person’s left behind the stomach.

Filters old red blood cells and helps fight infection.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 21.1 Anatomy of the Lymphatic and Immune Systems (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Spleen (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Pancreas

known

About 15 cm long, behind the stomach.

Makes digestive juices and insulin.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 23.6 Accessory Organs in Digestion: The Liver, Pancreas, and Gallbladder (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 17.9 The Endocrine Pancreas (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Pancreas (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Kidneys

known

Two bean shapes about 11 cm long at the back of the belly.

Together they filter about 180 L of blood plasma a day.

The right kidney usually sits a little lower, under the liver.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 25.3 Gross Anatomy of the Kidney (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Kidney (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Small intestine

known

About 3–5 m long in a living person.

Where most food is absorbed.

Drawing: Coils drawn as a simplified pattern, not each real loop.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 23.5 The Small and Large Intestines (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Small intestine (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Large intestine

known

About 1.5 m long, framing the small intestine.

Takes water back and holds trillions of gut bacteria.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 23.5 The Small and Large Intestines (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Large intestine (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Bladder

known

Stores urine: usually 300–500 mL.

Drawing: Drawn see-through so the reproductive organs behind it show.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 25.2 Gross Anatomy of Urine Transport (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Urinary bladder (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Uterus

known

A pear-shaped muscular organ about 7–8 cm long, behind and above the bladder.

Each month its lining thickens; if there is no pregnancy, the lining is shed as a period.

In pregnancy the early embryo implants in the lining and the placenta forms.

The uterus grows from about 60 g to about 1 kg and holds the baby for about 40 weeks.

In labour its strong muscle contracts to push the baby out.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 27.2 Anatomy and Physiology of the Ovarian Reproductive System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Uterus (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Ovaries

known

Two almond-sized glands, about 3–4 cm long, either side of the uterus.

They hold the eggs and make the hormones oestrogen and progesterone.

A baby girl is born with about 1–2 million immature eggs; only about 400–500 are released in a lifetime, usually one a month.

After an egg is released, the corpus luteum makes progesterone, which supports an early pregnancy.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 27.2 Anatomy and Physiology of the Ovarian Reproductive System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Ovary (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Skeletal muscles

known

About 600 muscles, roughly 30–35% of body weight in women.

They pull on bones through tendons.

Only the larger surface muscles are drawn.

Drawing: Main surface muscles only, shapes simplified.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 10.2 Skeletal Muscle (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.2 Naming Skeletal Muscles (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Skeletal muscle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Deltoid

known

The rounded shoulder muscle; lifts the arm sideways.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Deltoid muscle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Pectoralis major

known

The large fan-shaped chest muscle; pulls the arm forward and across.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Pectoralis major (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Biceps brachii

known

Bends the elbow and turns the palm up.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Biceps (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Forearm muscles

known

About 20 muscles that move the wrist and fingers.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Forearm (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Rectus abdominis

known

The long belly muscle, split into sections by bands of tendon.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.4 Axial Muscles of the Abdominal Wall, and Thorax (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Rectus abdominis muscle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

External oblique

known

The side belly muscle; twists and bends the trunk.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.4 Axial Muscles of the Abdominal Wall, and Thorax (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Abdominal external oblique muscle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sternocleidomastoid

known

The neck muscle that turns and tilts the head.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.3 Axial Muscles of the Head, Neck, and Back (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Sternocleidomastoid muscle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Quadriceps

known

Four muscles at the front of the thigh that straighten the knee.

One of the strongest muscle groups in the body.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Quadriceps (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sartorius

known

The longest muscle in the body, crossing the thigh diagonally.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Sartorius muscle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Adductors

known

Inner-thigh muscles that pull the legs together.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Adductor muscles of the hip (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Tibialis anterior

known

Lifts the front of the foot.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Tibialis anterior muscle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Gastrocnemius (calf)

known

The calf muscle; lifts the heel through the Achilles tendon.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Gastrocnemius muscle (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Arteries

known

Carry blood away from the heart (red here).

The aorta is about 2.5–3 cm wide.

Blood in the aorta moves on average about 20 cm/s; the moving dots use that speed.

All arteries together are roughly 9,000–19,000 km long (newer estimates); the old "100,000 km" figure counts every vessel and is too high.

Drawing: Main arteries are mapped; the small branches that appear as you zoom are a generated pattern, not a map.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 20.1 Structure and Function of Blood Vessels (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Artery (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Aorta

known

The biggest artery, about 2.5–3 cm wide.

It arches over the heart and runs down in front of the spine.

It splits into the two iliac arteries at about the navel.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 20.5 Circulatory Pathways (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Aorta (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Veins

known

Carry blood back to the heart (blue here; real blood is always red, darker in veins).

Valves stop blood falling back down the legs.

Drawing: Main veins drawn; small branches are a generated pattern.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 20.1 Structure and Function of Blood Vessels (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Vein (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Nerves

known

Nerves carry signals between the brain, spinal cord and body.

Fast nerve signals travel up to about 120 m/s.

The moving dots are shown much slower than real so you can see them.

Drawing: Main nerves drawn; small branches are a generated pattern. Signals slowed down.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 13.4 The Peripheral Nervous System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Nerve (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Spinal cord

known

About 43 cm long in women; ends near the first lumbar vertebra.

Below that, nerve roots continue as the "horse’s tail" (cauda equina).

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 13.2 The Central Nervous System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Spinal cord (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Sciatic nerve

known

The thickest nerve, up to about 2 cm wide, running down the back of the thigh.

Seen from the front it lies behind the femur.

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 13.4 The Peripheral Nervous System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Sciatic nerve (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Arm nerves

known

The median, ulnar and radial nerves run down the arm to the hand.

Hitting the ulnar nerve at the elbow is the "funny bone".

Sources: OpenStax Anatomy and Physiology 2e, 13.4 The Peripheral Nervous System (textbook · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Median nerve (Wikipedia) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Which cells build each part

  • heart → Heart muscle cell (cardiomyocyte): About 100 µm long, packed with mitochondria (about a third of its volume); usually 1–2 nuclei. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • liver → Liver cell (hepatocyte): About 20–30 µm, lots of ER and mitochondria; about a quarter have 2 nuclei. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • brain → Nerve cell (neuron): Body about 4–100 µm, with long branches; the brain has about 86 billion. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • nerve → Nerve cell (neuron): Its axon can be over 1 m long, from the spine to the toes. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • sciatic → Nerve cell (neuron): Its axon can be over 1 m long, from the spine to the toes. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • cord → Nerve cell (neuron): Motor neurons here send axons out to the muscles. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • lungs → Lung air-sac cell (alveolar cell): Type I cells are very thin, about 0.1–0.2 µm in places, so gas crosses fast. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • kidney → Kidney tubule cell: Covered in tiny finger-like microvilli and full of mitochondria to pump salts back. source (encyclopedia · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • stomach → Stomach acid cell (parietal cell): Makes hydrochloric acid with proton pumps; very rich in mitochondria. source (encyclopedia · 2025 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • smallint → Gut lining cell (enterocyte): Lives only about 3–5 days; its microvilli greatly widen the surface. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • largeint → Gut lining cell (colonocyte): Replaced about every 5 days. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • artery → Vessel lining cell (endothelial cell): A thin flat sheet one cell thick lines every blood vessel. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • vein → Vessel lining cell (endothelial cell): A thin flat sheet one cell thick lines every blood vessel. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • aorta → Vessel lining cell (endothelial cell): Under it sit layers of smooth muscle cells and elastic fibres. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • femur → Bone cell (osteocyte): Lives inside hard bone, linked to others by tiny channels; can live for decades. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • quads → Skeletal muscle fibre: One giant cell with hundreds of nuclei, up to several cm long. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • biceps → Skeletal muscle fibre: One giant cell with hundreds of nuclei, up to several cm long. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • pec → Skeletal muscle fibre: One giant cell with hundreds of nuclei, up to several cm long. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • ovary → Egg cell (oocyte): About 0.1 mm, the largest human cell, just visible to the eye. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • thyroid → Thyroid cell (thyrocyte): Makes the hormones T3 and T4 using iodine. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
  • pancreas → Pancreas cell (acinar or beta cell): Acinar cells make digestive enzymes; beta cells make insulin. source (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

Physics: forces, particles and light

FORCES: the four fundamental interactions · PARTICLES: matter, antimatter and force carriers · LIGHT: electromagnetic radiation, relativity and quanta

Newton's laws of motion

known FORCES · force, mass and acceleration

A force changes how an object moves: F = ma.

Every action has an equal and opposite reaction.

Published: Isaac Newton set out the three laws in the Principia (1687).

Still used: They describe everyday motion very well; relativity and quantum physics take over at very high speeds and very small scales.

  • Maths: Derivatives · acceleration is a derivative
  • Maths: Differential equations · motion is solved with differential equations

Sources: OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, 5.3 Newton's Second Law (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, 5.1 Forces (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Newton's laws of motion (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Newton's law of universal gravitation

known FORCES · every mass pulls every other mass

Two masses attract with a force that falls with the square of the distance.

The same law explains falling apples and planetary orbits.

Strength: The constant G is about 6.674 × 10⁻¹¹ m³ kg⁻¹ s⁻² (CODATA); it is the least precisely known fundamental constant.

Limits: It is an excellent approximation; general relativity replaces it for strong gravity and high precision.

Builds on: newton-laws

  • From Nothing to Us: Gravity · the same force on the From Nothing to Us map
  • From Nothing to Us: Solar system · keeps the planets in orbit

Sources: OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, 13.1 Newton's Law of Universal Gravitation (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · NIST/CODATA: Newtonian constant of gravitation (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Newton's law of universal gravitation (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Coulomb's law

known FORCES · electric charges attract or repel

Like charges repel, opposite charges attract.

The force falls with the square of the distance, like gravity, but is far stronger.

Scale: Between two protons the electric push is about 10³⁶ times stronger than their gravitational pull.

Next: Coulomb's law is one part of Maxwell's equations.

Builds on: newton-laws

  • From Nothing to Us: Electromagnetism · electromagnetism on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, 5.3 Coulomb's Law (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Coulomb's law (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Noether's theorem

known FORCES · symmetry gives conservation laws

Every continuous symmetry of the laws of physics gives a conserved quantity.

Same laws at every time → energy is conserved; at every place → momentum.

Proved: Emmy Noether proved it in 1918 (English translation 1971).

Everywhere: Rotation symmetry gives conservation of angular momentum; the symmetries of the Standard Model give conserved charges.

Builds on: newton-laws

  • Maths: Lie groups & representations · continuous symmetries are Lie groups
  • Maths: Groups · symmetry is described by group theory

Sources: Noether 1971: Invariant variation problems (Transport Theory and Statistical Physics) (paper · 1971 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, 9.3 Conservation of Linear Momentum (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Noether's theorem (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

General relativity

known FORCES · gravity is curved spacetime

Mass and energy curve spacetime; objects follow the curves.

It predicted the bending of starlight, seen in the 1919 eclipse.

Published: Albert Einstein, 1915–1916.

Tested: GPS clocks, black hole images and gravitational waves all match it.

Open: It does not fit with quantum physics: see Quantum gravity.

Builds on: universal-gravitation, special-relativity

  • From Nothing to Us: Black holes · black holes are a prediction of it
  • Maths: Riemannian geometry · curved spacetime uses Riemannian geometry
  • Maths: Curvature · curvature is the core idea

Series A, Containing Papers of a Mathematical or Physical Character) (paper · 1920 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax University Physics Volume 1, 13.7 Einstein's Theory of Gravity (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: General relativity (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Gravitational waves

known FORCES · ripples in spacetime

Accelerating masses send ripples through spacetime at the speed of light.

First detected by LIGO on 14 September 2015 from two merging black holes.

Since then: LIGO and Virgo saw two neutron stars merge in 2017, with light seen too.

How small: The stretch measured is far smaller than a proton across a 4 km detector arm.

Builds on: general-relativity

  • From Nothing to Us: Black holes · the first signal came from two black holes

(official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Gravitational wave (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Quantum chromodynamics

known FORCES · the strong interaction

The strong interaction binds quarks with gluons, carrying a charge called colour.

It gets weaker at short distances (asymptotic freedom, 1973).

Confinement: Quarks are never seen alone; they stay inside protons, neutrons and other hadrons.

Nuclei: A leftover of it holds protons and neutrons together in every nucleus.

Builds on: quarks

  • Big Bang to Today: Quarks bind into hadrons · when quarks first became bound

(paper · 1973 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · PDG 2024 review: quantum chromodynamics (gluons and the strong force) (review · 2024 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia: Quantum chromodynamics (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Weak interaction

known FORCES · changes one particle into another

The weak interaction can turn a neutron into a proton (beta decay).

It lets the Sun fuse hydrogen into helium.

Carriers: W and Z bosons, found at CERN in 1983.

Neutral currents: Gargamelle at CERN saw neutral currents in 1973, the first sign of the Z boson.

  • From Nothing to Us: Weak force · the weak force on the From Nothing to Us map
  • From Nothing to Us: Fusion in stars · it powers fusion in stars
  • From Nothing to Us: W & Z bosons · W and Z bosons

Sources: Hasert et al. 1973: Observation of neutrino-like interactions without muon or electron in the gargamelle neutrino experiment (Physics Letters B) (paper · 1973 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Arnison et al. 1983: Experimental observation of isolated large transverse energy electrons with associated missing energy at (Physics Letters B) (paper · 1983 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Weak interaction (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Electroweak interaction

known FORCES · electromagnetism and the weak force as one

Above about 100 GeV, electromagnetism and the weak interaction act as one force.

The Higgs field splits them apart at lower energies.

Theory: Glashow (1961), Weinberg (1967) and Salam built it.

Confirmed: Neutral currents (1973), the W and Z (1983) and the Higgs boson (2012).

Builds on: weak-interaction, qed

  • From Nothing to Us: Higgs field · the Higgs field
  • Big Bang to Today: End of the electroweak epoch · when the two forces separated

(paper · 1967 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Wikipedia: Electroweak interaction (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Fundamental interactions

known FORCES · four known forces

Every known force comes from four fundamental interactions.

Gravitation, electromagnetism, the strong and the weak interaction.

Strengths: Inside a nucleus the strong interaction is strongest; gravitation is by far the weakest.

Carriers: The Standard Model describes three of them with gauge bosons; gravitation is described by general relativity.

Builds on: general-relativity, electroweak, strong-interaction

  • From Nothing to Us: Quantum fields · quantum fields on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, 11.5 The Standard Model (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · CERN: The Standard Model (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Fundamental interaction (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Grand Unified Theory

unknown FORCES · one force behind three?

A proposed theory joining the strong, weak and electromagnetic interactions.

Many versions predict that protons decay; none has been seen.

First model: Georgi and Glashow proposed one in 1974.

Searches: Super-Kamiokande has watched for proton decay for decades without seeing one.

Builds on: electroweak, strong-interaction

Quantum gravity

unknown FORCES · gravity with quantum rules

No tested theory yet joins general relativity with quantum physics.

It matters inside black holes and at the very start of the universe.

Candidates: String theory and loop quantum gravity are the best known; neither has been confirmed by experiment.

Builds on: general-relativity, qed

Leptons

known PARTICLES · electron, muon, tau and neutrinos

Leptons do not feel the strong interaction.

The electron, found in 1897, was the first known elementary particle.

Six kinds: Electron, muon and tau, each with its own neutrino.

Charge: The electron, muon and tau carry charge −1; neutrinos carry none.

  • From Nothing to Us: Electrons & leptons · electrons and leptons on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Thomson 1897: Cathode Rays (Philosophical Magazine) (paper · 1897 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, 11.1 Introduction to Particle Physics (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Particle Data Group: Review of Particle Physics (review · year n/a · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia: Lepton (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Quarks

known PARTICLES · up, down, strange, charm, bottom, top

Protons and neutrons are made of quarks.

Proposed in 1964; seen inside protons at SLAC in 1968–1969.

Proton: Two up quarks and one down quark.

Neutron: One up quark and two down quarks.

Antimatter

known PARTICLES · every particle has an opposite

Each particle has an antiparticle with the same mass and opposite charge.

Dirac's 1928 equation predicted it; the positron was found in 1932.

Meeting: A particle and its antiparticle can annihilate into energy (E = mc²).

Today: CERN makes and traps antihydrogen to compare it with hydrogen.

Builds on: leptons, special-relativity

Gauge bosons

known PARTICLES · the force carriers

Forces in the Standard Model are carried by gauge bosons.

Photon (electromagnetism), 8 gluons (strong), W⁺, W⁻ and Z (weak).

Found: The W was first seen by UA1 at CERN in 1983, the Z soon after.

Builds on: photon

  • From Nothing to Us: W & Z bosons · W and Z bosons
  • From Nothing to Us: Photons · photons

Standard Model

known PARTICLES · the particle physics theory

The Standard Model describes all known elementary particles and three of the four forces.

Its predictions have passed every test at particle colliders.

Missing: It leaves out gravity, dark matter and neutrino masses.

Numbers: Its particle masses and couplings are measured, not predicted.

Builds on: quarks, leptons, gauge-bosons, electroweak

  • From Nothing to Us: Quantum fields · quantum fields on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Maths: Lie groups & representations · its forces are built on Lie groups

Higgs boson

known PARTICLES · found in 2012

The particle of the Higgs field, found by ATLAS and CMS at CERN in 2012.

Its mass is about 125 GeV.

Why it matters: The Higgs field gives the W and Z bosons, quarks and charged leptons their mass.

Builds on: standard-model

  • Big Bang to Today: End of the electroweak epoch · when the Higgs field switched on

Neutrino oscillation

known PARTICLES · neutrinos change type

Neutrinos change between their three types as they travel.

Seen by Super-Kamiokande (1998) and SNO (2002).

Meaning: Oscillation is only possible if neutrinos have mass, which the original Standard Model left out.

Builds on: leptons

  • From Nothing to Us: Neutrinos · neutrinos on the From Nothing to Us map

Neutrino mass

unknown PARTICLES · how heavy is a neutrino?

We know neutrinos have mass, but not how much each one weighs.

KATRIN (2025): below 0.45 eV, under a millionth of an electron's mass.

Open: Their absolute masses, their order and why they are so light are still unknown.

Builds on: neutrino-oscillation

  • From Nothing to Us: Neutrino masses · neutrino masses on the From Nothing to Us map

Baryon asymmetry

unknown PARTICLES · why is there more matter than antimatter?

The universe is made of matter, with almost no antimatter left.

Known physics does not explain how this imbalance arose.

Clues: Matter and antimatter behave slightly differently (CP violation), seen in baryons by LHCb in 2025, but known effects are too small.

Builds on: antimatter, standard-model

  • From Nothing to Us: Why matter won? · why matter won, on the From Nothing to Us map

Dark matter

we think PARTICLES · mass we cannot see

Galaxies spin and bend light as if there is about five times more matter than we see.

What dark matter is made of is still unknown.

Evidence: Galaxy rotation (Rubin and Ford, 1970), the cosmic microwave background (Planck) and gravitational lensing.

Searches: Detectors such as LZ have not yet found a dark-matter particle.

Builds on: general-relativity, standard-model

Maxwell's equations

known LIGHT · electricity, magnetism and light

Four equations describe all electric and magnetic fields.

They predicted waves of electric and magnetic field that travel at the speed of light.

Published: James Clerk Maxwell, 1865.

Meaning: Light is an electromagnetic wave.

Builds on: coulomb-law

  • From Nothing to Us: Electromagnetism · electromagnetism on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Maths: Vector calculus · written in vector calculus
  • Maths: Partial differential equations · they are partial differential equations

Electromagnetic radiation

known LIGHT · radio to gamma rays

Radio, microwaves, infrared, visible light, ultraviolet, X-rays and gamma rays are one thing.

They differ only in wavelength; visible light is a thin slice.

All the same speed: In empty space all of them travel at the speed of light.

Builds on: maxwell-equations

  • From Nothing to Us: Photons · photons on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Maths: Fourier analysis · light is analysed by wavelength with Fourier analysis

Sources: OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, 16.5 The Electromagnetic Spectrum (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · NASA Science: Tour of the Electromagnetic Spectrum (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Electromagnetic radiation (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Speed of light

known LIGHT · 299 792 458 m/s

Light in empty space travels at exactly 299 792 458 metres per second.

It is exact because the metre is defined from it.

Same for everyone: Michelson and Morley (1887) found no change with Earth's motion; special relativity explains why.

Builds on: maxwell-equations

Sources: NIST/CODATA: speed of light in vacuum (exact) (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · BIPM: the International System of Units (SI) (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Michelson & Morley 1887: On the relative motion of the Earth and the luminiferous ether (American Journal of Science) (paper · 1887 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Speed of light (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Special relativity

known LIGHT · space, time and E = mc²

The laws of physics and the speed of light are the same for every steady observer.

Moving clocks run slow; mass and energy are linked by E = mc².

Published: Albert Einstein, 1905.

Tested: Particle accelerators and satellite clocks confirm it every day.

Builds on: speed-of-light

  • From Nothing to Us: Hot particle soup · E = mc²: energy turned into particle pairs

Sources: Einstein 1905: Zur Elektrodynamik bewegter Körper (Annalen der Physik) (paper · 1905 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, 5.1 Invariance of Physical Laws (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Special relativity (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Planck's law

known LIGHT · black-body radiation

A hot object glows with a colour set only by its temperature.

Max Planck explained it in 1900 by assuming energy comes in small packets (quanta).

Start of quantum physics: Planck's quanta were the first step towards quantum theory.

In the sky: Stars and the cosmic microwave background shine close to this law.

Builds on: em-radiation

  • From Nothing to Us: Stars · the colour of a star shows its temperature

Sources: Planck 1901: Ueber das Gesetz der Energieverteilung im Normalspectrum (Annalen der Physik) (paper · 1901 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, 6.1 Blackbody Radiation (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Planck's law (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Photon

known LIGHT · a quantum of light

Light comes in packets called photons, each with energy E = hf.

Einstein used this in 1905 to explain the photoelectric effect.

Particle-like: Compton (1923) showed X-ray photons bounce off electrons like particles.

Force carrier: The photon carries the electromagnetic interaction.

Builds on: blackbody

  • From Nothing to Us: Photons · photons on the From Nothing to Us map

Wave–particle duality

known LIGHT · waves and particles at once

Light and matter both show wave and particle behaviour.

Electrons diffract like waves (Davisson and Germer, 1927).

Meaning: Quantum objects are neither classical waves nor classical particles.

Builds on: photon

  • Maths: Hilbert spaces · quantum states live in Hilbert spaces

Sources: Davisson & Germer 1927: Diffraction of Electrons by a Crystal of Nickel (Phys. Rev.) (paper · 1927 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, 6.6 Wave-Particle Duality (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Wave–particle duality (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Atomic spectra

known LIGHT · each element's light fingerprint

Each element gives out and absorbs light only at its own set of wavelengths.

Bohr's 1913 model explained hydrogen's lines with electron energy levels.

Used for: Spectral lines tell us what stars and galaxies are made of.

Data: NIST keeps a database of measured lines for every element.

Builds on: photon

  • All the Elements: Hydrogen · hydrogen, the first element
  • From Nothing to Us: All 118 elements · all 118 elements

Sources: Bohr 1913: On the constitution of atoms and molecules (Philosophical Magazine) (paper · 1913 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, 6.4 Bohr’s Model of the Hydrogen Atom (textbook · 2016 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · NIST Atomic Spectra Database (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Spectral line (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Quantum electrodynamics

known LIGHT · light and charged matter

QED is the quantum theory of light interacting with electrons and other charged particles.

It is one of the most precisely tested theories in science.

Built: Feynman, Schwinger and Tomonaga in the late 1940s.

Tested: The electron's magnetic moment was measured to about 0.13 parts per trillion in 2023, and matches the QED prediction.

Builds on: photon, special-relativity

  • From Nothing to Us: Electromagnetism · electromagnetism on the From Nothing to Us map
  • From Nothing to Us: Quantum fields · a quantum field theory

Sources: Feynman 1949: Space-Time Approach to Quantum Electrodynamics (Phys. Rev.) (paper · 1949 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Fan et al. 2023: Measurement of the Electron Magnetic Moment (Phys. Rev. Lett.) (paper · 2023 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Wikipedia: Quantum electrodynamics (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Cosmic microwave background

known LIGHT · the oldest light

Light released about 380,000 years after the Big Bang, now cooled to microwaves.

Found by Penzias and Wilson in 1965; mapped in detail by Planck.

Temperature: About 2.7 K, with a near-perfect black-body spectrum.

Tiny ripples: Its small hot and cold spots are the seeds of galaxies.

Builds on: blackbody

  • From Nothing to Us: Afterglow (CMB) · the afterglow on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Big Bang to Today: Recombination · when this light was set free
