Biology: cells, evolution and life systems

CELLS & MOLECULES: the chemistry and machinery inside every cell · EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS: where life came from and how it changes · LIFE SYSTEMS: energy, ecosystems and bodies

Elements of life (CHNOPS)

known CELLS & MOLECULES · six elements build most of life

Most of the mass of every living thing is just six elements: carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, oxygen, phosphorus and sulfur.

Carbon can make four bonds, so it builds the chains and rings of life's molecules.

Carbon: Each carbon atom forms four covalent bonds, giving a huge variety of molecule shapes.

Made in stars: Hydrogen comes mostly from the Big Bang; the other five were made in stars.

  • All the Elements: Carbon · carbon
  • All the Elements: Phosphorus · phosphorus, in DNA and ATP
  • From Nothing to Us: Fusion in stars · carbon, nitrogen and oxygen are made in stars
  • Virtual Cell: Carbon atom · a carbon atom in the Virtual Cell

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 2.3 Carbon (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Biomolecule (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Biological macromolecules

known CELLS & MOLECULES · proteins, nucleic acids, sugars, lipids

Four big families of molecules build every cell: proteins, nucleic acids, carbohydrates and lipids.

Most are long chains made by linking small units together.

Units: Amino acids → proteins; nucleotides → DNA and RNA; sugars → starch and cellulose.

Lipids: Fats and phospholipids are not long repeating chains, but they build membranes and store energy.

Builds on: chnops

  • From Nothing to Us: Amino acids · building blocks of proteins
  • From Nothing to Us: Nucleotides · building blocks of DNA and RNA
  • From Nothing to Us: Fatty molecules · membrane molecules
  • Virtual Cell: Protein · a protein in the Virtual Cell

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 3.1 Synthesis of Biological Macromolecules (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Biomolecule (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Cell theory

known CELLS & MOLECULES · every living thing is made of cells

All living things are made of one or more cells.

The cell is the basic unit of life, and every cell comes from an earlier cell.

Built: Schleiden and Schwann (1838–39) and Virchow (1855), after the first microscopes of Hooke and van Leeuwenhoek.

Viruses: Viruses are not cells; whether they count as alive is debated.

  • Virtual Cell: Typical human cell (generalised) · a typical human cell
  • From Nothing to Us: Protocells · the first cells on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Mazzarello 1999: A unifying concept: the history of cell theory (Nat Cell Biol) (paper · 1999 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.1 Studying Cells (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Cell theory (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells

known CELLS & MOLECULES · without and with a nucleus

Prokaryotes (bacteria and archaea) have no nucleus.

Eukaryotic cells (animals, plants, fungi, protists) keep their DNA in a nucleus and have organelles.

Size: Most prokaryotic cells are about 0.1–5 µm across; eukaryotic cells are usually about 10–100 µm.

You: Your body is built from eukaryotic cells, and carries many prokaryotes too.

Builds on: cell-theory

  • Virtual Cell: Nucleus · the nucleus
  • From Nothing to Us: Bacteria & archaea · bacteria and archaea
  • From Nothing to Us: Complex cells · complex cells

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.2 Prokaryotic Cells (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.3 Eukaryotic Cells (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Prokaryote (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Eukaryote (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Cell membrane

known CELLS & MOLECULES · a fluid mosaic of lipids and proteins

A thin double layer of phospholipids, studded with proteins, wraps every cell.

It lets some things through and keeps others out.

Model: The fluid mosaic model (Singer and Nicolson, 1972): proteins float in a fluid layer of lipids.

Thin: About 5–10 nm thick.

Builds on: macromolecules, cell-theory

  • Virtual Cell: Cell membrane · the membrane in the Virtual Cell
  • Virtual Cell: Phospholipid · a phospholipid
  • From Nothing to Us: Fatty bubbles · fatty bubbles on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Singer & Nicolson 1972: The Fluid Mosaic Model of the Structure of Cell Membranes (Science) (paper · 1972 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 5.1 Components and Structure (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Cell membrane (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

DNA double helix

known CELLS & MOLECULES · the molecule of heredity

DNA is two strands wound into a double helix, joined by base pairs: A with T, G with C.

The order of the bases stores genetic information.

Discovered: Watson and Crick proposed the structure in 1953, using X-ray data from Rosalind Franklin and Raymond Gosling.

Size: One set of human chromosomes holds about 3 billion base pairs.

Builds on: macromolecules

  • From Nothing to Us: DNA · DNA on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Virtual Cell: Chromatin (DNA) · chromatin in the Virtual Cell
  • Virtual Cell: Base pair (DNA) · a base pair

Sources: Watson & Crick 1953: Molecular Structure of Nucleic Acids: A Structure for Deoxyribose Nucleic Acid (Nature) (paper · 1953 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Franklin & Gosling 1953: Molecular Configuration in Sodium Thymonucleate (Nature) (paper · 1953 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 14.2 DNA Structure and Sequencing (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · NHGRI: Deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) fact sheet (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: DNA (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Central dogma of molecular biology

known CELLS & MOLECULES · DNA → RNA → protein

Genetic information flows from DNA to RNA (transcription) and from RNA to protein (translation).

It does not flow back from protein to DNA or RNA.

Stated: Francis Crick, 1958; restated in Nature in 1970.

RNA → DNA: Retroviruses copy RNA back into DNA; this fits Crick's rule, which only forbids protein → nucleic acid.

Builds on: dna

  • Virtual Cell: Ribosome · ribosomes make the proteins
  • From Nothing to Us: Proteins · proteins on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Crick 1970: Central Dogma of Molecular Biology (Nature) (paper · 1970 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 15.5 Ribosomes and Protein Synthesis (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Central dogma of molecular biology (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Genetic code

known CELLS & MOLECULES · three letters for each amino acid

Each three-letter codon in RNA stands for one amino acid, or "stop".

64 codons code for 20 standard amino acids, and almost all life uses the same code.

Cracked: Nirenberg and Matthaei read the first codon (UUU = phenylalanine) in 1961.

Shared: The near-universal code is strong evidence that all life shares an ancestor.

Builds on: central-dogma

  • From Nothing to Us: Genetic code · the genetic code on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Virtual Cell: Protein chain · a protein chain

Sources: Nirenberg & Matthaei 1961: The dependence of cell-free protein synthesis in E. coli upon naturally occurring or synthetic polyribonucleotides (Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. U.S.A.) (paper · 1961 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 15.1 The Genetic Code (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Genetic code (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Enzymes

known CELLS & MOLECULES · catalysts that speed up life

Enzymes are catalysts, mostly proteins, that speed up the chemical reactions in cells.

Each one fits its target molecule at an active site.

Proof: In 1926 James Sumner crystallised the enzyme urease and showed it is a protein.

RNA too: Some RNA molecules (ribozymes) are catalysts as well, including the core of the ribosome.

Builds on: macromolecules

  • Virtual Cell: Protein · proteins in the Virtual Cell
  • From Nothing to Us: Proteins · proteins on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Sumner 1926: The isolation and crystallization of the enzyme urease (Journal of Biological Chemistry) (paper · 1926 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.5 Enzymes (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Enzyme (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

ATP

known CELLS & MOLECULES · the cell's energy currency

Adenosine triphosphate carries energy around the cell.

Splitting off its last phosphate releases energy that powers work, from muscles to building proteins.

Recycled: Cells remake ATP from ADP and phosphate again and again, mostly with the enzyme ATP synthase.

Parts: Adenine, the sugar ribose and three phosphate groups.

Builds on: macromolecules

  • Virtual Cell: ATP · ATP in the Virtual Cell
  • Virtual Cell: ATP synthase · ATP synthase
  • All the Elements: Phosphorus · phosphorus

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 6.4 ATP: Adenosine Triphosphate (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Adenosine triphosphate (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Cell division

known CELLS & MOLECULES · mitosis copies, meiosis mixes

In mitosis one cell becomes two identical cells, for growth and repair.

Meiosis makes egg and sperm cells with half the chromosomes, and shuffles the genes.

First: Before dividing, a cell copies all of its DNA.

Bacteria: Prokaryotes divide more simply, by binary fission.

Builds on: dna, cell-types

  • Virtual Cell: Centrosome · the centrosome organises division
  • From Nothing to Us: Many-celled life · many-celled life

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 10.1 Cell Division (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 11.1 The Process of Meiosis (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Cell division (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Origin of life

unknown EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · how chemistry became biology

How non-living chemistry first became living cells is unknown.

Experiments show life's building blocks can form naturally, but no one has made life from scratch.

Classic test: In 1953 Stanley Miller made amino acids from simple gases and electric sparks.

When: Life was present by about 3.5 billion years ago; how it began is still open.

Builds on: chnops

  • From Nothing to Us: The first spark · the first spark on the From Nothing to Us map
  • From Nothing to Us: Early-Earth chemistry · early-Earth chemistry
  • Big Bang to Today: Oldest widely accepted fossils · the oldest widely accepted fossils

Sources: Miller 1953: A Production of Amino Acids Under Possible Primitive Earth Conditions (Science) (paper · 1953 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Des Marais et al. 2008: The NASA Astrobiology Roadmap (Astrobiology) (paper · 2008 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Wikipedia: Abiogenesis (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

RNA world

we think EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · RNA as both gene and enzyme

A leading idea: early life used RNA both to store genes and to speed up reactions, before DNA and proteins.

Ribozymes show that RNA can do both jobs.

Named: Walter Gilbert named the "RNA world" in 1986.

Status: Widely supported but not proven; how the first RNA formed is unknown.

Builds on: origin-of-life, central-dogma

  • From Nothing to Us: RNA world · RNA world on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Gilbert 1986: Origin of life: The RNA world (Nature) (paper · 1986 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: RNA world (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Last universal common ancestor (LUCA)

we think EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · the shared ancestor of all life

All living things today descend from one ancestral population, called LUCA.

Its age and features are estimates, worked out by comparing genes.

Evidence: A formal statistical test in 2010 strongly supported universal common ancestry.

Age: A 2024 study estimated LUCA lived about 4.2 billion years ago; other estimates differ.

Builds on: rna-world, genetic-code

  • From Nothing to Us: LUCA · LUCA on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Big Bang to Today: Last universal common ancestor · on the Big Bang to Today timeline

Sources: Theobald 2010: A formal test of the theory of universal common ancestry (Nature) (paper · 2010 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Moody et al. 2024: The nature of the last universal common ancestor and its impact on the early Earth system (Nat Ecol Evol) (paper · 2024 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Last universal common ancestor (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Three domains of life

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · Bacteria · Archaea · Eukarya

Comparing ribosomal RNA splits life into three domains: Bacteria, Archaea and Eukarya.

Archaea look like bacteria but are a separate branch of life.

Proposed: Carl Woese and colleagues, 1990.

Debate: Newer genome trees suggest eukaryotes grew out of the archaea, giving two primary domains.

Builds on: luca, cell-types

  • From Nothing to Us: Bacteria & archaea · bacteria and archaea

Sources: Woese et al. 1990: Towards a natural system of organisms: proposal for the domains Archaea, Bacteria, and Eucarya (Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. U.S.A.) (paper · 1990 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 20.1 Organizing Life on Earth (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 22.1 Prokaryotic Diversity (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Three-domain system (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Endosymbiotic theory

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · mitochondria were once bacteria

Mitochondria and chloroplasts began as free-living bacteria taken inside another cell.

They still have their own DNA and divide like bacteria.

Champion: Lynn Margulis (then Lynn Sagan) set out the case in 1967.

Relatives: Mitochondria are related to alphaproteobacteria, chloroplasts to cyanobacteria.

Builds on: cell-types

  • Virtual Cell: Mitochondrion · a mitochondrion in the Virtual Cell
  • From Nothing to Us: Complex cells · complex cells

Sources: Sagan 1967: On the origin of mitosing cells (Journal of Theoretical Biology) (paper · 1967 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 23.1 Eukaryotic Origins (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Symbiogenesis (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Origin of eukaryotes

we think EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · how complex cells began

Complex cells probably arose when an archaeal host merged with a bacterium that became the mitochondrion.

The closest known archaeal relatives are the Asgard archaea.

When: Clear eukaryote fossils are more than 1.5 billion years old; the exact start is uncertain.

Recent: A 2023 genome study placed eukaryotes within the Asgard archaea, near the Heimdallarchaeia.

Builds on: endosymbiosis, three-domains

  • From Nothing to Us: Complex cells · complex cells on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Zaremba-Niedzwiedzka et al. 2017: Asgard archaea illuminate the origin of eukaryotic cellular complexity (Nature) (paper · 2017 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Eme et al. 2023: Inference and reconstruction of the heimdallarchaeial ancestry of eukaryotes (Nature) (paper · 2023 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 23.1 Eukaryotic Origins (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Eukaryogenesis (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Mendelian inheritance

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · genes come in pairs

Traits are passed on by separate units (genes), one copy from each parent.

Gregor Mendel found the rules by breeding pea plants.

Published: Mendel, 1866; rediscovered in 1900.

Laws: Segregation: the two copies separate into different sex cells. Independent assortment: different genes are often inherited separately.

  • Maths: Probability · inheritance follows the laws of probability

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 12.1 Mendel’s Experiments and the Laws of Probability (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Mendelian inheritance (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Natural selection

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · variation + inheritance → adaptation

Individuals vary; variations that help them survive and reproduce are passed on more often.

Over many generations this adapts populations to their surroundings.

Published: Charles Darwin and Alfred Russel Wallace, 1858; Darwin's On the Origin of Species, 1859.

Seen today: Antibiotic resistance in bacteria evolves by natural selection.

  • From Nothing to Us: Animals · animals on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Darwin & Wallace 1858: On the Tendency of Species to form Varieties; and on the Perpetuation of Varieties and Species by Natural Means of Selection (Journal of the Proceedings of the Linnean Society of London. Zoology) (paper · 1858 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 18.1 Understanding Evolution (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Natural selection (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Modern synthesis

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · Darwin + genetics

In the 1930s–40s, natural selection and Mendelian genetics were joined into one theory of evolution.

Evolution became a change in how common gene variants are in a population.

Forces: Mutation, natural selection, gene flow and genetic drift.

Since: Molecular biology and genomics have extended it.

Builds on: natural-selection, mendel

  • Maths: Statistics & inference · population genetics uses statistics

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 19.1 Population Evolution (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Modern synthesis (20th century) (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Speciation

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · one species splits into two

New species form when populations stop interbreeding and evolve apart.

Often a barrier, such as a mountain range or a sea, separates them first.

Kinds: Allopatric (split by geography) and sympatric (in the same place).

Result: Over billions of years, speciation built the tree of life.

Builds on: modern-synthesis

  • From Nothing to Us: Plants · plants on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: OpenStax Biology 2e, 18.2 Formation of New Species (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Speciation (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Cambrian explosion

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · animal body plans appear

From about 539 million years ago, most major animal groups appear in the fossil record within a few tens of millions of years.

Eyes, shells and legs show up in early fossils such as those of the Burgess Shale.

Roots: Molecular clocks suggest animal groups began to split earlier, in the Ediacaran.

Why: The causes are still debated: oxygen, ecology and genetic toolkits.

Builds on: eukaryote-origin, speciation

  • Big Bang to Today: Cambrian explosion · on the Big Bang to Today timeline
  • From Nothing to Us: Animals · animals on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: Erwin et al. 2011: The Cambrian Conundrum: Early Divergence and Later Ecological Success in the Early History of Animals (Science) (paper · 2011 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 27.4 The Evolutionary History of the Animal Kingdom (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Cambrian explosion (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Mass extinctions

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · the big five die-offs

Five times in the last 540 million years, a large share of species died out quickly.

The latest, 66 million years ago, ended the non-bird dinosaurs.

Found: Raup and Sepkoski picked out the big mass extinctions in the marine fossil record in 1982.

Now: Many scientists warn that today's loss of species could become a sixth.

Builds on: speciation

  • From Nothing to Us: Mammals · mammals spread after the dinosaurs

Sources: Raup & Sepkoski 1982: Mass Extinctions in the Marine Fossil Record (Science) (paper · 1982 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 47.1 The Biodiversity Crisis (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Extinction event (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Human evolution

known EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · Homo sapiens, about 300,000 years

Humans are primates; our closest living relatives are chimpanzees and bonobos.

The oldest known Homo sapiens fossils, from Jebel Irhoud in Morocco, are about 300,000 years old.

Africa: Our species arose in Africa and later spread around the world.

Cousins: Neanderthals and Denisovans were close relatives; many people carry a little of their DNA.

Builds on: cambrian, modern-synthesis

  • From Nothing to Us: Homo sapiens · Homo sapiens on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Big Bang to Today: Homo sapiens · on the Big Bang to Today timeline
  • Human Body: Human body · the Human Body explorer

Sources: Hublin et al. 2017: New fossils from Jebel Irhoud, Morocco and the pan-African origin of Homo sapiens (Nature) (paper · 2017 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 29.7 The Evolution of Primates (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Human evolution (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Life beyond Earth

unknown EVOLUTION & ORGANISMS · are we alone?

No life has yet been found anywhere except Earth.

Astrobiologists search Mars, icy moons such as Europa and Enceladus, and the air of planets around other stars.

Why look: Liquid water, energy sources and the elements of life exist in many places.

Open: We know of only one example of life, so we cannot yet tell how common it is.

Builds on: origin-of-life

  • From Nothing to Us: Other worlds · other worlds on the From Nothing to Us map

Sources: NASA Astrobiology (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Des Marais et al. 2008: The NASA Astrobiology Roadmap (Astrobiology) (paper · 2008 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Wikipedia: Astrobiology (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Photosynthesis

known LIFE SYSTEMS · sunlight → sugar and oxygen

Plants, algae and cyanobacteria use light energy to make sugar from carbon dioxide and water.

Oxygen is given off as a by-product.

Overall: 6 CO₂ + 6 H₂O + light → C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6 O₂.

Old: Oxygen-producing photosynthesis evolved in cyanobacteria more than 2.4 billion years ago.

Builds on: atp, endosymbiosis

  • From Nothing to Us: Photosynthesis · photosynthesis on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Physics: Photon · light comes in photons
  • Physics: Electromagnetic radiation · sunlight is electromagnetic radiation
  • Virtual Cell: Glucose · glucose

Sources: Blankenship 2010: Early Evolution of Photosynthesis (Plant Physiology) (paper · 2010 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.1 Overview of Photosynthesis (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Photosynthesis (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Cellular respiration

known LIFE SYSTEMS · sugar + oxygen → ATP

Cells break down sugar with oxygen to make ATP, giving off carbon dioxide and water.

In eukaryotes most of this ATP is made in the mitochondria.

How: A flow of protons across the inner membrane drives ATP synthase (Mitchell's chemiosmotic theory, 1961).

Without oxygen: Fermentation makes far less ATP from each sugar.

Builds on: atp, endosymbiosis

  • Virtual Cell: Mitochondrion · a mitochondrion
  • Virtual Cell: ATP synthase · ATP synthase
  • Human Body: Lungs · your lungs bring in the oxygen

Sources: Mitchell 1961: Coupling of Phosphorylation to Electron and Hydrogen Transfer by a Chemi-Osmotic type of Mechanism (Nature) (paper · 1961 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 7.1 Energy in Living Systems (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Cellular respiration (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Great Oxidation Event

known LIFE SYSTEMS · life fills the air with oxygen

About 2.4 billion years ago, oxygen made by cyanobacteria began to build up in the air.

It changed Earth's chemistry and paved the way for oxygen-breathing life.

Evidence: Changes in sulfur isotopes and in the minerals found in old rocks.

Slow rise: Oxygen stayed far below today's level for more than a billion years.

Builds on: photosynthesis

  • From Nothing to Us: Oxygen rises · oxygen rises on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Universe: Earth · Earth in the Universe tour
  • All the Elements: Oxygen · oxygen

Sources: Lyons et al. 2014: The rise of oxygen in Earth’s early ocean and atmosphere (Nature) (paper · 2014 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Great Oxidation Event (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Ecosystems and food webs

known LIFE SYSTEMS · energy flows, matter cycles

An ecosystem is all the living things in a place together with their surroundings.

Energy flows from producers to consumers; often only about 10% passes on to each next level.

Classic paper: Raymond Lindeman described energy flow between feeding levels in 1942.

Decomposers: Fungi and bacteria break down dead matter and return its nutrients.

Builds on: photosynthesis

  • From Nothing to Us: Plants · plants
  • From Nothing to Us: Animals · animals
  • Universe: Earth · Earth in the Universe tour

Sources: Lindeman 1942: The Trophic‐Dynamic Aspect of Ecology (Ecology) (paper · 1942 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 46.1 Ecology of Ecosystems (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 46.2 Energy Flow through Ecosystems (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Ecosystem (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Carbon cycle

known LIFE SYSTEMS · carbon moves through life, air, sea and rock

Photosynthesis pulls carbon dioxide out of the air; respiration and decay put it back.

Oceans, rocks and fossil fuels store huge amounts of carbon for long times.

Today: Burning fossil fuels adds carbon to the air faster than natural processes remove it.

Builds on: photosynthesis, cellular-respiration

  • All the Elements: Carbon · carbon
  • Universe: Earth · Earth in the Universe tour

Sources: NASA Earth Observatory: The Carbon Cycle (official · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 46.3 Biogeochemical Cycles (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Carbon cycle (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Homeostasis

known LIFE SYSTEMS · keeping the inside steady

Living things keep their inside conditions, such as temperature and blood sugar, within narrow limits.

Mostly by negative feedback: a change triggers a response that reverses it.

Named: Walter Cannon coined the word in the 1920s (reviewed in 1929).

Example: Human body temperature is held near 37 °C.

Builds on: cell-membrane

  • Human Body: Human body · the Human Body explorer
  • Human Body: Kidneys · kidneys balance water and salts
  • From Nothing to Us: Organ systems · organ systems

Sources: Cannon 1929: Organization for physiological homeostasis (Physiological Reviews) (review · 1929 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 33.3 Homeostasis (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Homeostasis (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Nervous system

known LIFE SYSTEMS · neurons send electrical signals

Neurons carry fast electrical signals, called action potentials, along their axons.

At synapses, chemical messengers pass the signal on to the next cell.

Explained: Hodgkin and Huxley described the action potential with equations in 1952.

Physics: The signal is charged ions (sodium and potassium) flowing through channels in the membrane.

Builds on: homeostasis, cell-membrane

  • Human Body: Brain · the brain
  • Human Body: Nerves · nerves
  • From Nothing to Us: Brain · the brain on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Physics: Coulomb's law · ions push and pull by electric force
  • Maths: Differential equations · the Hodgkin–Huxley model is differential equations

Sources: Hodgkin & Huxley 1952: A quantitative description of membrane current and its application to conduction and excitation in nerve (The Journal of Physiology) (paper · 1952 · checked 2026-11-12: ok (DOI registered in Crossref; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · OpenStax Biology 2e, 35.2 How Neurons Communicate (textbook · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Action potential (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Consciousness

unknown LIFE SYSTEMS · how brains give rise to experience

How brain activity gives rise to conscious experience is not known.

Several scientific theories compete, and experiments to test them are under way.

Theories: Global workspace, higher-order, integrated information and others (reviewed in 2022).

Builds on: nervous-system

  • From Nothing to Us: Consciousness · consciousness on the From Nothing to Us map
  • Human Body: Brain · the brain

Sources: Seth & Bayne 2022: Theories of consciousness (Nat Rev Neurosci) (paper · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Wikipedia: Consciousness (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Universe: real images, one stop per scale

A zoomable 3D tour through real NASA / ESA images, one stop per scale. Pictures are only resized, cropped or wrapped onto a ball (all-sky maps); nothing is painted in. Sizes are on each stop, not drawn to scale with each other.

1. Earth

known our planet, about 12,742 km across · shown as photo map wrapped on a ball

Earth is a rocky planet with a mean radius of 6,371 km. The globe is wrapped with NASA's Blue Marble map.

What the picture is: A cloud-free mosaic of many satellite photos (NASA Blue Marble), not one snapshot. Real colours, real land and sea.

Size: Mean radius 6,371 km, so about 12,742 km across (NASA fact sheet).

Image: universe/earth.webp · credit: NASA Earth Observatory / Blue Marble · public domain

  • From Nothing to Us: Earth
  • Big Bang to Today: Earth and Moon form

Sources: NASA NSSDCA: Earth fact sheet (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · NASA Visible Earth: Blue Marble collection (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Image: Whole world, land and oceans (NASA Earth Observatory / Blue Marble, public domain) (image archive · year n/a · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

2. The Sun

known our star, 109 Earths across · shown as flat photo you can turn

The Sun has a mean radius of 695,700 km, about 109 times Earth's. Earth orbits it at a mean distance of 149.6 million km.

What the picture is: A real photo by NASA's Solar Dynamics Observatory taken in extreme ultraviolet light, which our eyes cannot see, so the colours are false colours.

Size: Mean radius 695,700 km; 109.2 × Earth (NASA fact sheet). Light from the Sun takes about 8 minutes to reach us.

Image: universe/sun.webp · credit: NASA/SDO (AIA) · public domain

  • From Nothing to Us: Solar system
  • Big Bang to Today: Sun and Solar System form
  • Physics: Electromagnetic radiation

Sources: NASA NSSDCA: Sun fact sheet (radius, Sun/Earth ratio, mean distance) (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · NASA Solar Dynamics Observatory (SDO) (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Image: the Sun by SDO's Atmospheric Imaging Assembly, 19 Aug 2010 (NASA/SDO, public domain) (image archive · year n/a · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

3. Our sky: the Milky Way from inside

known Gaia's map of 1.7 billion stars · shown as all-sky map on a ball around you

We live inside the Milky Way, about 27,000 light-years from its centre, so we see it as a bright band across the sky. ESA's Gaia mapped about 1.7 billion of its stars.

What the picture is: Gaia's all-sky map (Data Release 2), built from star measurements, wrapped on a ball around you. Look around: the bright band is the disc of our galaxy, and the two smudges lower right are the Large and Small Magellanic Clouds.

Be careful: A sky map shows directions, not distances: stars on it are at very different distances.

Where we are: The Galactic Centre is 8.178 kiloparsecs away (about 26,700 light-years), measured by the GRAVITY Collaboration in 2019.

Image: universe/gaia-sky.webp · credit: ESA/Gaia/DPAC · CC BY 4.0

  • From Nothing to Us: Galaxies
  • Physics: Dark matter

Sources: ESA: Gaia's sky in colour (Gaia DR2 all-sky map) (agency / lab · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Gaia Collaboration 2018: Gaia Data Release 2, summary of the contents (A&A 616, A1) (paper · 2018 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · GRAVITY Collaboration 2019: distance to the Galactic Centre, 8.178 kpc (A&A 625, L10) (paper · 2019 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Image: Gaia's all-sky view, ESA/Gaia/DPAC (CC BY 4.0) (image archive · year n/a · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

4. A spiral galaxy like ours (M101)

known the Pinwheel Galaxy, 25 million light-years away · shown as flat photo you can turn

No camera has ever left the Milky Way, so nobody has a photo of our galaxy from outside. M101, the Pinwheel Galaxy, is a real spiral galaxy seen face-on by Hubble.

What the picture is: Hubble's largest galaxy portrait: 51 exposures plus ground-based images (ESA/Hubble, 2006). The photo is flat; real galaxy discs are thin but not flat paper.

Size and distance: About 170,000 light-years across, nearly twice the Milky Way, and about 25 million light-years away (ESA/Hubble).

Honest note: Our galaxy is a barred spiral; M101 is used here only as a real example of what a spiral galaxy looks like.

Image: universe/m101.webp · credit: European Space Agency & NASA · CC BY 4.0

  • From Nothing to Us: Galaxies
  • Big Bang to Today: Earliest galaxies seen

Sources: ESA/Hubble heic0602: largest ever Hubble galaxy portrait, the Pinwheel Galaxy (M101) (agency / lab · 2006 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Image: M101, European Space Agency & NASA (CC BY 4.0) (image archive · year n/a · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

5. Galaxies everywhere: Webb's First Deep Field

known thousands of galaxies in a grain-of-sand patch of sky · shown as flat photo of a patch of sky

Point a big telescope at a tiny dark patch of sky and it fills with galaxies. This patch is about the size of a grain of sand held at arm's length.

What the picture is: NASA's Webb telescope, infrared camera NIRCam, 12.5 hours. Infrared light is shown as visible colours.

What you see: The galaxy cluster SMACS 0723 as it was 4.6 billion years ago. Its gravity bends light from galaxies far behind it into arcs (gravitational lensing).

Honest note: This is a flat photo. The galaxies in it are at many different distances; we do not show them in 3D.

Image: universe/webb-deep-field.webp · credit: NASA, ESA, CSA, STScI · public domain

  • From Nothing to Us: Galaxies
  • Physics: General relativity

Sources: NASA / STScI: Webb's First Deep Field (NIRCam image of SMACS 0723) (agency / lab · 2022 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Image: Webb's First Deep Field, NASA, ESA, CSA, STScI (public domain) (image archive · year n/a · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

6. The oldest light: the cosmic microwave background

known ESA Planck's map of the whole sky · shown as all-sky map on a ball around us

The farthest thing we can see is light released about 380,000 years after the Big Bang. ESA's Planck mapped it across the whole sky.

What the picture is: Planck's all-sky map of tiny temperature differences in the cosmic microwave background, wrapped on a ball with us at the centre. Microwaves are invisible, so the colours are false colours.

How far: Because space has expanded, the matter that sent this light is now about 46 billion light-years away: roughly the radius of the observable universe.

Honest note: It is a sphere around us because we see out in every direction; it is not a wall at the edge of the universe.

Image: universe/planck-cmb.webp · credit: ESA and the Planck Collaboration · CC BY 4.0

  • Big Bang to Today: Recombination
  • From Nothing to Us: Afterglow (CMB)
  • Physics: Cosmic microwave background

Sources: ESA: Planck CMB (the cosmic microwave background seen by Planck) (agency / lab · 2013 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · ESA: Planck and the cosmic microwave background (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Planck 2018 results VI: cosmological parameters (preprint · 2018 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Wikipedia: Observable universe (encyclopedia · 2026 · checked 2026-11-12: ok) · Image: Cosmic Microwave Background, ESA and the Planck Collaboration (CC BY 4.0) (image archive · year n/a · checked 2026-11-12: ok)

Origin key

  • Big Bang: Made in the first 20 minutes.
  • Cosmic-ray splitting: Fast particles break bigger nuclei.
  • Dying low-mass stars: Giant stars (1–8 Suns) catch neutrons slowly (s-process), then puff off their outer layers. (Most of our lithium: such stars and novae.)
  • Massive stars & their supernovae: Made by fusion, neutron capture and explosive burning in stars >8 Suns; spread by core-collapse supernovae. Includes most Cu and the simpler main-source choice for Zn, Ti and V; shares vary by isotope and remain debated.
  • Exploding white dwarfs: Type Ia supernovae: lots of iron-group metals.
  • Merging neutron stars: Rapid neutron catching (r-process). Maybe also rare supernovae (debated).
  • Radioactive decay only: No long-lived form; made as other elements decay.
  • Human-made: Made in reactors or accelerators (some also in traces or in stars, briefly).

Not made yet

redUnunennium (temporary name) (Z 119): Not made yet. Hunts running: Dubna (JINR) began Bk-249 + Ti-50 on 1 June 2026, for several months. RIKEN (V-51 + Cm-248) paused after reaching its planned sensitivity, with no atom reported, and is planning its next step.

Sources: JINR 2026: Dubna starts its element-119 experiment (Bk-249 + Ti-50) (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · RIKEN (TASCA26 abstract): the V-51 + Cm-248 search for element 119 (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

redUnbinilium (temporary name) (Z 120): Not made yet. Berkeley showed a Ti-50 beam can make superheavies (2 livermorium atoms in 22 days, 2024); 120 is expected to be 10–20× harder and could take years. ORNL is making a Cf-249 target (2026). An older GSI search (2011) saw one possible chain, never confirmed.

Sources: US DOE 2025: new progress toward discovering new elements (Ti-50 beam) (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Berkeley Lab 2023: testing a new approach to superheavy elements (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · ORNL talk 2026: Cf-249 target for an element-120 attempt (agency / lab · updated page · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Hofmann et al. 2016 (EPJ A): GSI search for element 120 (Cr-54 + Cm-248), one unconfirmed chain (paper · 2016 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

orangeIsland of stability (Z Island): Predicted: nuclei near Z ≈ 114–126 protons and N ≈ 184 neutrons could live much longer than their neighbours. Today's superheavies have too few neutrons to reach it; nobody has made N = 184.

Sources: Nature Reviews Physics 2023: superheavy elements and the limit of the periodic table (review · 2023 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · JINR 2026: Ti-50 vs Cr-54 beams for elements 119/120 (paper · 2026 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)

redWhere the table may end (Z ~172): Around Z ≈ 170 the innermost electron level would sink into "negative energy" (estimates 164–174); extended tables stop near 172–173. Nuclei may stop holding together before that.

Sources: Schwerdtfeger et al. 2023: relativity and the end of the periodic table (preprint · 2023 · checked 2026-11-11: ok) · Pyykkö 2011: a suggested periodic table up to Z ≈ 172 (PCCP) (paper · 2011 · checked 2026-11-11: ok (DOI registered; publisher blocks automatic checks)) · Nature Reviews Physics 2023: superheavy elements and the limit of the periodic table (review · 2023 · checked 2026-11-11: ok)
